# Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The project "Evolving Ecosystem-Based Resilience of Vulnerable Rural Communities in the Beni River Basin" aims to enhance the resilience of Bolivia's rural communities against climate change by increasing the adaptive capacity and livelihoods of vulnerable populations living in the Beni River Basin.

## Project Description

1. The project is designed to address risks linked to climate change, such as increased vulnerability of water resources, land degradation, and loss of biodiversity.
2. It aims to enhance the resilience of rural communities through ecosystem-based approaches that focus on improving livelihoods, ecosystems, irrigation infrastructure, and food security in the Beni River Basin.
3. The project will increase the adaptive capacity of vulnerable populations by addressing climate change impacts and promoting sustainable land use practices.

## Project Implementation

1. The project has been developed with the aim of changing the current paradigm of climate change vulnerability and community resilience.
2. It is well-suited to climate change and enables restoration of ecosystems, thereby enhancing social-ecological resilience in the Beni River Basin.
3. The project will be implemented through a phased approach, focusing on increasing adaptive capacity and livelihoods.

## Project Outputs

1. Improved ecosystem functions (primarily hydrological regulation) on one hand, and climate and social-ecological resilience on the other.
2. Increased adaptive capacity of vulnerable populations in the Beni River Basin.
3. Enhanced food security for rural communities through improved irrigation infrastructure and sustainable land use practices.

## Project Implementation

1. The project will be implemented by the Government of Bolivia, acting through the Ministry of Environment and Water.
2. It is well-suited to climate change and enables restoration of ecosystems, thereby enhancing social-ecological resilience in the Beni River Basin.
3. The project will be implemented through a phased approach, focusing on increasing adaptive capacity and livelihoods.

## Project Outputs

1. Improved ecosystem functions (primarily hydrological regulation) on one hand, and climate and social-ecological resilience on the other.
2. Increased adaptive capacity of vulnerable populations in the Beni River Basin.
3. Enhanced food security for rural communities through improved irrigation infrastructure and sustainable land use practices.

## Project Implementation

1. The project will be implemented by the Government of Bolivia, acting through the Ministry of Environment and Water.

A

resilient landscapes and reconfiguring food systems. The paradigm shift comes in part through the permanent transition from traditional agricultural practices characterized by high climate vulnerability and increasing loss and damage, to a system in which diverse agroecosystems are managed for resilience and long-term productivity. By combining innovative insurance products that make term investments financially viable, and are able to diversify their incomes and access markets, with an integrated approach to land management and water resources, the project will create a virtuous circle of climate resilient production for vulnerable farmers.

a) Project components: The project consists of five interdependent components that build on each other in a coherent approach that includes:

(i) Improving the capacity of small-scale farmers to manage their agroecosystems sustainably (ii) On-farm diversification and income security through climate-resilient agricultural systems, including improved water management, regular water regulation and supply, reduction of erosion and disaster risk and (iv) Strengthening the corresponding governance structures at local level. The project will be implemented in 12 municipalities in the province of La Paz, Bolivia.

(v) An integral and transformative aspect of the proposal is catalyzing finance for long-term resilience building through insurance products that make term investments financially viable, and are able to diversify their incomes and access markets. It is estimated that the project will directly benefit at least 51,561 heads of households (70% of the total population of the county) with an average income increase of USD $24 per month. The indirect beneficiaries include 389,000 people (beneficiaries are 1,251,798 (19.1% of total national population and 53.7% of the Main Region municipality project). The project places strong emphasis on engagement of women and youth participation.

The gender diagnosis has identified inequality gaps related to access to land, credit, extension services, and decision-making power. These inequalities are also reflected in relation to expected outcomes of the Project. Improvements in food security due to training and inputs received may not be equally distributed among men and women farmers.

5. The project consists of five interdependent components that seek to build climate resilience in the agroecological systems via integrated ecosystem management and enhanced governance:

Component 1: Strengthened food and income security in changing climate through climate-resilient agricultural systems Component 2: Improved water resources management for long-term resilience building

Component 3: Restored and conserved micro-watersheds and ecosystem functions and services

Component 4: Enhanced governance structures at local level to support integrated ecosystem management, climate-informed integral micro-watershed management, and access to financial mechanisms.

6. Bolivia is already experiencing a range of climate-related impacts including an increase in the national average temperature by 0.5°C over the last two decades (from 13.2°C to 13.7°C) since the late 1970s. The country is also experiencing rainfall variability and has observed a decreasing trend in average annual rainfall of approximately -4% per decade.

7. In addition, Bolivia is one of the most vulnerable countries in Latin America with respect to climate change impacts on the economy, particularly agriculture: The Vallejo Macro Region – consisting of 65 municipalities covering 338,000 ha – is one of the most important agricultural regions in Bolivia and has been identified as a priority area for adaptation. It is characterized by low adaptive capacity translates into very high vulnerability.

8. These impacts are expected to increase due to climate change and will be exacerbated by the projected decrease in soil fertility and climate and limited availability of water resources during the dry season. This represents a deep socio-ecological risk considering that in 2016, agriculture consumed approximately 75% of total national freshwater use, representing an important share of the country’s water footprint (the National Gross Domestic Product), and producing more than 80 percent of the country's food supply.

9. Climate change is expected to increase the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as an increase in temperature from +2°C to +4°C. This change will directly affect the availability of water in quantity and quality, due to changes in precipitation patterns and increased evaporation rates. The climate model projections for the Vallejo Macro Region (VMMR) show a decrease of approximately 20% in the northeast and eastern parts of the Vallejo macro-region, while in the southern part there is an increase of up to +15%. This will have significant impacts on water availability and the formation of landslides.

10. Women are particularly vulnerable due to the effects of climate change: Women make up at least 48% of agricultural production systems in Bolivia. Their main activities are crop production, bee keeping, forestry, animal husbandry, and selling agricultural

Measures should be taken at the national level to promote the implementation of Integrated Sustainable Production Systems within Territorial Management should increase the resilience in the face of climate change, while contributing to the conservation of biodiversity and natural resources. The promotion of integrated and sustainable forest management and the management of other components of Mother Earth, to contribute to the achievement of the objectives set out by the National Forest Policy (NFP) and the National Biodiversity Strategy approach (at the national level) for implementing non-market schemes for reducing deforestation and forest degradation.

Taking into account the importance of indigenous peoples' knowledge in the face of climate change, it is necessary to take indigenous peoples' knowledge into account, in order to contribute to climate change mitigation and adaptation of ecosystems and agricultural systems.

13. The total project cost is USD 63 million, which is comprised of USD 33.3 million of GCF funding, in the form of grants, and USD 30 million of co-financing from the Government of Bolivia, acting through the Ministry of Environment and Water (MINAM). The implementation of the project will be carried out by the FAM, with technical assistance provided by FAO. The overall quality assurance and oversight of the project will be administered by FAO as Accredited Entity responsible for the overall quality assurance and oversight of the project.

FAO is also responsible for the preparation of the Project Document (PD) and the Joint Mitigation and Adaptation Mechanism (JAM). The PD will be prepared in accordance with the GCF guidelines, and the JAM will be developed by FAO. The FAM will be Executive Entity, exclusively responsible for the implementation of activities funded from co-financing resources.

## B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

### 4. Bolivia: Exposure to climate change and its effects on agriculture

#### a. Socio-economic context

**4.1 Background**

Bolivia is highly vulnerable to the effects of climate change, such as increased extreme events which are affecting waterways (droughts, floods), hills, and the negative ecological, economic, and social impacts expected to be exacerbated by climate change.

**4.2 The agricultural sector in Bolivia**

The agricultural sector in Bolivia is characterized by a complex set of interactions involving the timing and volume of water, soil, plants and location of irrigation, and agricultural seasonality. There are currently 151,082 farms in Bolivia (FAO, 2016). These farms are classified as follows:

- **Smallholder family farming**: This is one of the most vulnerable areas in the country due to its semi-arid climate and limited availability of water resources during the rainy season. The majority of these farms have less than two hectares of land (FAO, 2016). These farms are characterized by a complex set of interactions involving the timing and volume of water, soil, plants and location of irrigation, and agricultural seasonality.

- **Large-scale commercial farming**: This type of farming is characterized by a complex set of interactions involving the timing and volume of water, soil, plants and location of irrigation, and agricultural seasonality. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE B

## Forests and ecosystem services

### Agricultural systems rely mostly on ecosystem functions and services, particularly hydrological functions for the irrigation of crops in the Valley Macrón region.

- **Water availability:** The Valley Macrón region is water poor not only to local communities but also to its dry rivers. Forest natural ecosystems also play an important role in maintaining the quality of surface water.
- **Forest cover changes during the last few decades have deeply modified the provision of hydrological services:**
  - In a biodiversity hotspot, the Valley Macrón region is one of the most biodiverse areas and are critical for hydrological regulation and provisioning, water infiltration, climate regulation, and prevention of soil erosion. The forested area has been reduced by about 50% in the last few decades.
- **The largest remaining forested area is the valley of the river Bóvina, which is a priority area for conservation to ensure the continued provision of ecosystem services:**
  - About half of the remaining forests are primary forests. There are significant pressures on these forests, which are managed by local communities and private owners.
- **The main drivers of forest cover change in the Valley Macrón region include factors such as population growth, poverty and weak export market of the country. However, during the 1950s, Bolivia's deforestation rate increased due to a combination of these factors:**
  - The area of ended soil erosion has increased by 86% (from about 42 to 78 million hectares). While the rate of net erosion has slowed down since then.
- **The forested area in the Valley Macrón region is estimated at 1,300 km², or 27.5%. This endangered ecosystem affects 45% of the country and up to 75.96% of the land in the valleys of the rivers:**
  - **Bóvina:** 18%
  - **Macrón:** 30%
  - **Pacora:** 20%

### Biodiversity

- **The Valley Macrón region is one of the most biodiverse areas and are critical for hydrological regulation and provisioning, water infiltration, climate regulation, and prevention of soil erosion.**
- **The largest remaining forested area is the valley of the river Bóvina, which is a priority area for conservation to ensure the continued provision of ecosystem services:**

### Challenges and Opportunities for Development

- **Challenges:** The main challenges faced by communities in the proposed intervention area include water scarcity and management, desertification, and land use.

## c. Climate context (historical and projected climate change)

The IPCC report (2021) highlights the increased likelihood of more frequent extreme weather events and droughts on the global scale, with a warming land surface (likely 1 to 4 times faster than the ocean surface). It is virtually certain that the land surface will continue to warm more than the ocean surface (likely 1.4 to 7.1 times more). The South American Monsoon region in particular has experienced an increase of extreme precipitation events and a decrease in the number of dry days, with increasing drought (high confidence). Along the same line, there are projections of increased agricultural and ecological drought for the mid-21st century. For Bolivia specifically, the IPCC report projects that the country will experience more frequent and intense heat waves and fire weather (high confidence) which will affect a wide range of sectors, including agriculture, forestry, health, and ecosystems. At some time in this century, it is virtually certain that the average annual temperature over Bolivia will exceed 20°C for a 2°C global warming level and above. The project acknowledges the recent IPCC AR6 report and the fact that a set of new greenhouse gas emission scenarios have been developed to reflect the latest science.

The historical climate context (1975-2014) is described in Section A.3, while projections for future climate change impacts are based on RCP 8.5, since hydrological cycles are highly impacted by radiative forcing temperature variability. Studies predict that the dry season will become longer and drier, with a decrease of precipitation during the wet season (high confidence). This is primarily due to the predicted decrease in productivity, which for a number of key crops might reach 30% or more. Smallerholder farmers are particularly vulnerable because they have limited access to irrigation systems and water resources. In the Andean region, and the average maize yield is only 2.23 t/ha. Unsustainable land use change (particularly deforestation) and changing precipitation patterns will further exacerbate this situation.

Climate change impacts on agriculture in Bolivia are described in Section A.4.1. The projected decrease of agricultural production is primarily due to decreasing climate resilience, particularly concerning water and nutrient cycling exacerbate these situations. Hydrological changes will also have an impact on the livestock sector (Section A.4.2). This section provides a more detailed description of the results from the hydrological modeling for water balance, see Section 7 Annex A: Feasibility.

Climate change impacts are expected to be felt across all sectors in Bolivia and will have significant implications for the agricultural sector, given its effects on temperature, precipitation, intensity and frequency of extreme events, and glacial runoff (Section A.4). The projected decrease in maize yield is only 2.23 t/ha (high confidence) by 2050 (and year) and the intermittent sources of water define both the Vaupés Macro region's exposure and sensitivity to climate change.

## Past and current climate trends

The country has a total of 14 meteorological stations, of which only 39 stations have complete data for the period of time. Of 2015 years and 241 days have data from 20 years. Figure 1 shows a map of the meteorological stations in Bolivia and neighboring countries. Table 1 provides an overview of the historical climate context (1975-2014) for each station, including the average annual temperature, precipitation, and glacial runoff. The table also includes information on the agricultural sector, given its effects on temperature, precipitation, intensity and frequency of extreme events, and glacial runoff.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 13

## Climate Scenarios for Mexico

### Introduction to Climate Projections

#### 2015-2099: Feasibility Study and Climate Projections

**33.** For the purposes of this Feasibility Study, Fundación Amigos de la Naturaleza (FAN) prepared a climate analysis in 2017 and updated it in 2021 for the Valles Macro region. For the climate change scenarios modeling, a spatial analysis of climate patterns was performed using the data from the Mexican National Meteorological Institute (INM). The precipitation, defining the degree of correlation with current climate trends using the SEMARNAT weather station database (1973-2014), was adjusted to the 1981-2010 period. To better characterize and represent the spatial distribution of precipitation, the climate data measured by stations were adjusted via the NCEP/NCAR reanalysis dataset (Kalnay et al., 1996) and the ERA5 dataset (Copernicus Climate Change Service). This adjustment was made on a regional scale to calibrate the data with stations close to the Valles Macro region.

**34.** The methodology used for the climate projections is based on five models, selected due to their high correspondence with the observed climate in Mexico and the ability of the models to represent the spatial distribution of precipitation (Dufresne et al., 2013). These models are: CMIP5-CanESM2 (Boer et al. 2011), CMIP5-MIROC5 (Watanabe M et al. 2010), MRI-CGCM3 (Yukimoto S. 2012), and the coupled model ECHAM6-HAMOCC (Roeckner et al., 2017). The information base was integrated with the climate data from the CMIP5 ensemble (Coupled Model Intercomparison Project) determining the monthly temperature (maximum, minimum, and mean) and precipitation for each of these models. A statistical analysis was performed to determine the most representative model. Then, a combination of all five models was taken to moderate extreme trends. This enables the better assessment of possible future impacts while maintaining the consistency with the observed climate in Mexico.

**35.** The baseline year for the climate change projections covers 1950-2000. The projections are made for the 2050 and 2080 scenarios, which represent a period of high uncertainty due to the lack of knowledge about future greenhouse gas emissions as well as the results for RCP6.3, Section A in Annex B - Feasibility study.

**36.** Climate change projections show an increase in average temperature (Figure 4.1), mainly in the valleys, where temperatures will reach annual averages of 25°C (RCP4.5) and 28°C (RCP8.5). The projected increase is more significant for RCP8.5 than for RCP4.5. In the highlands, there is a greater increase at the end of the dry season. These trends will undoubtedly impact the biodiversity, in particular resulting in changes in the distribution and abundance of species.

**37.** Climate change scenarios (RCP 4.5) for 2050 show that precipitation follows a pattern of higher rainfall in the northern part of the Valles Macro region and lower rainfall in the southern part according to the RCP8.5 scenario and 20% according to the RCP6.3 scenario. This trend will make water supply critical (Figure 4.1).

### References

**11** For reference: The areas with the highest rainfall are concentrated in the Carrasco protected area and in the southern region of the Valles Macro region, where precipitation is between 2000 mm per year on average. In the central zone, it ranges from 600 to 800 mm per year, and in the lower part, in the valleys, varies between 900 and 1300 mm per year.

**12** Consultation of the National Commission for Climate Change at UNCCD. Available online: https://cuncd.org/es/actualidad/consultas/nccn/NCCN2018info.pdf

## Water Balance Scenarios for the Valles Meco Region

### Background and Methodology

The water balance model is based on the Green Climate Fund (GCF) project "Valles Meco: Building Resilience to Climate Change in the Amazon Basin." Sustained supply of water will be restricted to the areas with the highest vegetation cover in the Northwest and Southern regions of the Valles Meco basin. The results from the water balance modeling will inform the choice of climate-proofing irrigation technology and methods aligned with the project's goals.

### Water Balance Scenarios

The water balance scenarios are based on the following assumptions:
- **Scenario 1 (S1):** No change in precipitation.
- **Scenario 2 (S2):** A 5% increase in precipitation.
- **Scenario 3 (S3):** A 10% increase in precipitation.

### Results

The results from the water balance modeling will inform the choice of climate-proofing irrigation technology and methods aligned with the project's goals. The scenarios are based on the following assumptions:
- **Scenario 1 (S1):** No change in precipitation.
- **Scenario 2 (S2):** A 5% increase in precipitation.
- **Scenario 3 (S3):** A 10% increase in precipitation.

### Conclusion
It is observed that the deficit increases under future conditions. In the Amazon region, a current deficit of 12.9% (RCP 6.0) and 37.1% (RCP 4.5) is expected to increase by 2036–2085 to 23.8% for RCP 6.0 and 39.5% for RCP 4.5, assuming a scenario of 2018 as the base year. The deficit in the Pota River region is currently at 37.1%, which will increase by 2036–2085 to 46.4% (RCP 6.0) and 59.5% (RCP 4.5), assuming a scenario of 2018 as the base year.

### Project target area: Valle de Macorón

The project target area consists of 68 municipalities in the Valle de Macorón region, which is one of the most climate vulnerable areas in Bolivia. The population here is 3,590,400 and it has a population of approximately 2,320,741 people, mostly (96%) dedicated to small-scale (partially subsistence) farming. Approximately 63% of the total production comes from agriculture, which represents an important source of income for the majority of the population, especially in the extreme poverty group. The Valle de Macorón region is one of the most vulnerable areas of the country due to semi-arid climate and limited availability of water resources during the dry season. It is estimated that, in 2015, agriculture at the

nature has employed more than 29% of the labour force and contributed 15.3% to the national GDP [4]. The Valles is characterized by subsistence family farming with cultivation plots of less than 2 ha each with low productivity. Irrigated plots are even rarer, as they are used for cash crops such as tomatoes, onions, garlic, and other vegetables that provide food for the family food basket, so the effects of climate change on food producers result in shortages of products in the basic food basket.

48. The most important crops in this area are beans, corns, potatoes, broad beans, peas, tomato, cucumber, paprika, chard, celery, cabbage, lettuce, onions, garlic, carrots, radishes, and citrus fruits. 49. Over 90% of Bolivia's water use is for the agricultural sector; however, previous evaluation studies of the irrigation systems conducted in the Valles Macizo-region show that there are problems with the new and old irrigation systems (above 30%), infrastructure maintenance is problematic in some cases, and the transition to new irrigated cropping practices and innovation is slow. There are several reasons for this: first, the lack of access to water resources; second, the low level of technical skills; third, the fact that existing systems reported at national level are unable to access water consistently and efficiently; exceeding smallholder vulnerability thresholds; fourth, the lack of institutional capacity in the sector; fifth, the lack of financial resources. The projects are those with support managed by NAMO and/or foundations, which monitor the operation and maintenance in the region. In this context, the most vulnerable people in the Valles Macizo-region are the subsistence farmers who have a long experience of traditional systems, the quality and safety of the water source, the functionality of the irrigation infrastructure, and the financial resources to invest in new technologies and practices.

Self-management of irrigation showed that, after three years, only 20% of the irrigation systems presented problems for continuity. The main reasons were: lack of maintenance; low level of technical skills; and insufficient access to water availability due to changes in precipitation patterns, increased evapotranspiration losses due to higher temperature, and greater frequency of droughts. ## Climate vulnerability and impacts in the Valle Macro region

A vulnerability assessment was conducted for the Valle Macro region in order to identify the level of vulnerability of the municipalities within this region, taking into account their exposure (sensitivity to climate change), and (2) relative capacity to respond to these impacts, described by 13 indicators. The baseline used is the year 2000 with climate projections using RCP 4.5 for 2050 presented in Table 6 of Annex 2, Feasibility study.

For detailed information on the methodology for vulnerability assessment, see Section B of Annex 2, Feasibility study.

## Table 5: Criteria used for the vulnerability assessment and prioritization of project sites

| Vulnerability Component | Criteria |
|---|---|
| Exposure | * Change in the annual water balance between the baseline and the future scenario |
| Sensitivity | * Erosion risk |
| * Flood risk |
| * Biomass |
| * Area under irrigation |

In order to specify the impacts of climate change on agriculture in Bolivia, the changes in land cover and land use over the period 2010 – 2020 were analyzed. This was done by simulating the average hydrologic cycle for the climate of 1980-2014 compared with that of 2050–2079, using a model developed at the University of California, Berkeley.

# Key climate change hazards associated impacts and project adaptation interventions

## Climate risk
### Increasing temperatures and precipitation variability will reduce crop yields in the central part of the study area.

#### Effects of impact
- Rising temperatures will alter the suitability of crops for growth (FAO, 2017) and e.g. maize (Te et al., 2013).
- Reduce a certain crops' ability to withstand heat stress, drought, and flooding (Khan et al., 2007; Sarker, Kishore, 2015).

#### Adaptation interventions
- Livelihood diversification (meaning that farmers are not only dependent upon agriculture for their livelihood).
- Production and multiplication of heat-tolerant crops.
- Intercropping, sowing cover crops to reduce evaporation from exposed soil and by increasing soil organic matter.

#### Adaptation benefits
- Avoid losses of livelihoods for farmers by diversification into other income-generating activities (e.g. livestock or chain of hives) that lead to increased resilience.
- Farmers have adopted climate change adaptation practices such as agricultural production under irrigation, intercropping, and agroforestry.

## Rising temperatures result in soil degradation, the erosion of topsoil, and reduced yields, resulting in a loss of livelihoods for farmers. Changes in rainfall would increase runoff in the region. Uncovered or open land surfaces would be more prone to erosion due to increased runoff.

#### Effects of impact
- Soil management with the aim to increase soil fertility. This includes the application of organic manure / grass mulch, and leguminous cover crops for erosion control on crop / rotation / fallow fields.
- Agroforestry: Retention of trees in agricultural land (e.g. alley cropping, agroforestry).
- Conservation agriculture: Reduced tillage, reduced use of chemical fertilizers, reduced soil erosion, increased macromineral content and organic matter through surface runoff, mulching, cover crops, intercropping half-moons, crop rotation, and fallow.
- Soil management on degraded soils and slopes in the region. This includes the application of agroforestry practices that stabilize the slope, reduce erosion and retain soil erosion.

#### Adaptation interventions
- Enhanced habitats for wildlife and other species of ecosystem services.
- Use of other forms of soil degradation and land use change mitigation by restoring the soil not only through conservation agriculture but also by the adoption of alternative agricultural uses.

## Soil management with the aim to increase soil fertility. This includes the application of organic manure / grass mulch, and leguminous cover crops for erosion control on crop / rotation / fallow fields.
- Agroforestry: Retention of trees in agricultural land (e.g. alley cropping, agroforestry).
- Conservation agriculture: Reduced tillage, reduced use of chemical fertilizers, reduced soil erosion, increased macromineral content and organic matter through surface runoff, mulching, cover crops, intercropping half-moons, crop rotation, and fallow.
- Soil management on degraded soils and slopes in the region. This includes the application of agroforestry practices that stabilize the slope, reduce erosion and retain soil erosion.

#### Adaptation benefits
- Improved food security.
- Increase in income from the sale of livestock or other agricultural products.
- Increased crop production by improving the availability of nutrients.
- Enhanced habitats for wildlife and other species of ecosystem services.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND WINDING UP PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 24

As a result of the reduced precipitation and increased temperature, more than one-third of agricultural land in the region is affected by droughts, with the 2nd highest population density (see column **1**).

The number of droughts has been on the rise for the last few decades. One drought can cost an average of $30 million USD and affects 685,000 hectares of agricultural land in each country.

A drought is defined as a period when rainfall is less than half of the land cultivation potential (350-350 of 1,120 million hectares), resulting in crop losses (with soybeans), leading to food insecurity and poverty.

### Adaptation measures for regions with sufficient surface water for irrigation

Increased water efficiency by the farmers for irrigation and the agricultural production sector can increase the productivity of crops on irrigated land. Efficient use of surface water, efficient water management practices, and improved soil health can lead to an increase in crop yields of 75%.

### Adaptation measures for regions with limited surface water for irrigation

(1) Developing crop calendars
(2) Changing the existing crops to crops with lower water requirements
(3) Introducing drought-resistant varieties
(4) Ensuring irrigation is based on rainwater harvesting
(5) Acquisition of hydropower to maintain soil moisture and implementation of improved soil health practices.

### Establishment of agroecological management at the family and community level for lowland areas

The establishment of agroecological management strategies includes: (1) diversification of crops, (2) different crop rotations, (3) intercropping, (4) increasing biodiversity at the farm level, and (5) reducing the number of natural enemies.

### Reduced impacts of pests will be increased by improved soil health and increased resilience of the ecosystem to the outbreak of pests.

### More frequent and intense heatwaves

According to the projected increase in rural populations, this will result in a reduction of FAD

### Provision and implementation of anti-nail rust

Increased resistance of the farmers to hailstorms, and the improvement of soybean crop production and the food security of the country.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 25

## Between 1% and 38% of total production.

The records shows that in Bolivia, between 1% and 38% of the total production is affected by climate change. This total, 89%, represent four types of extreme events: floods (44%), hailstorms (20%), droughts (15%) and droughts (14%). The lower agriculture season (October to April) in 2021 and winter May to August 2021 were the most affected, with a total of 122,939 families. In the 2020-2021 agricultural season, floods occurred with 45% of the population followed by droughts.

### More frequent but unpredictable frost events

According to reports from themselves, farmers can observe the issue of losses of up to 80% in their crops although frosts are a recurring phenomenon. The changes observed in climate variability over the last decade mean that they are occurring more frequently and in areas where they have occurred less usually.

## Provision and implementation of thermal barriers

Increased resilience of the farming sector, therefore securing continued crop production is key to food security of the country.

58. Bolivia's existing policy instrument guiding the national actions on climate change is the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs). ## Related projects/interventions

### B

**RECEM Valles project (Odú3.1)** will support livelihoods, farmers, and private-public initiatives in the project area to adapt practices to preserve and restore environmental functions and services, including water resources, for women in secure land tenure.

59. Other relevant policies include Law No. 745 for the "Decade of Irrigation 2015-2025". The law defines strategies to be employed by the government to promote the development of irrigation systems, improve the quality and quantity of water resources, recycle wastewater for irrigation, and promote and state-of-the-art irrigation practices such as solar pumps, drip irrigation, and precision agriculture.

60. Furthermore, a series of decrees have recently been approved to implement the National Forests and Restoration Program (PNAR), which aims at reducing deforestation and forest degradation in Bolivia by 2030 through the reduction of deforestation by 20% in 2020. Additionally, the Plurinational Political and Strategy Action Plan for the Integral and Sustainable Management of Forests, Water Resources, and Natural Resources (PNAM) was approved to promote the conservation of biodiversity under the principles of Harmony with Mother Earth and Living Well. The project contributes directly to the implementation of these regulatory frameworks.

61. Moreover, in line with Law No. 745 for "Mother Earth and Integral Development for Water Life", the process for Development of Irrigation Production Systems (PIDA) was approved by the Plurinational Government in 2018 as a framework of decision-making, fostering the implementation of the Joint Mechanism for Adaptation and Migration (JAMAN). The same goal includes the implementation of integrated water resources management and the management of other components of Mother Earth's life, such as forests, soils, water resources, and biodiversity. JAMAN is a tool that allows the government to implement an approach (at the national level) for implementing non-market schemes for reducing deforestation and forest degradation through the development of irrigation systems in areas with high vulnerability to climate change and other threats.

62. The project will contribute directly to the implementation of the PNAR, PNAM, and JAMAN by promoting indigenous peoples' knowledge into account, in order to contribute to climate change mitigation and adaptation of ecosystems and water resources. This is done through the participation of indigenous peoples in the design and implementation of an integral watershed management (including restoration with native species) and the incorporation of traditional knowledge as a tool for the conservation and sustainable use of natural resources.

63. The project will also contribute to the implementation of Law No. 745 for "Mother Earth and Integral Development for Water Life" by promoting the PNAR, PNAM, and JAMAN through the participation of indigenous peoples in the design and implementation of hydropower projects in accordance with the regime and policies of the central government.

64. This project will contribute to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) 2030 by promoting the conservation of forests, resources and forests, without authority with regard to bans, despite their important role in territorial management and sustainable use of natural resources. The project will also contribute directly to SDG 15 on life on land through the design, implementing, and managing projects for the use of water resources. This is done in coordination with indigenous peoples and farmer communities. In this institutional context, the Federation of Associations of Municipalities (FAM) plays a key role as the main institution responsible for the implementation of the PNAR, PNAM, and JAMAN involved in the project. As a result, FAM is a EE in the project governance structure (via the Project Steering Committee, PSC, and Technical Working Group). The project will contribute to the achievement of SDG 15 by promoting the conservation and sustainable management of forests. Available at: https://curi.us/2h6t8b9

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND

## B (a): Theory of change narrative and diagram (lines: 1500 words, approximately 3 pictures plus diagram)

### Background analysis

The project is specifically tailored to address overland institutional, financial, and information barriers linked to climate change that inhibit residency building by vulnerable populations. At the local level, these barriers are socio-technical and organizational in nature. The project will build on lessons learned from previous projects. RECCIM-Vales addresses such barriers using an integrated (i.e., socio-ecological and institutional) and multi-scale approach. To address the overland institutional, financial, and information barriers linked to climate change that inhibit residency building by vulnerable populations at the local level, the project takes a transdisciplinary approach to addressing the interrelated social, technical, organizational, and economic factors that contribute to these barriers. The project will build on lessons learned from previous projects. RECCIM-Vales addresses such barriers using an integrated (i.e., socio-ecological and institutional) and multi-scale approach.

### Diagram

The diagram outlines the identified barriers and the project's approach to address them.


---
**Page 32**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 29

## Smallholder Farmers have limited technical support to apply the appropriate technical know-how and innovation to adopt traditional agroecosystems to increase climate variability.

Smallholder farmers have developed locally adapted agricultural practices that are well suited for their specific conditions, including soil type, rainfall patterns, and opportunities. However, such practices are not enough to cope with current and projected climate change in the Valley of Mexico region. The lack of access to information on climate change, coupled with limited access to extension services and agricultural practices, which often provide inadequate support. Moreover, the extension services cannot reach farmers who live far from urban centers.

## Smallholder Farmers lack support for marketing, selling opportunities for their agricultural produce, and opportunities to be part of value chains.

Smallholder farmers are not able to benefit from the increasing demand for organic products or other high-value agricultural chain value for smallholder farmers (i.e., those vulnerable to climate change) with an average of 2 ha of agricultural land. Smallholder farmers face a number of challenges in accessing markets, including low market access or low-income return (mainly due to transportation expenses combined with the cost and unfair selling prices). In addition, smallholder farmers are often excluded from value chains because they lack the necessary resources such as capital, infrastructure, and marketing skills. Smallholders could benefit from collective initiatives, such as enhanced collection and handling facilities. Moreover, the lack of market access is a major barrier to increasing their income levels and improving their livelihoods. The lack of equity access and reduced opportunities for smallholder farmers are also linked with gender inequality in many markets. Despite government efforts to improve the situation, these challenges remain a challenge for smallholder farmers.

## Limited financial services hinder smallholder farmers' ability to invest in climate change adaptation practices.

Smallholder farmers in the Vallea Macizo region have experience water shortages due to prolonged periods of drought and flooding. These events are exacerbated by climate change. Smallholder farmers who rely on rainfed agriculture, insufficient irrigation coverage and inadequate water use technologies result in low agricultural productivity. Moreover, smallholder farmers face a number of challenges when it comes to accessing credit from formal lenders such as commercial banks or microfinance institutions. This is because they lack collateral for loans and are often excluded due to their limited access to financial services.

## Limited access to information on climate change adaptation practices hinders smallholder farmers' ability to invest in the technologies and inputs they need.

Smallholder farmers face challenges accessing and participating in markets under stable and fair conditions. Lack of stable access to markets is a major barrier to increasing their income levels and improving their livelihoods. The lack of equity access and reduced opportunities for smallholder farmers are also linked with gender inequality in many markets. Despite government efforts to improve the situation, these challenges remain a challenge for smallholder farmers.

## Lessons from previous initiatives on establishing insurance products for smallholder farmers.

The project will be financed by the World Bank through FDES. Agricultural Insurance is one of the most important risk management tools available to producers and third-tier insurance companies. However, although in Bolivia and the countries of the Andean region some progress has been made in this area, the coverage provided by agricultural insurance products remains limited and confined to the poorest strata; many subsistence farmers, thus leaving out an important segment of production activities. The reasons for this are varied: (i) the lack of a clear definition of what constitutes an insured incident and how it has been hampered by a number of factors, among which we highlight the following: (ii) the high risk on the part of the farmers to obtain insurance; (iii) the low level of awareness about the benefits of agricultural insurance products. The latter is also at the high cost of obtaining and processing the information, (iv) lack of knowledge and understanding on the part of the farmers regarding the coverage provided by these products.

## Mitigado Programme is a national programme implemented by the central government which has a wide scope in terms of its objectives and activities.

The Mitigado Programme is a national programme implemented by the central government which has a wide scope in terms of its objectives and activities. The programme aims to (i) provide technical assistance through centralized administrative approach in providing physical infrastructure and equipment and (ii) normative constraints that limit

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 18

## Lack of systematic and long-term monitoring and evaluation processes, climate data and best practices in adaptation

One of the main challenges is that many projects are not systematically monitored or evaluated. They lack a clear monitoring framework, which is often coordinated or planned, or they do not adequately inform planning, policymaking and budgeting decisions. This results in poor project performance and limited learning from experience.

## Lack of integrated and participatory micro-watershed management skills and planning coordination to guide climate resilient practices

Lack of integrated watershed management skills and planning coordination is a major barrier to the adoption of climate-resilient practices, particularly on farms. The lack of knowledge about how to manage water resources in a changing climate, coupled with poor land-use planning, results in low levels of adaptation.

## Local institutions lacking strong governance mechanisms for climate change and natural resource use due to limited capacity

Local institutions are often weak or absent, which limits their ability to implement effective policies and practices. This is particularly true when it comes to managing water resources and adapting to the impacts of climate change.

## Adaptation barriers

Farmers will operate as a group, with the support of extension services, to ensure that they understand the exposure to climate hazards that subsistence and monoculture farmers face. The project will also provide training on climate variability implementation in the following stages in the project (i) design based on the local context; (ii) planning and implementation of the project activities; (iii) adaptation according to smallholders' needs and strategies for scaling up the capacities and skills of extension services, including the use of mobile technology for content building; and (iv) monitoring, evaluation, and continuous learning. ## B

### Capacity-building sessions for farm producers will be conducted under Output 4.2 to enhance their knowledge on climate risks, climate information and how to plan for adaptation.

The project ensures that all activities will consider the collection of local traditional knowledge and its integration with scientific knowledge in order to identify locally relevant adaptation solutions and information for the local context. Grounded sources, such as community-based organizations (CBOs), farmers’ associations, and other platforms, will enable the practical integration of these two types of knowledge. Applications of this knowledge will be supported by a gender-sensitive approach that will also promote the dialogue between traditional and scientific-technical knowledge with the aim to enhance women’s empowerment.

The capacity and technical advisory services integrate gender sensitivity as part of the project activities in order to ensure equal access for men and women to resources, equitable and active participation of women and their improved access to knowledge and information. The project will also promote the adoption of climate-smart agricultural technologies in agriculture, participation in the market opportunities to be fostered, and the development of a gender-sensitive approach that will enhance women’s empowerment towards gender equality.

### Technical advisory services on water management practices and the climate-proofing of irrigation systems will enhance the efficient use of water and provide technical support for the implementation of the project activities described under Output 2.1 on revitalizing irrigation systems and making them resilient to climate change by 2030. The project proposes two approaches: (a) a participatory approach based on the results of the hydrological modeling, which identifies regions with water deficit where there is no water deficit, the technification will enhance the efficient use of water, such as in the Bolivar Valley Micro-region; and (b) a participatory approach for regions where there is already a high deficit of water. The project proposes the following: (i) to implement climate-proofing irrigation systems during the dry season and therefore there is no risk for irrigation during the growing season; (ii) to implement climate-proofing irrigation systems based on rainwater harvesting, which can be used in the dry months.

The first approach will be implemented in 10 municipalities of the Bolivar Valley Micro-region. If not impossible for smallholders to generate sufficient income or access affordable credit to invest in water management infrastructure and technologies, the project proposes a participatory approach that supports them by implementing income-generating activities (e.g., association production enterprises), as described in Output 1.2). A total of 48 ha will be irrigated with climate-proofing irrigation systems during the dry season.

The second approach is to implement climate-proofing irrigation systems for smallholder farmers within the project area. This will result in increased and more stable harvests, which will contribute to food security and income generation opportunities when feasible, large cities within the project area. The second approach is contributing to the implementation of Output 2.1.

Additionally, the project proposes that under Output 2.2 will train smallholder farmers via FTS on adequate water use and management and invest in their implementation on the farm which includes aspects of crop management for enhancing soil moisture retention.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 32

## Institutional barriers

### Lack of systematic and holistic monitoring and evaluation processes and data

The project has envisioned an integrated monitoring and evaluation process to ensure that all the outputs are being achieved in line with the proposed goals, for long-term monitoring systems, and methodologies (Outputs 3.2 and 4.3).

The project will promote highly participatory processes to strengthen micro- watershed communities to develop governance mechanisms for water management by strengthening their capacity to manage water resources sustainably and to establish multi-stakeholder dialogue. Activities under Output 3.1 will implement and develop a monitoring system that ensures the availability of water resources in a quantifiable manner, with data available in permanent quality and quantity. Both the inventory of water sources and the monitoring systems will provide valuable information for the generation and strengthening of climate-resilient planning and management strategies for sustainable use of water resources.

Additionally, under this output, the project will support the development of methodologies to ensure that the collected data is used effectively by the monitoring system and thus support the resilience of agroecosystems. For the implementation of these activities, the project will provide training and technical assistance regarding water use in productive systems.

### Lack of integral and participatory watershed management plans for climate-resilient ecosystem restoration and conservation practices

The project will promote a multi-level approach to empower both local and national institutions to effectively integrate climate adaptation interventions across agricultural landscapes. The project will support the development of integrated watershed management and to foster conservation and restoration of ecosystem functions through the implementation of an integrated watershed management plan covering 17,510 ha of land, which is linked to the restoration of other ecosystems though soil and water conservation practices. Integrated watershed management will enhance and gradually secure the water cycle and groundwater recharge in the project area. The project will also support the development of methodologies for implementing climate-resilient agriculture (Component I) and climate proofing integrated watershed management plans (Component II). The ecosystem-based approach for restoration of ecosystem functions is in line with the Sustainable Land Management (SLM) component of the National Watershed Resources and Integrated Watershed Management.

## Project Interventions

### Limited access of smallholder farmers to finance and market information for resilience.

The project will strengthen the capacity of farmer organisations and develop tools and guidelines to facilitate farmers to overcome financial constraints. Under 4.2 (a) it will establish a revolving fund, which will provide credit to smallholders through innovative financial instruments that enable the implementation of climate-proofed agricultural practices. The revolving fund will be managed by the project and will have the capacity of communities, smallholders and associations on financial management and provision of microfinance services.

Project interventions in Component 1 and 2 will reduce the risk of borrowing from the commercial banks and other formal sources of finance for smallholder farmers.

At the same time, the project will reduce the non-systemic climate-related risk that may cause a default by smallholders to their lenders.

### Smallholder farmers lack support for marketing and selling opportunities on their agricultural produce.

The project will establish financing and faster income markets. Project interventions under Output 1.2 will aim to establish market fairs and mobile public resources centres in the project area, which will provide information about market prices and training in business development to ensure adequate planning and management of production and marketing activities. The project will also provide technical assistance for the part of business development; farmers will learn how to apply and manage production contracts and other financial instruments that are available on the market.

The project will build the adaptive capacity of the smallholder producers by generating additional and diversified income sources through the sale of their agricultural produce in markets, as well as through the provision of services such as agro-processing and value addition. The project will also provide technical assistance to key financial institutions to facilitate the creation of suitable financial products and services that meet the needs of smallholders.

The project will develop a Financial Strengthening Plan for FONASURGAE to leverage existing resources, including those from other donors, to support the development of new financial products and services. The project will also provide technical assistance to India to develop and implement innovative financing mechanisms and to analyze possibilities to extend this activity to other private financial institutions.

The project will provide technical assistance and guiding tools to key financial institutions to facilitate the creation of suitable financial products and services that meet the needs of smallholders (Outcome 4.3). It will also develop a Financial Strengthening Plan for FONASURGAE to leverage existing resources, including those from other donors, to support the development of new financial products and services.

The project will provide technical assistance to India to develop and implement innovative financing mechanisms and to analyze possibilities to extend this activity to other private financial institutions.

## B

### Theory of change of RECEM - Valles project

The project adopts an integrated approach to build climate resilience in the water and agriculture sectors generating benefits to the most vulnerable people, particularly women and youth, through the implementation of farm and watershed scale guided by solid national and local institutional structures with cataloging financing opportunities to ensure sustainability at both levels. The project contributes to the GCF Fund-level impacts (A1.0) Increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the most vulnerable people, contributing to poverty reduction; (A2.5) Improved water security for food production and human consumption; and (A4.5) Improved resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem services. The project achieves its paradigm shift objective through transforming management practices and enhancing climate resilient ecosystems and community capacity to adapt to climate change.

## Project Strategic Goal
- Strengthen climate resilience of smallholder farmers through diversified climate-resilient agricultural and water management practices.

### Volume 1: Financial Plan
#### Outcome 2.1
- Increase the number of households adopting climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices by more than 50% from baseline to endline.
- Activities:
  - Activity 2.1.1: Design, develop, and implement CSA training programs for farmers.
  - Activity 2.1.2: Provide access to credit facilities for CSA adoption.

#### Outcome 3.1
- Increase the market share of climate-smart agricultural products by more than 50% from baseline to endline.
- Activities:
  - Activity 3.1.1: Develop and implement marketing strategies for CSA products.
  - Activity 3.1.2: Establish partnerships with local and international buyers.

### Volume 2: Institutional Framework
#### Outcome 4.1
- Strengthen the institutional framework to support climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices by more than 50% from baseline to endline.
- Activities:
  - Activity 4.1.1: Develop and implement policies that support CSA adoption at the national level.

### Volume 3: Monitoring, Evaluation, Learning
#### Outcome 7.1
- Strengthen monitoring, evaluation, learning (MEL) systems for CSA practices by more than 50% from baseline to endline.
- Activities:
  - Activity 7.1.1: Develop and implement MEL frameworks for CSA adoption.

### Volume 4: Knowledge Management
#### Outcome 8.1
- Strengthen knowledge management systems for CSA practices by more than 50% from baseline to endline.
- Activities:
  - Activity 8.1.1: Develop and implement knowledge sharing platforms for CSA best practices.

## Project Activities
### Activity 1.1
- Design, develop, and implement CSA training programs for farmers.
- Activities:
  - Activity 1.1.1: Conduct baseline assessments of farmer needs and capacities.
  - Activity 1.1.2: Develop tailored training modules on CSA techniques.

### Activity 1.2
- Provide access to credit facilities for CSA adoption.
- Activities:
  - Activity 1.2.1: Establish partnerships with financial institutions.
  - Activity 1.2.2: Conduct awareness campaigns about the benefits of CSA financing.

### Activity 3.2
- Develop and implement marketing strategies for CSA products.
- Activities:
  - Activity 3.2.1: Conduct market research to identify potential buyers.
  - Activity 3.2.2: Develop promotional materials for CSA products.

### Activity 4.2
- Develop and implement policies that support CSA adoption at the national level.
- Activities:
  - Activity 4.2.1: Engage with government stakeholders in policy development.
  - Activity 4.2.2: Monitor and evaluate policy implementation effectiveness.

### Activity 7.2
- Develop and implement MEL frameworks for CSA adoption.
- Activities:
  - Activity 7.2.1: Establish monitoring indicators for CSA practices.
  - Activity 7.2.2: Conduct regular evaluations of CSA adoption progress.

### Activity 8.2
- Develop and implement knowledge sharing platforms for CSA best practices.
- Activities:
  - Activity 8.2.1: Collect and analyze data on CSA success stories.
  - Activity 8.2.2: Organize workshops to share CSA experiences among farmers.


## Barriers and risks
### Financial barriers
- Lack of financial resources for CSA adoption.
- High costs associated with CSA practices.

### Institutional barriers
- Weak institutional capacity in implementing CSA policies.
- Lack of coordination between different stakeholders involved in CSA implementation.

### Knowledge barriers
- Limited knowledge about CSA techniques among farmers.
- Inadequate awareness and understanding of the benefits of CSA practices.

# B.3. Project/programme description (max. 2500 words, approximately 5 pages)

## Project objective

85. The project objective is to increase the resilience of smallholder farmers in the Valle Macro-region of Bolivia. This will be achieved by implementing regular and equitable access to climate information, improving the management of small-scale irrigation systems, managing agricultural ecosystems sustainably, on-term climate-proofing irrigation systems, and strengthening the corresponding governance and institutional capacities at local level to support this objective.

86. The project is designed with 4 components that seek to achieve climate-resilient agriculture in the Valle Macro region through an integrative and systemic perspective. The project design includes 4 interdependent components that seek to achieve climate-resilient agriculture in the Valle Macro region, which will be managed by local communities, including their functions and services, and which the local population and authorities can manage themselves.

Component 1: Climate-resilient agricultural systems and practices

- Component 2: Smallholder water resources service to reduce the risk from droughts and low rainfall

- Component 3: Climate-resilient livestock management

- Component 4: Enabling conditions created to implement and upscale climate-resilient agrometeorological management, climate-informed decision-making, and adaptive capacity building.

87. Component 1 of the project focuses on climate resilient agriculture implemented and managed by farmers for increasing the resilience of smallholder farmers in the Valle Macro region against the main climate risks the project seeks to overcome: the increase in average temperatures and decreasing trend in rainfall. These practices include a shift to producing crops with greater resilience to higher temperatures and other conditions.


---
**Page 41**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 18

## Component 2: Climate-resilient agricultural systems with drip irrigation

### Objectives:
- Promote activities based on supporting farmers in marketing their products and diversification of their agricultural base income streams.
- Focuses on water use reduction (the water losses through inefficient irrigation systems).
- Activities promote the modernization and revitalization of existing irrigation systems, including the installation of new ones.

### Activities under Component 2:
1. **Modernization and revitalization of existing irrigation systems:** This includes the implementation of technological innovation in irrigation water management practices.
2. **Incorporation of drip irrigation:** This involves the installation of drip irrigation systems or the conversion of existing sprinkler systems to drip irrigation, as well as the harvesting of rainwater among others.

### Climate-resilient practices:
- These practices are consistent with the results of the climate vulnerability assessment and are suitable for the regions where there is no water deficit.
- They ensure that the water resources can be restricted but conserved, ensuring availability not only for the current year but also for future years. This will help to ensure the sustainable management of the water base to better address the irrigation demands as implemented.

### Component 3: Ensuring the sustainability of water resources
- This component ensures that the water resources are conserved and managed sustainably.
- It includes activities such as the development of a detailed project design, which is described in section 17 of Annex C, Feasibility Study. The project will continue to be implemented through the same institutional structure.

### Component 4: Institutional strengthening
- This component aims at strengthening institutions for climate finance to sustain and strengthen the Components 1, 2, and 3.

## Project area

This project is designed to promote the social and productive activities of smallholders and their organizations and communities so that they not only adapt to climate change impacts and vulnerability but also build their livelihoods in a sustainable manner. The project will cover a detailed description of the project design and rationale see section 17 in Annex C, Feasibility Study.

### Project area
- **Valles macro-region:** This region includes the provinces of Tarija, Santa Cruz, Potosí, Chuquisaca, Cochabamba, and La Paz (Bolivia). The criteria used for prioritization include:
    - High vulnerability to climate change.
    - Contribution to the basic food basket of the main cities.
    - Areas where agriculture is a key livelihood activity.

### Resources and Integrated Watershed Management
- A map showing the project area will be included in section 17 of Annex C, Feasibility Study. The project area includes:
    - High vulnerability areas: These are areas with high water risk, where climate change is likely to impact the aquifer conditions.
    - Priority areas for conservation of biodiversity: These areas have good condition and generate exceptional services in particular hydrogeological contexts.

### Activities
- **Water activities:** This includes the development of local methodologies and field trials of advanced technologies. The project will monitor and evaluate new knowledge, lessons learned, and best practices.
- **Project implementation:** This involves the identification of beneficiaries, selection criteria, systematic coding, and a plan for dissemination to producers, local governments, NGOs, and others through the Valles Macro-region.

### Project Implementation
- A detailed project design for the first year will be developed in collaboration with provincial and national authorities regarding enhancing agricultural systems, watershed restoration and management, and sustainable land use. The water management program of this project will be developed during the first year of the project implementation.

## Direct beneficiaries

The direct beneficiaries of the proposed project are vulnerable smallholders (defined as small-scale family farmers relying on agriculture for their livelihoods) in Bolivia's Valles Macro-region. These include 10,543 families with an average size of 2 ha who are at greatest risk of being pushed into conditions of extreme food insecurity due to climate change (all of those in communities that have been identified as vulnerable). The project will also benefit the local government and other stakeholders.

### Project beneficiaries
- **Beneficiaries:** These include approximately 0.5 ha of land, which primarily produces maize, vegetables, potatoes, wheat. Interventions will be also of direct benefit to central and sub-national government representatives and other stakeholders in the Valles Macro-region.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 39

## B

### Component 1: Strengthened food and income security in changing climate through climate resilient agricultural systems

#### Concept Note 1 aims at developing resilient production systems through the implementation of climate-resilient agriculture technologies (including thermal blankets, anti-hail nets, hydrogels), crop diversification, agro-ecological management and improved livestock practices. This approach is based on the assumption that a diversified portfolio of climate-resilient agricultural systems is a strategy for the conservation and recovery of degraded soil which also provides mitigation co-benefits such as carbon sequestration in soils and vegetation. The concept note proposes to support smallholder farmers, particularly women, who are transforming and reinvigorating agricultural systems to effectively support sustainable development and ensure food security in a changing climate. This approach contributes to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) related to food systems and thus restoration of ecosystem functions and resilience to extreme climate events.

#### The concept note is aligned with the following three pillars of the Climate Change Adaptation Strategy for the Integrated and Sustainable Management of Forests and Mother Earth, and the Economic and Social Development Sector Plan 2018-2023 (ESDP). It contributes to the achievement of the SDGs related to food systems by supporting sustainable use of water resources, adaptation to climate change and enhancement of forest carbon stocks. Carbon sequestration in soils and vegetation is a mitigation co-benefit of this approach.

#### Additionally, total capacity will be developed among rural producers, to ensure sustainability beyond the scope of the project. The concept note proposes to support the development of local capacities through training, mentoring, technical assistance, and identification of opportunities for productive diversification. Both are adaptation strategies in themselves and offer alternative sources of income, which could ensure the long-term sustainability of the project investments.

#### During the International Year of Family Farming 2014, small-scale family farmers were defined as a form of agroecological, livestock, fisheries, foresters, aquaculture, and pastoralism that is managed or owned by family members; relies predominantly on family labour, including children; and has a primary objective to meet the food needs of its members. Small-scale family farms are also referred to in the Agricultural Development Sector Plan 2018-2023 (Towards 2050) of the Ministry of Rural Development and Lands (MVRDYL) as "family farms". The concept note proposes to support smallholder farmers, particularly women, who are transforming and reinvigorating agricultural systems as a strategy that diversifies production in harmony with Mother Earth, or to provide food security for future generations. Small-scale family farms can be found on indigenous lands, including ancestral territories, national forests, communal land of origin or indigenous peasant territory; (ii) community properties; (iii) peasant plots; and (iv) smallholdings.

#### Ministry of Foreign Affairs of the Plurinational State of Bolivia (2013). https://www.cancilleria.gob.bo/moneda/motivo/1703

## Component 2: Smallholder water resources secured to reduce the risks from droughts and rain fall

106. Component 2 aims to become an important resilient irrigation technology for practices of efficient use of water and counteract the water deficit that is a consequence of the effects of climate change. Component 2 was designed with the various irrigation scenarios as indicated in the GIM Plan. The activities within this component are aimed towards the revitalization of existing irrigation systems, the development of new irrigation systems to reduce droughts and low rainfall. It will also develop capacities and provide training for the management of climate-resilient irrigation systems.

## Output 2.1: Enhanced and modernized on-farm climate-proof irrigation systems (SCF - 536; CO-FINANCING: 20 M€)

### Activity 1.2.1a
- i) To identify and demonstrate technologies for those regions with moderate water deficit (10-20 percent of the water supply available). Climate proofing the irrigation systems in these regions consist of harvesting rainwater that will be used for other purposes.
- ii) To identify and demonstrate technologies for those regions where there is no water deficit. The focus here would be on enhancing existing irrigation systems to ensure they are climate-proof.

### Activity 1.2.3
- i) To implement technologies for those region, drip and sprinkler irrigation technologies will be promoted to enhance the efficiency of water use.
- ii) Adaptation practices will be focused on: (a) changing the planting time to ensure that sowing starts in the rainy season; (b) using drought-resistant varieties; (c) improving soil management techniques to reduce evaporation and water requirements. All these adaptation practices are focused on coping with drought.

### Activity 1.2.3
- i) To identify and demonstrate technologies for those regions where there is no water deficit. Climate proofing the irrigation systems in this region will be used to further design the correct measures given by the hydrological balance, under activity 2.1.3, representing communities and households that are most vulnerable.

## Sustainability of Irrigation O&M

The next phase of the project is designed to build on lessons learned from past irrigation efforts, through a focus on interventions that are financially viable for farmers, and through establishing an operation and maintenance (O&M) programme that effects better water use.

### 5.10 The financial analysis presented in Annex 3 shows that projects intervention reduces farmer's climate change-related losses and increases their income by up to 28%. This is because the project interventions reduce the costs of irrigation, which are mainly related to financial barriers relates to farmers' risk aversion, access to capital, and knowledge and prevailing practice constraints. The project also provides a range of other benefits for farmers including improved water use efficiency, increased productivity in agricultural activities (including irrigated) and provide increased financial certainty to sustain these measures into the future. The farmers improved and more predictable income increases their willingness to access productive credit, while the project empowers an awareness of irrigation as a feasible option for increasing farm productivity.

### 5.11 Regular monitoring of the O&M PROGRADUO program and self-management of irrigation study showed that after three years only, 20% of the irrigated systems presented problems for continuity and sustainability. The factors contributing to this were (a) the lack of a proper maintenance plan, (b) the poor quality of the irrigation infrastructure, and (c) the low quality and safety of the water source; (d) the functionality of the irrigation infrastructure; and (e) the boreholes located in the agricultural area.

### 5.12 The results of studies on irrigation projects in the 1990s (ICRISAT, 1991), reported that the provision of Technical Assistance (TA) to the farmers was not sufficient for the sustainability and continuity of these projects because the project had an exclusively technical approach focused on delivery of equipment, without taking into account the social aspects related to the use of irrigation. The studies also observed that the successful technical irrigation projects are those with support managed by NGOs and/or local communities.

### 5.13 The results from the study conducted in the micro-watersheds of the project area showed that the farmers contributed directly to the design of the current project proposal, which includes measures that ensure the sustainability of the irrigation systems, such as: (a) the establishment of a water users association (WUA), (b) the creation of a technical team and capacity development of stakeholders for the correct operation and timely maintenance of irrigation systems, and (c) the implementation of an O&M plan to ensure the continuity and sustainability of the project investments in irrigation management. The O&M (Annex 21) provides further details on how these lessons have been incorporated into the design of the project.

### 5.14 The project includes clear measures to engage and strengthen the continuum capacities of the beneficiaries and the communities, through the establishment of a water users association (WUA), which will be responsible for irrigation management. This includes training to ensure that water storage facilities are kept clean of organic matter, in order to avoid methane emissions from these facilities.

### Component 3: Restored and conserved micro-watersheds and ecosystem functions and services

Component 3 aims to improve the water security of communities and smallholder farmers, to ensure the sustainability of their climate change adaptation measures. The project will facilitate the development of integral micro-watershed management and water use plans to enhance water availability for irrigation in the future. This will be achieved through the establishment of a WUA, which will manage and/or micro-banks, as a tool for informed, long-term, climate-sensitive planning processes. The proposed investments comprising this component, will include restoration processes in micro-watersheds with a new to restore and conserve soils.
