## Component 4: Enabling conditions created to implement and upscale climate-resilient agroecological management, climate-informed integrated micro-watershed management, and access to financial mechanisms

### Activity 1.2.1: Facilitating the development of financial products to enhance a long-term resilience-building for smallholder farmers.

**Description:** This component aims to develop financial products that will enable smallholder farmers in the project area to adopt climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies, including integrated land management plans (including Integrated Territorial Development Plans, PTDDs) focused on adaptation and mitigation to climate change and disease risks. The development of these financial products will be based on the principles of integrated water resources management and integral watershed management and conservation.

### Activity 1.2.2: Facilitating the development of financial products for sustainable land use management

**Description:** This component aims to develop financial products that will enable smallholder farmers in the project area to adopt climate-resilient agricultural practices and technologies, including integrated land management plans (including Integrated Territorial Development Plans, PTDDs) focused on adaptation and mitigation to climate change and disease risks. The development of these financial products will be based on the principles of integrated water resources management and integral watershed management and conservation.

### Activity 12.3: Facilitating the identification of financial mechanisms to support long-term climate-resilient agricultural practices

**Description:** This component takes a strong, innovative approach to facilitate the identification of financial mechanisms to support long-term climate-resilient agricultural practices in the project area. The objective is to identify and develop innovative financial instruments tailored to the needs of smallholder farmers in the project area. These financial instruments will be developed through partnerships with national funds as well as public and private sector financial institutions such as Development Bank (DB), Associations, Development Banks (ADB), etc., and other international organizations such as the World Bank (WB) and the Asian Development Bank (ADB). In addition to these financial mechanisms, this component also aims to facilitate the development of partnerships with national and international financial institutions such as the Productive Development Bank (PDB), Associations, Development Banks (ADB), etc.

### Activity 12.4: Facilitating the identification of innovative financing instruments for climate-resilient agricultural practices

**Description:** This activity will be used by all municipalities through the FAM and agreements signed with SENAMHI.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 49

## Output 4.2 Improved financial mechanisms that support climate-resilient agricultural production and irrigation systems to mobilize increased finance for farmers (GCF: 1.84M)

### Background
125 Agriculture finance and agricultural insurance are strategically important for building resilience to climate change and advancing the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). Despite significant progress in recent years, access to agricultural finance often remains a challenge. Limited access to finance prevents many farmers from adopting new technologies or practices that can improve their productivity.

### Objectives
- A mismatch between the financial needs of farmers and the types of financial products and services available on the market.
- High transaction costs due small-scale and dispersed customers, coupled with under-developed customer information systems.
- Market failure due to high investment risk, information gaps and asymmetries, and weak risk mitigation mechanisms.

### Proposed Actions
130 The GCF funds will provide necessary co-investment funds, which together with the technical assistance provided by the financial institutions, will lead to the actual financial mechanism: 1) Design and implementation of pilot initiatives of green finance and insurance products for smallholder farmers in Mozambique; 2) Develop a Financial Sector Strategy and a Financial Strengthening Plan for FOMABISQUE and E3; 3) Develop and implement innovative insurance mechanisms to NSA. The project will also support the development of an index-based insurance product that can be used as a tool to mainstream climate change in the Water Funds and the creation of the index-based infrastructure. The development of new financial instruments and products will contribute to the achievement of the SDGs, particularly Goal 13 on taking urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts.

### Activities
131 Activities under this output will strengthen and design - together with national and sub-national entities, financial institutions for rural agriculture, and other stakeholders - a set of innovative financial mechanisms with a focus on ecosystem-based climate change. These mechanisms will be more viable and attractive thanks to the interventions in Components 1 and 2 that reduced the cost of credit and increased access to finance. The project will also support the development of new insurance products to improve their capacity to access funding for climate adaptation actions. This output will promote association to foster inclusion, reduce transaction costs, and increase financial resilience.

### Outputs
132 Project outputs include: 1) Design and implementation of pilot initiatives of green finance and insurance related funds and scale up and increment the number of beneficiaries. Additionally, the project will provide specialized Technical Assistance (TA) for the development of innovative insurance mechanisms and to analyze the potential for extending this activity to other private financial institutes.

### Activities

This will be done through the design of innovative financial product and instrument design, research, capacity building, and promotion of the fund. #### Development and implementation of project-related funds and assets to increase the number of beneficiaries (project interventions will develop a Financial Strengthening Plan for FONAGROQUE to support the development of financial instruments).

- 100 Real guarantees are the traditional means used to guarantee loans, for example, for a loan for the purchase of a house, the guarantor is usually a family member or friend who agrees to be responsible if the borrower fails to repay. Non-conventional guarantees are aimed at facilitating the obtaining of loans from the productive sector, so that the client can present any type of asset as collateral (a car, a motorcycle, a bicycle, a tractor, a piece of machinery or equipment, inventory or artwork, an invention, among others). Non-conventional guarantees will go through a process of evaluation, assessment and control by the financial institution. The purpose is to reduce the risk for the financial institution.
- To date has not been implemented an agile and differentiated product for agricultural or natural resources projects. RECEM VIE is also seeking to strengthen the capacities of lending institutions in general, especially in rural areas, to provide credit services that are more appropriate to the needs of small farmers. # Overview of the financial instruments that the project will support to ensure climate resilience and project sustainability.

## Climate resilient credits and concessional credits lines

| Financial mechanism | Actions | Project Interventions to mobilize finance on the mechanisms |
|---|---|---|
| **Climate resilient credits and concessional credits lines** | Customizing agriculture financial products and services for climate resilient agriculture: The project will provide the following technical assistance to the national banking sector (especially smallholder farmers) to engage in climate resistant agriculture: (i) remove the barriers that prevent an increase in agricultural financing; (ii) integrate principles of climate resilience (both ecological and social dimensions) into credit assessment criteria, including the use of scientific research on effective and affordable innovations related to credit assessment criteria; (iii) develop and implement climate resilient credits and concessional credit lines; (iv) test and implement climate resilient credits and concessional credit lines in pilot banks. | |
| **Productive Development Bank (PDB), Foundation for Financial Development (FONAFIFO)** | Faster measure to enable agriculture financing policies: The Project will provide technical assistance for designing and implementing financial products and services that remove the barriers that present an increase in agricultural financing; (i) design technical assistance to the national banking sector (especially smallholder farmers) to engage in climate resistant agriculture, including the development of climate resilient credits and concessional credit lines; (ii) develop and implement climate resilient credits and concessional credit lines in pilot banks. | |
| **National funding** | Technical assistance for FONASBOQUE to improve and implement the FONASBOQUE financing program in the Valles Macro region: Considering that sub-national governments are among the financial institutions most affected by climate change, technical assistance for the improvement of the FONASBOQUE financing program should be provided. This will include a joint effort between the national government and municipal governments located in the Valles macro-region. This will be undertaken through the implementation of a pilot project with the aim that FONASBOQUE and sub-national governments, together with members of the financial sector, can work to define the scope and priorities of the program focusing on the conservation of natural resources and the protection of ecosystem functions and services for climate change resilience. | |

In its regional approach, the FONASBOQUE financing program integrates the inclusion of climate change mitigation and adaptation measures in the design of projects that include the conservation of water sources, wetlands, and organic matter.

## Strengthening capacities of FONABOSQUE's staff in climate-resilient agriculture

The project will provide capacity building to the executive director and his team on the principles of integrated micro-watershed management, restoration of ecosystems, and sustainable land use practices, with a view to include such products in their financing criteria.

## Strengthening capacities of sub-national governments in developing and implementing climate-resilient agriculture

The project will provide capacity building for government staff and participants of the consultative terminal platform on the design of the risk-indexed micro-insurance product. This will enable FONABOSQUE, for financing integrated micro-watershed management and sustainable land use practices, to include such products in their financing criteria.

## Strengthening capacities of the terminal platforms to prioritize climate-resilient agriculture

Considering that FONABOSQUE is a public institution with limited resources, it will be necessary to consider the costs. RECEM Valley will then from the consultative terminal platform in designing and implementing the risk-indexed micro-insurance product.

## Risk-indexed micro insurance

### Terms of agricultural insurance
The project will initiate the development and implementation of an agricultural insurance scheme for the Macizo region, which will consider the application of non-conventional insurance products. The project will also provide technical assistance to the terminal platforms in designing and implementing the risk-indexed micro-insurance product.

### Design of the risk-indexed micro insurance
The project will provide technical assistance in the design of the risk-indexed micro insurance for the Macizo region, which will be implemented by FONABOSQUE. The terminal platforms will be organized to provide a platform to engage between farmers and insurance companies. The design process will take into consideration the needs and local situation of the most vulnerable farmers.

### Design and dissemination of the implementation methodology
The project will develop a methodology for implementing and accessing financial and insurance services in the Macizo region, which will serve as a strategy for risk-indexed micro-insurance to be implemented by FONABOSQUE. The methodology will also provide a framework for designing innovative forms targeting the most vulnerable farmers.

### Capacity building of relevant local actors
Smallholder farmers and their organizations will receive capacity-building support on the relevant processes and practices (e.g., crop diversification, soil conservation) caused by climate events and natural disasters. This will enable agro-climatic platforms and the meteorological services to provide information about production planning and protection. With this, the project will be able to disseminate innovative forms of insurance products to the most vulnerable smallholder farmers.

### Provision of technical assistance for risk-indexed micro-insurance decision-making
Through the institutional strengthening of public decision-makers in the Macizo region, the project will help to enable agro-climatic platforms and the meteorological services to provide information about production planning and protection. With this, the project will be able to disseminate innovative forms of insurance products to the most vulnerable smallholder farmers.

## Water Funds

### Improvement of water fund mechanism: Staff of the different water funds will receive technical assistance to ensure that their funds are designed and implemented in a way that ensures sustainable water management, restoration of ecosystem functions and services, and climate change adaptation.

1. **Develop a line of financing for mitigation and adaptation to climate change**, with the aim of generating sustainable and resilient agricultural production systems, improving water availability and access, reducing the current flooding season, conserving water sources and recovering degraded ecosystems.
2. **Define the funding criteria and the funding mechanism**, including improved monitoring and evaluation frameworks as it relates to climate change adaptation and mitigation.

### Capacity building of potential beneficiaries: Through the project, smallholder farmers, local organizations and the consultation process will be strengthened capacity in terms of knowledge, procedures and requirements to enable them to access the water funds.

* Platforms for dialogue between the project with different stakeholders and objectives.

## B.4. Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

### Governance
The proposed governance mechanisms for project execution, coordination and oversight have been agreed in close consultation with the Ministry of Environment and Water (MINAM) – at behalf of the GCF), and the Federation of Associations of Municipalities of Peru (FAMP).

The FAM will be seen as both Accredited Entity (AE) and Executing Entity (EE) (GCF process for this project has a set-up with that arrangement). The AE will be responsible for the overall management of this project, including: (i) all aspects of project appraisal; (ii) administrative, financial, and technical oversight and supervision throughout project implementation; (iii) ensuring funds are disbursed in accordance with the approved budget and GCF rules and regulations as well as the timeliness and quality of reporting to the GCF; and (iv) project closure and evaluation. GCF will assume these responsibilities through its accredited entity, the FAM. The AE and EE will be subject to a Financial Agreement (FA) and/or Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) between FAM and GCF.

The FA/FAA will be signed by the FAM and GCF. The FA/FAA will include a team comprising relevant staff from the FA Country Office in Bolivia, the FAO Regional Office for Latin America and the Caribbean in Santiago (Chile), and FAO

## Executive Entity (EE)

142. FAO will act as EE and will be responsible for the management of the GCF proceeds and will bear the overall responsibility for fulfilling the EE functions of this project.
143. More detailed implementation arrangements of the project are described in Figure 10 below.

### Project Implementation Structure

144. The project will be implemented under the following structure:

- a. FAO, the AE, and as the EE all GCF-funded project activities will be responsible for the GCF proceeds. In this context, FAO will act as the Project Management Unit (PMU) with overall responsibility and will:
  - i. coordinate the work of two Territorial Operating Units (TOUs), led by the PMU.
  - ii. lead the PMU unit collectively perform all EE functions for the project.
- b. The Goll, acting through MMAuA, and FAM will be EEs for activities funded by their co-financing resources. As such:
  - i. the PMU will coordinate with the Goll, acting through MMAuA, and FAM to ensure inter-institutional coordination.
  - ii. the PMU will lead the project implementation in close collaboration with the Goll, acting through MMAuA, and FAM.

### Project Steering Committee (PSC)

145. The PSC is responsible for the overall direction of the project, including:
- a. approving the project's strategic framework.
- b. ensuring inter-institutional coordination.
  - i. The PSC will be comprised of high-level representatives from MMAuA, MoHST, and FAO.
- c. finalizing decisions on the operation of the PSC.

### Technical Committee (TC)

146. The TC is responsible for:
- a. ensuring inter-institutional coordination among the partners and consistency of the outputs with the project's strategic framework.
  - i. The TC will be comprised of high-level representatives from MMAuA, MoHST, and FAO.
- b. approving the project's implementation plan.
- c. approving the project's budget.
- d. approving the project's work plan.
- e. approving the project's monitoring and evaluation framework.

### Project Management Unit (PMU)

147. The PMU will be responsible for:
- a. leading the project implementation at the national level, including:
  - i. project coordination with MMAuA, MoHST, and FAO.
  - ii. ensuring inter-institutional coordination.
  - iii. implementing the project's work plan.
  - iv. monitoring progress against the project's objectives.

### Territorial Operating Units (TOUs)

148. The TOUs will be established at the local level to serve as the key channel of communication between the PMU and MMAuA, MoHST, and FAO. Each TOU will be located in Chikwawa, Phalombe, Piusa, and Taratu. Each TOU will be headed by a Regional Project Director, appointed by the PMU. The Regional Project Director will be supported by:
- i. a Technical Assistant.
  - a. Nutrition Specialist(s).
  - b. Soil/Sanitation Specialist(s), as appropriate.
- ii. Administrative Assistant.

149. The Regional Project Director will ensure effective liaison and coordination with the PMU and other TOUs during the implementation of the project activities.

## For Component 1, GCF process will be executed directly by FAO and will be utilized to:

- The implementation of climate-resilient technologies such as thermal blankets, anti-hail nets, hydrogels and solar tents.
These technologies will be disseminated among the beneficiaries of the project with the technical assistance to guarantee their proper use and maintenance.
- Promote sustainable and resilient agriculture through practices such as conservation agriculture, crop rotation, productive diversification, intercropping, agroforestry, and integrated pest management.
- Promotion of Land Use and Command Management Plans in coordination with MDNY (Ministry of Rural Development) and Local Government Units (LGUs) to ensure the proper use of land by Agriculture and Forestry (AFO) to promote the sustainable use of the land.
- Identification of high-value commercial and product value products.
- Comply with the national regulation of the participatory guarantee systems (third-party certification) in order to participate in the market, as well as the analysis of fair prices
- Improvement of the existing marketing centers for agrocommercial products.
- The promotion and participation in fairs to promote the products to achieve access to national and international markets.

157. For Component 2, GCF proceeds will be executed directly by FAO and will be utilized to:
- Develop and implement resilient irrigation technologies for efficient use of water.
- Purchase and distribute drought-resistant seeds and other inputs to smallholder farms; reduce the risks of droughts and low rainfall generated by the effects of climate change.
- Establish and expand existing water storage systems, such as community reservoirs and family water tanks, drip and sprinkler irrigation technologies, lining of irrigation canals and improved storage to reduce seepage and other losses.
- Upgrading an inventory of irrigation systems at municipal level and farmer level.

158. For Component 3, GCF proceeds will be executed directly by FAO and will cover the costs of:
An integrated water resources management project in the micro-watershed of the Project Area:
- Development of integrated watershed management plans and local water use plans, as well as implementation of appropriate technologies to improve ecosystem functions and services that will stabilize water supply for irrigation purposes in the micro-watershed.
The Project Area is located within a broader landscape-based climate change adaptation strategy, including co-benefit the potential of carbon fixation and capture of the biomass and soil.

159. Finally, for Component 4, the GCF proceeds will be executed directly by FAO and will finance:
- Design of Sector Development Plans for comprehensive development.
- Identification of key stakeholders, actors and institutions and institutionalization of the National Early Warning System to ensure that this information reaches the producer for decision-making planning.
- Strengthening of the capacity of local communities in terms of awareness raising and sharing of lessons; design and implementation of pilot evidences of green credits and concessional credit lines and provide technical assistance to farmers.
- Develop a Financial Strengthening Plan for FONAGROQUE to cover new climate-related funds, scale up, and improve its services.
- Provide specialized Technical Assistance to INSa to develop and implement innovative insurance mechanism and to analyze the potential of carbon capture and sequestration.
- Generation and collection of evaluative data, and impact evaluation for the project.

Track record of FAO role as AG and EE
FAO is a UN agency that provides development cooperation in the member countries on issues of food and agriculture, aiming at the goal of food and nutritional security for all (SDG1) and increasingly focusing, under its strategic priorities, to address the challenges posed by climate change. FAO has been working with governments of national and local level, in response to climate change, the deterioration of natural resources and degradation of ecosystems.
At a global level, FAO is one of the three UN agencies that have signed the Global Framework for Climate-Resilient Agriculture (GFRA) and concrete actions to support priority public policies and technical assistance programs of the countries in line with the global framework. The GFRA was adopted by the 2015 Paris Agreement on climate change, which sets out a set of five priority pillars (SPF) that help eliminate hunger, food insecurity and malnutrition: SPF1 Make agriculture forestry and fisheries more resilient to climate change; SPF2 Ensure sustainable land management; SPF3 Promote sustainable use of water resources in line with the three FAO regional initiatives (RIs) approved at the LAC-36/90th FAO Latin American Regional Conference in 2015.
At a regional level, FAO is one of the three UN agencies that have signed the GFRA and concrete actions to support priority public policies and technical assistance programs of the countries in line with the global framework. The GFRA was adopted by the 2015 Paris Agreement on climate change, which sets out a set of five priority pillars (SPF) that help eliminate hunger, food insecurity and malnutrition: SPF1 Make agriculture forestry and fisheries more resilient to climate change; SPF2 Ensure sustainable land management; SPF3 Promote sustainable use of water resources in line with the three FAO regional initiatives (RIs) approved at the LAC-36/90th FAO Latin American Regional Conference in 2015.
At a national level, FAO is guided by the Governing bodies with Ministries of Agriculture, Forestry and Fishery and related institutions regarding land use and water resources management. The GFRA was adopted by the 2015 Paris Agreement on climate change, which sets out a set of five priority pillars (SPF) that help eliminate hunger, food insecurity and malnutrition: SPF1 Make agriculture forestry and fisheries more resilient to climate change; SPF2 Ensure sustainable land management; SPF3 Promote sustainable use of water resources in line with the three FAO regional initiatives (RIs) approved at the LAC-36/90th FAO Latin American Regional Conference in 2015.
FAO Bolivia
The Bolivian Representation is a body since 1978, providing technical assistance for the development of local, municipal, regional and national policies, programs and projects focused on eliminating hunger and malnutrition, especially supporting vulnerable populations around the adverse effects of climate change through risk reduction-management projects. ### 13 FAO BOLIVIA OFFICE PROJECTS

The Fund is supporting 13 projects implemented by the FAO Bolivia Office. These projects are carried out through a sequenced-agility assessment process, which include FAD-Bolivia Country Office, the Regional Office for Latin America and the Caribbean (RO-LAC), and the Headquarters in Rome.

Each project is led by a team of experts from the FAO Bolivia Office, as well as a team of officials and specialist consultants in the areas of forestry, natural resources, risk management, and climate change. Each project also has a local partner organization that provides technical assistance to communities at risk.

In addition, the FAO Bolivia Office has professional field staff and specialized national consultants with a wide thematic experience to support the implementation of the projects.

### 14 THE RECAM VALUE PROJECT AREA IS HIGHLY DIVERSE AND EXTREMELY VULNERABLE

The Valles Macroregion consists of 65 municipalities covering an area of 83,620 km² (a map at a comparison is this twice the size of Switzerland). It covers an altitudinal range from sea level to over 4,000 meters. The region has a population of approximately 1 million people and represents one-third of Bolivia's total population.

The project area is one of the lowest in the world (27 inhabited/km²) and road infrastructure in all areas in Bolivia are very poor. The average annual rainfall varies from less than 500 mm to over 3,000 mm per year.

The project will target 23,681 agroecological management + 4,449 ha agricultural land + 17,513 ha of areas in prioritized municipalities. The average annual rainfall varies from less than 500 mm to over 3,000 mm per year.

The average cost for moderate degraded areas in Latin America is between USD $200ha and $400/ha. Therefore, the project will target 17,513 ha of areas in prioritized municipalities with a total estimated cost of USD $3.5M.

### 16 CONVENTIONAL CREDIT PROJECTS ARE DEFINED AS FINANCIAL RESOURCES AND, AS SUCH, CAN NOT BE INVESTED IN PRIVATE PLOTS OF LAND (Law No. 1739)

The GCF will support vulnerable smallholder farmers to be connected from the irrigation hydrometric network to their farms through a water supply system. The project will also provide technical assistance and training on climate change risks in the food production in areas with high vulnerability to climate change, poverty and food insecurity.

### 17 BOLIVIA'S NDC INCLUDES THE NEED FOR CLIMATE RESILIENCE ACTION IN THE AREA OF WATER RESOURCES

Bolivia's NDC includes the need for climate resilience action in the area of water resources. The country is systematically addressing climate risks. With the government unable to provide the significant financing it requires to address these risks, there are increasing calls from civil society and other stakeholders for greater support.

### 18 FAO PROJECTS ARE FOCUSING ON CLIMATE CHANGE RISKS IN THE VALLES MACROREGION

The project will target vulnerable smallholder farmers living in conditions of poverty and extreme poverty in the Valles Macroregion. These farmers are most vulnerable to climate change as they currently depend on aging and inefficient irrigation systems, as well as increasingly variable rainfall for primary subsistence agriculture.

This project proposes a project that will provide technical assistance and training on climate change risks in the food production in areas with high vulnerability to climate change, poverty and food insecurity. ### Table 12: Economic analysis results

As indicated in the economic and financial analyses, the project is justified by the long-term improvements in sustainable production experienced by farmers. These benefits would be utilizable to households without the significant capacity building and institutional strengthening required for the adoption of climate-smart agriculture practices that are essential for the conservation of ecosystem functions and services, which cannot be captured by private actors.

This project will provide technical assistance to smallholder farmers in Bolivia to adapt their agricultural systems to climate change. Budgetary analyses indicate that national budgetary resources will be able to meet only a fraction of the financing needed to carry out the entire program. The project is therefore designed as an investment in the development of a financial landscape for smallholder farmers, with basic infrastructure (e.g., on-farm climate-proof irrigation) and training in climate-resilient agricultural management to be provided by public institutions. This will enable smallholder farmers to access credit markets more easily than they do now, so that smallholders from accessing credit and markets will be overcome with training, engagement with financing entities, and access to market information. The project will also provide technical assistance for the development of a financial landscape more effectively through use of readily accessible climate and agricultural information. This project will enhance the productive capacity of smallholder farmers in Bolivia by providing them with the necessary infrastructure and training to adapt their agricultural systems to climate change.

A careful analysis of the financing landscape for smallholder agriculture in Bolivia concludes that this is the most appropriate option for the country, given its current financial constraints and the need to provide a strong foundation for the transition to the post-GCF phase. For more information on the financing landscape, see sections 4 and 6 of the Feasibility Study: Annex 2.

The project will be implemented in two phases:

1. The first phase is expected to last from January 2013 until December 2015.
2. The second phase is expected to last from January 2016 until December 2017.

B.6 Exit strategy (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The exit strategy for this project ensures that the barriers to climate resilience overcome sustainability, so that project components are integrated in the local development and agricultural management planning process. Recognizing the importance of long-term sustainability, the project has been designed from the outset to align incentives between key stakeholders, strengthen intersectoral cooperation, and ensure a sustainable transition towards more resilient production systems in support of project objectives. The exit and sustainability strategy includes the following mechanisms:

### Foreword

At the farmer / community level, the project creates a positive feedback loop between adoption of climate-resilient agricultural management and diversified and increased incomes. The project interventions are designed to reduce climate risks and increase resilience at this level by providing technical assistance on climate-resilient agricultural practices that help farmers diversify their incomes beyond crop production. In addition, the project supports long-term structural improvements in farmers' livelihoods through the provision of credit facilities, insurance mechanisms, and other financial instruments that contribute to long-term resilience against climate change.

At a municipal level, the project will support the development of an integrated agricultural extension assistance plan for FONAGROBQUE that will further increase support. FONAGROBQUE is providing to GMSs and GAGs through programs of innovation and technical assistance in order to improve their capacity to provide services to farmers. The project also contributes to Bolivia's Development of new public and private financial mechanisms (facilitate the identification of financial mechanisms for supporting small-scale producers) that will contribute to long-term resilience against climate change.

At a national level, the project will support the development of the institutional framework, as well as raising awareness and strengthening capacities of farmers to understand how to access these mechanisms. These interventions will be further supported by the implementation of an integrated agricultural extension assistance plan for FONAGROBQUE, in order to leverage climate finance towards the restoration of ecosystems and agricultural landscapes at municipal level. For more information, please consult document: Activity 4.2.1 in Annex 2, Feasibility study.

### Technical sustainability

The ecosystem-based climate adaptation proposed by the project combines two major technical approaches (i.e., ecosystem management and agroecology) that are complementary to each other. The first approach is based on the restoration of the core contribution of the project to technical sustainability: Ecosystem function restoration implies a long-term resilience approach, which includes the following components: 1) reforestation and afforestation; 2) soil conservation and water management; 3) biomass production and soil organic matter accumulation. All of them are necessary for a complementary and self-sufficient adaptation strategy that will contribute to long-term sustainability.

At a national level, the project will strengthen the capacities of key organizations at all levels of government, particularly those in charge of land use planning and environmental management. The project will also support the development of programs and projects providing technical and other assistance to vulnerable smallholder farmers (including women and older people), helping them to adopt climate-resilient agricultural practices that are adapted to their specific needs.

The project will provide substantive solutions to problems of production and sustainability that build resilience across the crops, pastures and forested portions of national territory. The project will also contribute to the conservation and restoration of biodiversity in the valleys of the Macusani region, which is a priority area for climate change adaptation efforts and progress towards the achievement of the United Nations' Sustainable Development Goals.

Long-term programs by NGOs such as the MIGRAGS and MRIRGO, will incorporate the lessons learned from this project into their activities. These organizations are already working with smallholder farmers in the Macusani region to train producers on how to take climate-informed decisions, and to design and implement agricultural adaptation solutions. The project contributes by providing technical assistance for the development of a training program that will be delivered by "promotors", who will then support the local agricultural extension services to reach a wider population in more solitudes. These organizations are also working with smallholder farmers on climate-resilient irrigation systems, and will receive training for the management of on-farm climate resilient irrigation systems, and will employ a "learning by doing" approach to test these solutions.

Additionally, an OIM Plan Annex 21 has been designed, which outlines as a requirement that the end user (the farmer) ensures the sustainability of the project components in order to ensure long-term resilience against climate change and sustainability. For this purpose, and to ensure sufficient water flow during project implementation, MRIRGO, the municipalities and the irrigation Committee will sign a commitment that endorses the OIM requirement as well as the ensured water flow

## Institutional sustainability

The project will be a long-term solution to the institution and governance barriers that currently limit climate resilience in the Valle Macizo-region. The project will work directly on the municipality level, mainstreaming climate change in the Integrated Territorial Development Plans (ITDPs) of the municipalities and in the development of their territorial planning instruments.

Likewise, the participation of the Ministry of Planning in the design and implementation of investments in the country will be a factor that contributes to institutional sustainability. The project will also work with the National Institute for Forest Management (INIFOR) to provide information and influencing the formulation of goals and actions, ensuring that climate change adaptation is mainstreamed into the planning processes at all levels.

Additionally, the Economic and Social Development Plan - 2021 - 2025 (PDES) will be soon formalized. This will be the basis for the project to work with the Ministry of Planning (MOP) in the design and implementation of investments that are aligned with the updating and sustainability of the project components.

### Mainstreaming climate change adaptation

The MOP is an excellent opportunity to further mainstream the Project components into these institutions and their lines of work. The project will also seek to strengthen the institutional capacity of the MOP, through the design and implementation of training activities for its personnel on the topics related to the project, as well as examples of pathways of implementation of the Joint Mitigation and Adaptation Mechanism for the Integrated and Sustainable Development of the Valle Macizo Region (JMA-ITSDVM), such as strategic national (e.g. future 2021–2025 PDES) and territorial planning instruments (e.g., PTDS).

### Strengthening local governance

At the same time, the project will seek to strengthen the capacity of the autonomous territorial entities (municipal governments, departmental governments, indigenous and native people's governing bodies), as well as civil society organizations in the Valle Macizo region. This ensures that the actions proposed in the Project are socially visible, building stakeholder ownership from the very beginning of their implementation.

The project will also seek to strengthen the capacity of the municipalities through the design and implementation of training activities for its personnel on the topics related to the project, as well as examples of pathways of implementation of the Joint Mitigation and Adaptation Mechanism for the Integrated and Sustainable Development of the Valle Macizo Region (JMA-ITSDVM), such as strategic national (e.g. future 2021–2025 PDES) and territorial planning instruments (e.g., PTDS).

The project will capitalize on existing governance structures and initiatives at the local and national levels, enhancing governance mechanisms for climate change adaptation in the Valle Macizo region.

### Strengthening municipalities

The project will seek to strengthen the capacity of the municipalities through the design and implementation of training activities for its personnel on the topics related to the project, as well as examples of pathways of implementation of the Joint Mitigation and Adaptation Mechanism for the Integrated and Sustainable Development of the Valle Macizo Region (JMA-ITSDVM), such as strategic national (e.g. future 2021–2025 PDES) and territorial planning instruments (e.g., PTDS).

The municipalities, ensuring their continued use and ability to promote project components over time, will be responsible for the capacity platforms under the RCEM-Valle project.

## Scaling up

### The scaling-up process

The scaling-up of the project's components and lessons learnt will contribute to the replication in other macro-regions of the country. This will be done through the design and implementation of a strategy for the scaling-up of the project in the new PDES, which sets the basis for national economic planning and public investment.

In the project completion process, the lessons learned and best practices obtained during the project implementation phase will be disseminated to other regions and farmers. The project will also seek to strengthen the institutional capacity of the Ministry of Planning (MOP) in coordination with national and sub-national authorities, in order to generate the formulation of policies and the awareness of actors at all levels on climate change adaptation.

The project will also seek to strengthen the institutional capacity of the Ministry of Planning (MOP) in coordination with national and sub-national authorities, in order to generate the formulation of policies and the awareness of actors at all levels on climate change adaptation. The project will also seek to strengthen the institutional capacity of the Ministry of Planning (MOP) in coordination with national and sub-national authorities, in order to generate the formulation of policies and the awareness of actors at all levels on climate change adaptation.

### Financial mechanisms

The other financial mechanisms, such as INIFOR and BSEP, will be scaled to other regions and farmers.

## Leveraging additional funding from FONAGOSQUE

To provide for leveraging additional and new funds from other sources from FONAGOSQUE, the project proposes the following activities:

- Build capacities of FONAGOSQUE to increase its possibilities to access other climate-related funding, helping the Fund to become a more attractive partner for public and private investors.
- Strengthen the institutional capacity of FONAGOSQUE and financial institutions. Regarding the interest of the government to enhance FONAGOSQUE's capabilities, efforts to develop organizational and operational capacities will be part of the activities of the GCF project.

## GCF resources will be invested in building capacity for demand-driven integrated solutions for landscape management and climate-resilient agricultural systems and food security.

The project promotes institutional planning and coordination across government officials and communities to overcome the sectoral and non-sectoral barriers that hinder the adoption of climate-smart agriculture. The project also builds the organizational capacity of farmers to plan and implement resilient local agricultural and water management solutions, adopt technology-based approaches for sustainable land use, and improve access to markets.

At the institutional level, the project strengthens the capacity of key institutions to deliver climate-resilient solutions for farmers. This project building is critical to long-term sustainability, as it empowers Bolivia institutions to continue scaling up climate-smart agriculture.

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

D.1. Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

202. The project builds on best practices and lessons learned from post-adaptation-focused interventions in Bolivia that have had a tangible impact on reducing vulnerability to climate change. These include: i) the promotion of agroforestry systems; ii) investments on restoring ecosystem functions and services at on-farm and micro-watershed level, improving crop productivity, enhancing water availability for irrigation and livestock watering, and increasing resilience against droughts; iii) promoting sustainable forest management practices for depredation and pressure on scarce natural resources; and building capacities of rural communities to better manage their forests. The project will also build on the experience gained from the Joint Mitigation and Adaptation Programme (JMAP), which has been implemented in Bolivia since 2013, and which has had a significant impact on ecological but also social resilience, which is a major underlying goal of the Joint Mitigation and Adaptation Programme. The project will also build on lessons learned from other projects that have had an impact beyond the Valley micro-region, inside and outside the national boundaries.

203. The project will contribute to reducing vulnerability by increasing the adaptive capacity of households in the target communities through a) improved access to climate information (e.g., weather forecasts, early warning systems), b) increased access to training in climate-resilient practices and other project activities. As a result of the project, the direct beneficiaries will have better access to climate information and will be able to adapt their livelihoods by reducing the impact of climate change-related rainfall variability and drought. The distribution of project activities across the four project components (i.e., water security, health and well-being, food security, and ecosystem functions) ensures that all aspects of adaptive capacity are addressed.

204. Indirect beneficiaries: Additionally, 1,267,789 people (35% of the national population, 48% women, and 52% men) will be indirectly affected by the project through increased access to climate information and improved livelihoods. Factors influencing the extent of this dynamic were assessed as the spatial proximity of the target municipalities.

205. The project has been designed to contribute to three of the GCF’s adaptation results areas:

1. Increased resilience of households in vulnerable regions: The project will increase the resilience of 8,531 vulnerable people in the Valley micro-region in Bolivia against drought events and land degradation by promoting agroforestry systems and sustainable forest management practices (e.g., improved soil moisture retention, climate-proofing technologies (e.g., irrigation), solar tents, thermal blankets, and hail nets), timely weather and climate information services, and capacity building for better management of natural resources. The project will also contribute to the emergence among these approaches will foster to constitute better sustained livelihoods and with it, replicability and sustainability of this topic, in an environment where households are already facing water insecurity.

206. GCF’s adaptation results area 2 (A2) - Increased resilience of health and well-being, and food and water security: The project will contribute to food and water security by encouraging resilient agricultural management that reduces climate change risks on the one hand, while increasing productivity and improving livelihoods for vulnerable households on the other. Where households already are facing water insecurity, the project will monitor or increase the number of food secure households in the Valley micro-region in Bolivia. The project will also contribute to the resilience of 8,531 head of households if we will be water secure in the time of drought.

## D2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The proposed project aims at changing the current paradigm of conventional and ecosystem degrading agriculture, to a new paradigm based on transformational production systems that while it adapts to climate change also restores ecosystems and further enhances their resilience. This will be achieved by scaling up innovative financial mechanisms piloted in other contexts for Bolivian farmers (Component 4) and by providing additional technical assistance from the GCF, which adds three critical elements: (1) access to credit and insurance products that lock long-term investments financially viable; (2) a strong enabling environment with supportive policies and institutions; and (3) overcoming financial barriers to climate resilience. The project will create a virtuous circle and lead to a paradigm shift in climate resilient production systems.

### Paradigm shift

The 250-year-old paradigm comes from its focus on land-based farming from traditional agricultural practices characterized by high chemical dependency, vulnerability, and increasing loss of damage, to a system where farmers know how to use more efficient practices, are able to access credit and insurance products that lock long-term investments financially viable, and are able to diversify their livelihoods. This will be achieved through the following components:

1. **Component 1:** Scaling up innovative financial mechanisms for Bolivian farmers (e.g., microfinance, insurance, etc.) to overcome financial barriers to climate resilience.
2. **Component 2:** Providing additional technical assistance from the GCF, which adds three critical elements: access to credit and insurance products that lock long-term investments financially viable; a strong enabling environment with supportive policies and institutions; and overcoming financial barriers to climate resilience.

### The project delivers the following paradigm-shifting pathways

1. **Component 3:** Providing additional technical assistance from the GCF, which adds three critical elements: access to credit and insurance products that lock long-term investments financially viable; a strong enabling environment with supportive policies and institutions; and overcoming financial barriers to climate resilience.
2. **Component 4:** Supporting access to financial mechanisms for farmers to sustain adaptation measures in agriculture.

### Potential for scaling up and replication

The REEM - Valle project has been designed to foster scaling-up and replication of the proposed adaptation measures. First, it builds on lessons learned and best practices from previous ecosystem-based climate adaptation projects in Bolivia, with interventions that reflect the needs, constraints, resources, and capabilities of beneficiary farmers and their communities. Second, it provides a strong enabling environment for scaling up by providing technical assistance and stimulating local economic development with positive feedback loops on poverty reduction (Component 1). Third, it supports policy dialogue to ensure that the project’s lessons learned are integrated into national policies.

# Potential for knowledge sharing and learning

## The project will provide a solid foundation for knowledge generation, sharing and learning due to several factors.

### First, stakeholder engagement and network building as a central feature of the project.
Real-time results from the project will be shared with stakeholders through regular meetings, workshops, seminars, training sessions, and other events. These will include farmers' associations, cooperatives, NGOs, government agencies such as FES and producers' associations.

### Second, the project will identify and participate, as relevant and appropriate, in policy-making processes at national level (e.g., National Climate Change Strategy) and at country level.
Third, the project will identify, analyze, and share lessons learned that might be beneficial in the design and implementation of similar projects. This will involve an active process of integrating successful adaptation actions within existing policies and programs.

## Contribution to post-Covid-19

### The Bolivian legal framework has established the sustainable "management of life systems" as one of the instruments of public policy.
This is based on the National Framework of Mother Earth and Integral Living to Downward. This approach is based on the recognition and respect for the integrity, interdependence, and complementarity between all living beings within a given territory.

### Within the framework of interactions among rights, obligations and duties, the aim is to build a life system based on integral management.
This includes the promotion of sustainable production systems that are integrated into the national legal framework. The Law 308 establishes the Joint Mitigation and Adaptation Action Plan (JMAAP) as an instrument for the implementation of the National Framework of Mother Earth, at both local and international levels.

### The JMAAP is a multi-sectoral agreement.
It is based on the principle that all sectors are responsible for the implementation of actions to reduce emissions and adapt to climate change. This includes agriculture, forestry, energy, transport, industry, etc., as well as public and private entities at national level.

### The project will contribute to the implementation of the Joint Mitigation and Adaptation Action Plan.
The rehabilitation function of production systems is essential for the implementation of other activities, like organic farming, as part of a climate-resilient agriculture in production system.

### D.4. Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Vulnerability of the country and/or specific vulnerable groups, including gender aspects

**227**: Bolivia was ranked as one of the most vulnerable countries, among the top 10 countries globally, on the 2010 Global Long-term Climate Risk Index. The country is located in a region with high vulnerability to climate change impacts.

#### Environmental and social co-benefits (max. 50 words)

**230**: Over the last few years, Bolivia has made great progress in the reduction of poverty, jointly with climate change adaptation and mitigation initiatives, reinforcing the resilience of rural communities and production systems. The country promoted an ecosystem approach to sustainable development, which led to a significant increase in forest cover from 1985 (36%) to 2014 (57%), and a decrease in deforestation rates from 36.7% to 16.9%, on average.

At the same time, the government is setting up national programmes such as "MAGUA" (90% of which are implemented by indigenous communities) and "Bolivian Forests", which aim at promoting sustainable use of natural resources, guided by the Economic and Social Development Plan, for forest joint adaptation and mitigation, water resource management, reforestation, and restoration of degraded lands.

# Alternative sources of financing

## The country is facing a significant financial challenge given Bolivia’s strained economic circumstances and the transformation the fund offers by potentially triggering further investments.

Currently, domestic budgets for building resilience among smallholder farmers are insufficient to meet the needs of the most vulnerable. This results in an increasing number of Bolivians living in extreme poverty. This highly vulnerable population can easily face dire consequences if their livelihood deteriorates.

### New sources of financing

#### National budgetary resources
- The country’s national budget is expected to reach USD 10 billion in 2023, with a GDP growth rate of approximately 4%.
- However, the budget is projected to be insufficient for effective climate change management. Therefore, there is limited financial ability to increase climate proofing investments and any ongoing efforts to reduce vulnerability.

#### International support
- The country has received significant international support over time that ensured the initial climate financing provided by the GEF will be leveraged to maximum advantage.
- National budgetary resources and international support are expected to continue in 2023. However, the country’s limited financial ability to increase climate proofing investments and any ongoing efforts to reduce vulnerability.

### D. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Bolivia’s Third National Communication
- The project is fully aligned with national priorities identified in Bolivia’s Third National Communication.
- The project will contribute to reaching the following targets as defined in the country’s NDC: 20 million hectares with increased agricultural productivity and food security; 5.4 million hectares of forest area under sustainable management; one million hectares under irrigation system production farming; etc.

#### Consultation process
- The NC3 includes a consultative process involving key stakeholders, including the Ministry of Planning (Ministerio de Planificación), the MAFMA, and other key stakeholders.
- This will be an important opportunity for the country to engage with its partners in the implementation of targeted response adaptation interventions.

#### National Development Strategy
- The NC3 notes that agricultural production with the participation of smallholders and communities makes an important contribution to national development. It also highlights the importance of sustainable land management, including Sustainable Management of Forests and Mother Earth.

### 6. The total project cost is USD 82.3 million, comprising USD 49.0 million of co-financing and USD 33.3 million of GCF funding in the form of grants.

#### GCF grant support will provide the minimum concessional funding necessary to make the project viable.
- GCF support will address the lack of resources for climate change adaptation by providing financial, technical, and institutional support to help Bolivia adapt its agricultural systems and technology to address the challenges of climate change. At present, public resources are insufficient to achieve meaningful mitigation or adaptation in the agricultural sector.

#### Efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed project
- Please see Annex 3 – Financial and Analytical Finance for a detailed description of the methodology for evaluating the project’s efficiency and effectiveness.
- The project is evaluated using a combination of the project agronomic performance, financial, cost-benefit analysis, which considers the increase in production in climate resilient agricultural systems, comparing the situation with business-as-usual (BAU) scenario. The project also includes an economic evaluation based on the difference between the full costs of project implementation and the difference that the full costs of project implementation are included, as are social benefits that might not be captured to date.

#### These costs include GCF investment and co-finance with partners and Government during the project period as presented in Annex 3 – Financial and Analytical Finance.
- Project benefits include the cumulative net financial benefits for participating farmers compared to business-as-usual (BAU) scenario. The project is evaluated using a combination of the project agronomic performance, financial, cost-benefit analysis, which considers the increase in production in climate resilient agricultural systems, comparing the situation with business-as-usual (BAU) scenario.
- The sensitivity analysis is performed using alternative discount rates of ranging from 5% to 20%. The base case assumes a 10-year investment horizon and an estimated Economic Internal Rate of Return (EIRR) for the five-year implementation period, and for an estimated 10-year investment lifetime.

#### The discounted net present value (NPV) is calculated over the five- and ten-year implementation periods when only marketable benefits (those that can be captured directly by private actors) are included.
- The project’s discounted net present value is negative over the five- and ten-year implementation periods when only marketable benefits (those that can be captured directly by private actors) are included.

Where applicable, the NPV and IRR are calculated (1) assuming business-as-usual, (2) assuming the project investments are made directly by farmers without external support, (3) assuming a highly concessional loan at 0.8% interest and a 5 year tenor, and (4) assuming a highly concessional loan at 0.8% interest and a 10 year tenor. The NPV and IRR calculations are always provided considering capacity building and support to strengthen the enabling environment. Scenario (2) assumes farmers will make investments directly without external support. This scenario is considered as the worst case, because it assumes that farmers will find the means to implement these measures independently when there is no evidence of this happening in the current situation. Scenario (3) is considered as a best-case scenario for the NPV and IRR calculations, which is what is possible without GCF support. Scenario (4) is included for the sake of completeness, but is considered inappropriate in the current context because it assumes that farmers will be able to make investments with concessional loans at 0.8% interest rates and low administrative costs and the implementation delays that would be incurred. Furthermore, a farm survey may discourage the most vulnerable farmers from taking out such loans.

264. The financial analysis for each output is calculated from the private perspective. The financial analyses use both IRR and NPV as metrics to evaluate the project’s profitability. The NPV and IRR calculations are always provided considering capacity building and support to strengthen the enabling environment. These calculations are always applicable in a rural farming context. For example, farmers may make an initial investment at the beginning of the project period (year 0) and then receive returns over time. Solar tents/ greenhouses

265. The up-front costs of tomato production with artificial greenhouses / solar tents make this investment uneconomic without concessional loans. If a loan is provided at 0.8% interest and a 10 year tenor, the NPV becomes positive after 7 years. After 10 years the remaining returns unattainable compared to BAU. IRR cannot be calculated for the BAU scenario because financial flows are negative throughout the period of analysis. A subsidy at 0.8% spreads out the costs of the intervention but does not address the high up-front investment cost. The concessional loan provided by the GCF grant support covers the up-front costs of the investment, making the climate resilience measure financially viable over all periods. The greater returns as a result of GCF support demonstrate the incremental benefit of project investment.

224. Anti-hail nets

160. Using anti-hail (AH) cannaid grain production results in highly profitable, but with the risk of significant losses due to hail damage. Without project support, the use of anti-hail nets reduces peach production losses due to hail damage, but the high-up-front investment cost makes this intervention financially unviable for most farmers. It is not necessary to pay back the GCF grant support because it further reduces financial attractiveness from the farmer’s perspective. GCF grant support includes the up-front investment cost and results in much higher financial NPV.

Thermal energy

267. The analysis assumes minor frost affecting pea output in years 3 and 5, and early severe frosts every few years for 10 years. In reality the timing of moderate and severe frosts is unpredictable. In the absence of GCF support and in the loan scenario, NPV of the benefit from thermal energy is negative because the up-front investment cost is higher than the returns over time. The analysis shows that farmers can overcome these financial barriers during the project period, resulting in positive NPV after the 5- and 10-year periods.

Horticulture

268. The use of hydroponics and other out-of-season raising measures for produce production provides attractive returns to farmers. However, the intervention raises labor costs due to migration during dry season and simultaneously help to improve profits during these periods. Without this intervention farmers face highly variable revenues and increased risk of losses. However, the analysis shows that up-front costs mean NPV is lower than BAI without GCF support, and as noted earlier, the without-GCF

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 81

## Green Climate Fund

### Counterfactual Scenarios

Counterfactual scenarios explore the need for outreach, technical assistance and capacity building to deliver these interventions. Partial (80%) GCF support improves NPV and makes the intervention more financially attractive to farmers. A greater grant contribution (90%) would make further incentivization in the near term by making the MNP higher than the BAU.

#### Organic agriculture and certification/production processes

260. Organic farming is a promising adaptation strategy for crops and livestock that are threatened by climate change, while certification will result in higher prices per kilogram. The production and certification of organic tomatoes yields positive returns compared to conventional tomatoes (see Table 1). The concessionary loan scenario makes the intervention more financially attractive than BAU over the 5-year period of analysis.

270. Sustainability is a long-term investment for farmers. Returns are negative over the first 3 years of analysis with both scenarios. They remain marginal in the base scenario over a 10-year period but are more strongly positive with GCF grant support, improving year even longer (20-year) periods of analysis. The concessionary loan scenario does not smooth cash flows as much and is less attractive than BAU for farmers.

#### Sustainable agriculture

275. Sustainability is a long-term investment for farmers. Returns are negative over the first 3 years of analysis with both scenarios. They remain marginal in the base scenario over a 10-year period but are more strongly positive with GCF grant support, improving year even longer (20-year) periods of analysis. The concessionary loan scenario does not smooth cash flows as much and is less attractive than BAU for farmers.

#### Concessionary agriculture

271. The implementation of conservation agriculture yields negative returns compared to BAU over 5 years in the without-GCF loan grant scenarios. The relatively high initial investments / opportunity costs are rewarded by greatly increased revenues in year 6 onwards, but this is not enough to make the intervention more financially attractive than BAU for farmers.

272. Excluding GCF grant support, NPV is negative over a 10-year period of analysis with both scenarios. The concessionary loan scenario provides incremental support that makes MNP more attractive than in both the short and longer term.

#### Water harvesting

273. The upfront costs of water harvesting investments, farmers face decreased yields and increased labor costs during drought years. Water harvesting investments are expected to increase yields and reduce labor costs, resulting in higher net financial returns over a 10-year period of analysis with GCF grant support. However, the concessionary loan scenario provides incremental support – NPV is considerably lower than BAU for 5 years and only marginally better over 10 years. Loans are significantly less attractive to farmers without GCF grant support; this reduces these up-front costs and makes this intervention much more attractive to farmers.

#### Resilient on-farm irrigation

274. The upfront costs of resilient on-farm irrigation systems result in an extended payback period that makes these investments unattractive for farmers over the 5-year period of analysis with both scenarios. However, NPV is positive as a result of climate change. NPV without GCF investment is lower than BAU over the 5-year and 10-year periods of analysis, and even less attractive to farmers in the longer term (20 years). The concessionary loan scenario provides incremental support that makes this intervention much more attractive to farmers.

#### Conclusions

276. The results indicate that the project yields significant short-term and long-term financial benefits for farmers because of increased resilience, and positive social environmental externalities that justify public investment.

# E. LOGICAL FRAMEWORK

## E.1 Project/Programme Focus

Please indicate whether this proposal is for a mitigation or adaptation project/programme. For cross-cutting proposals, select both.

- Reduced emissions (mitigation)
- Increased resilience (adaptation)

## E.2 GCF Impact level: Paradigm shift potential (max 600 words; approximately 1–2 pages)

This section of the logical framework is meant to help a project/programme monitor and assess how it contributes to the paradigm shift described in Section D.2 above by applying three assessment dimensions: scale, replicability, and sustainability.

Assessment Dimension | Description | Rating | Potential scenario (Description) | How the project/programme will contribute
---|---|---|---|---
Scale | At present, effective and long-term land use planning is constrained by weak national governance structures, inadequate approaches to natural resources management (e.g., lack of water and forest) and limited incentives tailored to effectively support smallholder farmers in the adoption of | Low | Local and national governance structures are strengthened and able to plan and implement long-term land use plans that will reduce the vulnerability of smallerholder farmers to climate change. This is complemented by tailored climate risk management strategies, which will allow smallholder farmers to effectively adopt and sustain agricultural practices. | The project adopts a multi-level approach to empower both local and national institutions to design and implement interventions across agricultural systems and landscapes. It supports the training of key stakeholders (smallholders, public officers, local CSOs) in their capacity building on climate resilient land use planning and water management for climate change adaptation. These local stakeholders, through their participation in the project’s multi-level risk management system, will improve planning instruments to reduce risks.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 84

## Replicability

### Low

**Smallholder farmers are experiencing numerous challenges and often are locked in a cycle of poverty and climate vulnerability. As such, most vulnerable households have limited access to credit, adopt low-cost or no-cost adaptation strategies, reducing their future resilience potential, trapping them in a cycle of poverty and further increasing their vulnerability.**

Smaller farms and enterprises lack knowledge and experience with modern water-efficient agricultural practices and experienced significant impacts on their productivity due to water for irrigation is predominantly provided by farmers in remote areas where water systems do not reach.

**Lessons learned and best practices from the implementation of the resilient agriculture project will be replicated in Bolivia, and proven irrigation systems are replicated in other regions. The MPO will include the replication of the project as a basis for national economic planning and public investment.**

**Replication will be achieved via:**
- Through the dissemination of climate resilient technologies such as solar tents, drip irrigation, and soil restoration practices, which are based on best practices from Bolivia.
- This project will be able to benefit 100 farmers in the micro region that can be replicated in other agriculture enterprises.

**Through Farmer Field Schools, capacity building to other regions of the country.**

- Increased financial access, especially on credit, will be provided and can be replicated to other agriculture enterprises as well.
- Other financial mechanisms such as insurance and crop loans will be provided to other regions and farmers as well, given that both INSA and BDP offer these services.

Financial mechanisms fall for example include improving capacity of FOMA and BDP and develop a plan to leverage new climate related funds in order to increase the number of beneficiaries

## Sustainability

Smallholder farmers tend to have little or no access to financial services which limits their capacity to invest in climate-resilient technologies and inputs they need to plan for and adapt to climate change, to increase their resilience to climate variability and reduce food insecurity and poverty.

At the farmer / community level, the project aims to provide smallholder farmers with a myriad of risks and challenges that impact agricultural production and livelihoods. These include the associated financial risks such as high input costs, and systemic risks, such as pests, diseases, weeds, and plant diseases. Financial constraints (e.g., lack of banks, microfinance institutions, or credit rating companies) are thus discouraging smallholder farmers from accessing financing to farmers. ## E

### Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### 271. In the case of Associated Entity, FAO specifies that the FAO-GCF project will provide technical oversight and supervise the implementation of this project in accordance with the F&A and the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) signed between FAO and the GCF. As per the GCF Monitoring and Accountability Framework, and in accordance with the AMA, the project will be subject to an independent third-party evaluation at the end of the project period. The evaluation will include a Final Evaluation Report, an independent Final Evaluation report, and an Impact Evaluation report. FAO will also provide semi-annual and annual Financial Reports throughout project implementation.

#### 273. FAO will manage and coordinate reporting to the GCF according to its standards and procedures. The monitoring will take place under the following three levels:

**Project execution level:**

* Through the continuous implementation of a monitoring plan to understand effectiveness, targeting and verifying that the program is making as well as implementing a learning plan so elements emerging from the monitoring systems can feed back into the project implementation and planning Components

* This will be done through regular visits by FAO staff to the field, including the TC, PSC and GCF counterparts. The visits will also include technical assistance to beneficiaries and monitor achievements and impacts. Governmental areas and resulting intervention areas will be also analyzed via remote sensing and production of thematic maps.

* Data collected from the monitoring plan will be compiled in a dedicated Project Implementation Atlas. Data supervision reports and conclusions obtained by the above-mentioned process will be presented annually via a "dedicated Project Implementation Atlas". Data, supervision reports and conclusions obtained by the above-mentioned process will be presented annually to the GCF as well as to other stakeholders.

**Sub-project level:**

* The sub-project level will support the TC and PSC in reviews and analyzing progress reports and to assess performances against bases and targets. FAO will also, secure according to its rules and regulations, financial control and midterm evaluation and final evaluation phases via an independent and external evaluation.

#### 282. In accordance with the AMA between FAO and GCF, the FAO Office of Evaluation will be responsible for the independent interim and final evaluations. The evaluations will be conducted in accordance with the GCF’s Monitoring and Accountability Framework and the GCF’s Independent Evaluation Guidelines. The evaluations will provide detailed findings and strategic recommendations to improve implementation, setting out any necessary corrective measures for the remaining period of the project. The final evaluation will

assesses the relevance of the intervention, its overall performance, as well as sustainability and scalability of results; differential impacts and lessons learned; convergence in climate change delivery with other multilateral entities; gender equity; and inclusiveness in result areas. # F. RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## 1. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

Please describe financial, technical, operational, environmental/political, money laundering/terrorist financing (ML/TF), Medium-term funding gap, and other risks that could prevent the achievement of project objectives from being achieved. Also describe these proposed risk mitigation measures. Insert additional rows if necessary.

For probability: High has significant probability; Medium has moderate probability; Low has negligible probability For Description: Describe the nature of each risk factor and its impact on the project

## Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | Medium |

Description: Limited implementation of commitments by political decision-makers in the design, preparation, presentation and public investment of the project.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The design of the project has been co-created in light collaboration with the national and local authorities and integrates their political priorities. Moreover, the project is designed in such a way that it will directly contribute to national and international climate change commitments. The project also includes a governance strategy which aims at ensuring that the project is implemented in an inclusive manner; consists of holistic awareness and engagement strategy tailored to enhance the project ownership of national and sub-national stakeholders. This will be achieved through the creation of an institutional strategy to facilitate political processes and enable national and local government involvement.

## Selected Risk Factor 2

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | High | Medium |

Description: Change of central and local authorities during implementation of the project.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The probability of this potential risk has given rise to a commitment from the government during the project period. The risk that new officials will not support the work will be mitigated through information sessions to update them on the importance of the project, as well as by ensuring that they are involved in the planning and implementation process. This is also agreed with the local authorities and will be supported through plans and strategies with local communities that benefit from the Project aim to establish a permanent and continuous work agenda.

## Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Low |

Description: Poor adoption and buy-in of agroecological management and technologies by communities and producer associations

### Mitigation Measure(s)

This risk will be mitigated through the use of participatory approaches in planning and implementing activities at the local level. This approach incorporates extensive and inclusive consultation of different stakeholders including indigenous peoples and marginalized groups, as well as women and youth. The project also aims to strengthen the capacity of communities and producer associations through actions that strengthen their participation and rights in natural resources management.
