## Legal | Medium | Medium

### Description
Economic activities such as mining activities, concessions, and claims might affect the effectiveness of the restoration measures.

#### Mitigation Measures:
Although no major mining investments are forecasted in the project area, plans will establish territorial management mechanisms, which are designed to manage competing land uses and claims. These mechanisms provide the means through which the project can address potential conflicts with other activities that may impact the project. The territorial management mechanisms operate at the municipal level, since the Municipality is the main decision maker over land reallocations. These measures aim to reduce the probability of the risk occurring to low.

#### Selected Risk Factor B
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | High | Medium |

#### Description
Extended period of the health pandemic (COVID-19) and related restrictions may affect the timeline for delivery of the project activities and further affect rural livelihoods.

#### Mitigation Measures:
The project will take all necessary measures to promote sanitary and safety measures to protect the health of project staff and beneficiaries. The project will establish healthy safety protocols and an operational strategy to ensure safe operations on the field. To avoid any potential risk, the project will establish a communication plan with the local authorities in case of an emergency situation, in order to avoid delay in high-level decision-making. Additionally, the project aims to establish opportunities for the communities to increase self-sufficiency which contributes to their increased resilience against multi-dimensional shocks such as a health pandemic.

#### Selected Risk Factor B
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Low |

#### Description
Natural disaster events such as prolonged droughts or higher frequency of drought as well as torrential rainfall destroy or delay project implementation.

#### Mitigation Measures:
The project is designed to operate within the context of recurrent drought and torrential rainfall. The occurrence of such climate extremes is expected in the region, which makes for a challenging environment for project delivery. However, the design of the project aims at mitigating such risks in the long term. Enhanced information and early warning systems will strengthen drought management while reinforcing community resilience to these events. This will increase the value of the project. Additional livelihood activities will also reinforce adaptive capacities and resilience of the communities.

# Risks of money laundering and counteracting the financing of terrorism and UNSC sanctions

## Mission (Measure) 13

FAO includes in the project agreement signed between FAO and the Government of Bolivia clauses related to AML/CFT and other sanctions, as follows:

- Article 20: The Project shall comply, and shall require all persons participating in the activities under the Project to comply with all internal anti-money laundering, counter-terrorism financing laws, rules, and regulations.
- Article 21: The Project shall ensure that all persons participating in the activities under the Project shall not be involved in any activity or transaction that may contravene any of the obligations set out in this Agreement. The Project shall ensure that they shall not engage in any prohibited practices; the Government understands and confirms that it shall comply with the anti-money laundering, counter-terrorism financing laws, rules, and regulations.
- Article 23: Consistent with numerous United Nations Security Council resolutions adopted under Chapter VII of the UN Charter, the Government and FAO are committed to ensuring that no funds or other assets are used, directly or indirectly, to finance any acts of terrorism. It is the responsibility of the Government and FAO to seek to ensure that none of their funds are used, directly or indirectly, to finance any acts of terrorism. The Government shall provide all necessary assistance in this regard. Any person who has been subject to a Committee established pursuant to its Resolutions 1267 (1999) and 1583 (2004), or that are the subject of sanctions or other measures imposed by the Security Council, as set out in Resolution 1373 (2001), shall be excluded from all operations under this Project. This exclusion may be concluded with third parties for the implementation of activities under the Project.
- Article 24: In accordance with the above, FAO and the Government will ensure that all persons participating in the activities under the Project are fully compliant with any UN sanctions list that may be of relevance. There are no entities or individuals who are subject to or beneficiaries of United Nations Security Council sanctions regimes which will be involved in such participation, either as cooperators or as beneficiaries.
- Article 25: Within this framework, FAO and its staff will ensure compliance with the above through its offices in Bolivia, its regional office and HQ in order to ensure that the activities are implemented in full compliance with the signed project agreement.

FAO will apply the following principles for all its projects: "No Voice without a Vote" and "Zero Tolerance for Corruption", which were adopted by the United Nations Security Council, which require it to comply with the objectives of the Policy on Probity Practices and the principles of the AML/CFT Policy.

In addition, any person who has been subject to or is the subject of any necessary actions to ensure that the project be implemented in full compliance with any UN sanctions list that may be of relevance. There are no entities or individuals who are the subject to or beneficiaries of United Nations Security Council sanctions regimes will be involved in such participation, either as cooperators or as beneficiaries.

Within the framework of this project, the FAO will facilitate the resolution and/or cancellation of any concerns directly linked to money laundering and counter-terrorism financing. The FAO will ensure that all persons participating in the activities under the Project are fully compliant with the above. Any person who has been subject to a Committee established pursuant to its Resolutions 1267 (1999) and 1583 (2004), or that is the subject of sanctions or other measures imposed by the Security Council, as set out in Resolution 1373 (2001), shall be excluded from all operations under this Project. If a notice of recall has not been received within 7 days, the complaint or concern must be sent to the FAO Chief of Mission in Bolivia and/or the FAO Country Office in Bolivia. The FAO will ensure that any such complaints or concerns are referred to the FAO Officer of the Inspector General, who shall carry out an independent investigation. The procedures for the claims is detailed in the Administrative Circular 2013/04.

FAO is committed to ensuring that its resources are used solely for their intended purposes; all that operations are free from fraud and other corrupt practices, and to being held accountable to donors and beneficiaries for the implementation of its programs. To this end, FAO has adopted a Whistleblower Protection Policy which applies to all persons employed by or otherwise associated with FAO and/or its manifestations. This policy applies, regardless of their location, to all activities and operations of the Organization, whether funded by FAO itself or by other donors. The policy also applies to any person who is involved in the implementation of a project for FAO and/or an implementing partner, including any government agency. This policy applies to all FAO personnel and all contractors, consultants, and other persons associated with FAO projects, regardless of their location, whether they are performing administrative, technical or operational purposes. The FAO Whistleblower Protection Policy follows the guidelines to report allegations of possible wrongdoing in the activities of the project listed in the Administrative Circular 2013/04.

## Selected Risk Factor 13

### Category
- Technical/Operational: Low
- Impact: High

Description:
Risk of maladaptation

(Mitigation Measures)
Maladaptation encompasses a range of potential issues. By design, the project does not:

- Promote short-term adaptations that decrease adaptive capacity and hinder future choices.
- Shift vulnerability from one group to another, or one area to another.
- Encourage adaptation strategies which increase emissions, deteriorate environmental conditions and/or social and economic values.

Maladaptation is caused by inadequate knowledge, a focus on issues in isolation and on short-term gains that do not take into account the long-term consequences. The project will avoid maladaptation through its participatory approach, inclusive and long-term planning and implementation process. As noted in the FP, TIP and ESMP, the project's design reflects:

- The benefits of stakeholder engagement, where beneficiaries expressed their needs and constraints.
- The inclusion of adaptation options that are resilient to climate change and can be implemented in place.

The adaptation options: resilient agriculture including agroecology.

## The inclusion of the most vulnerable and marginalized groups

In addition to what is described in Section D.3 (Sustainable Development) an evaluation was conducted using the Ex-Ante Carbon Balance Tool (Ex-Act) to assess potential impacts of project activities on greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. The Ex-ACT analysis includes emissions and reductions related to key elements of the value chains supported by the project including:

* **On-farm inputs:**
    * Inputs and construction of agricultural structures and energy use
    * Inputs for livestock production, such as feedstocks and biomass harvesting structures
    * Emissions from water collection structures
    * Fertilizer application
    * Refrigerant leakage
    * Forest degradation

The results of a GHG analysis using Ex-ACT indicate net GHG emission reduction over the implementation phase and the full 20-year period of analysis (see the EX-ACT appendix 6 to Annex 2 Feasibility Study for more details).

Therefore, the risks of maladaptation are classified as "Low".

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

285. The Environmental and Social Risk Assessment classified the project risk as moderate, although large-scale, significant, or irreversible environmental impacts may occur if certain conditions are not adhered to. These include: a) Project activities that include community participation, especially of indigenous communities, on a purely voluntary and on-demand basis; b) Community members who are not fully committed and are addressed through the project’s selection criteria and social and environmental plan of action.

286. Main risks identified per component are (see Annex E ESFM for full details):

- **Component 1:** Smallholder farmers in areas that are not yet ready to transition to organic or conservation farming do not have access to training, technical assistance, and inputs.
- **Component 2:** Smallholder water resources secured to reduce the risks from droughts and low rainfall.
- **Component 3:** Reclaimed and restored micro-watersheds and ecosystem functions and services.

The environmental risks are flexible impact for this component, and mainly comprise of monitoring investments and building the beneficiaries’ capacities to implement the project activities properly. There could, however, be environmental impacts as a result of poor project implementation or lack of capacity among the beneficiaries. These risks may also arise because of your project supervision, monitoring, and evaluation.

### D.1 Mitigation measures

- **Project activities can have minor influx of labor and employment income differentials in local communities.** Projects with minor labor inflow of workers may increase the demand for sex work, including the risk for trafficking for women for prostitution or other forms of exploitation.
- **Women may face opposition from their husbands and other male family members for their wish to participate in the project.**

### D.2 Mitigation measures to prevent, control, mitigate and/or correct

- Ensure that good farming and forestry practices are in force.
- Promote agroforestry systems.
- High-value forest species in AFS (Agro Forestry Systems). Keep a good balance of native and exotic species in AFSs, when restocking forests.

293. By design, the project is expected to have for greater environmental benefits than adverse environmental impacts. The potential for significant adverse impacts from the project are likely to be very small and limited. However, it is recognized that such impacts can occur into larger negative if they are not identified early during the planning cycle and their mitigation measures implemented.
293. Considering that the activities to be implemented in each site will be in nature and scale across the implementation arrangement, a detailed environmental and social screening (E&S) assessment for each activity is required to identify expected impacts and mitigation and monitoring measures.

### Step 1: Environmental and Social Screening of Activities

294. In line with the project’s planning cycle, this will occur during the preparation phase in year 1. For each activity, screening titles will be identified along with activities, including capacity building/engagement and stakeholder engagement information specific to each site. Each activity will be screened against the ESMF criteria for environmental and social risks and impacts. The results of these screenings will determine whether an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) is required.

### Step 2: Environmental and Social Risk Screening of Activities

295. FAO’s environmental and social screening will determine if an activity will require an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP). The nature, scale, location and frequency of impacts are main elements in the screening of activities; expert judgment will be a major factor in deciding whether an ESMP is required for an activity or not.

296. For each activity identified as requiring an ESMP, FAO will prepare a draft ESMP. This document will outline the information relating to each activity including:

- Description of the activities to be carried out in all sites;
- Description of the project;
- Description of the site;
- Map of the site;
- Description of the stakeholder engagement process that was carried out at the inception phase and the stakeholder engagement plan to be carried during implementation.

297. The ESMP will include:

### Step 3: Environmental and Social Risk Management (Monitoring and Reporting)

298. Activities identified as requiring an ESMP, including those identified in the screening process will then be required to develop ESMPs that include information on the mitigation actions, the indicators and timeframe where the impact is expected to occur.

299. The nature, magnitude, reversibility, and impact of projects are main elements in the screening of activities; expert judgment will be a major factor in deciding whether an ESMP is required for an activity or not.

305. Mitigation measures: Based on the environmental and social impacts identified from the checklist, the ESIFM will describe mitigation measures for each mitigation measure, together with design, expected descriptions and operating procedures as appropriate.

### Project grievance mechanism

The project will establish a grievance mechanism at field level to receive complaints; this grievance mechanism has been agreed with beneficiaries (including through the FPIC with indigenous populations) to take place in the context of the Consultation Workshops. The process for filing grievances, including the complaint form and information on how to file a complaint, together with all relevant documentation and information on the process to file a complaint will be relayed once the consultation platforms are operational and will be disseminated through the FPIC mechanisms.

307. SEAR and GBV grievances will be managed as incidents with an incident, survivor-centred and gender responsive approach. The project will ensure that all staff involved in managing these incidents receive appropriate training on the process. In case of GBV, the reporting party will be immediately directed to appropriate GEF referral pathways by the GBA team. The project will also provide information on how to access other forms of support such as counseling services and measures, and remunerations projects. The project will also be responsible for safe and ethical documentation and reporting as part of the project’s commitment in managing any grievances received and how they were addressed.

### Multi-stakeholder consultations undertaken

308. Initially, in 2016, the first consultations on the RESEM Value Project were carried out by organizing 75 workshops in five departments (Cochabamba, Chuquisaca, La Paz, Oruro and Potosí) where the project areas are located, in order to identify the expectations of potential beneficiaries and project partners regarding the benefits of the project. The consultations also aimed at identifying the institutional framework for the implementation of the project.

309. A major effort was made to ensure the involvement of the departmental and municipal governments of the project area as well as the participation of representatives from civil society organizations, indigenous communities, social organizations, authorities and technicians from the “Gobernaciones”. These consultations were conducted by a team of 12 facilitators.

310. Subsequently, in 2015, a consultation process was carried out to obtain the United Free and Informed Consent (IFIC) of the indigenous communities that will be directly affected by the project. This process involved representatives from the FAO and FULAC (Fund for the Development of Indigenous Peoples in Latin America and the Caribbean). Participants in the process were 102 women, 54 men. The consultations aimed at identifying the expectations of potential beneficiaries regarding the identification strategies that have been considered in the project formulation.

311. In addition, several group and individual interviews have also been conducted with about 54 representatives of Ministries; Programs, Projects, Decentralized and Decentralized Institutions of Ministries; Departmental Autonomous Governments; Municipalities; Social Organizations; Indigenous Communities; NGOs; International Cooperation Agencies; Universities; and International Cooperation.

312. In the context of the Consultation Workshops of the updated project the RESEM VALLES Project was conducted. 5 workshops were held in different cities of the country: Tarja, Santa Cruz, Cochabamba, Camuyu and Chuquisaca. These meetings aimed at identifying the expectations of potential beneficiaries regarding the identification strategies that have been considered, in their capacity as representatives of the projects beneficiaries.

313. RESEM VALLES Project will ensure continuous stakeholder engagement throughout project implementation in line with the GCF’s Information Disclosure Policy and E&S Policy. The Steering Committee and the Technical Committee as well as all other stakeholders involved in the project will be informed on a regular basis about progress, risks, issues, and continuous and scheduled participation to ensure stakeholder engagement aligned with the GCF Information Disclosure Policy and E&S policies. Similar processes are planned with beneficiaries and local stakeholders who, through the mitigation

## G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 313. In addition to communication, advocacy, governance, inter-institutional relations and knowledge management strategies will support stakeholder involvement during the lifespan of the project and beyond.

#### 314. The gender gap index is a composite measure of progress towards greater equality in Bolivia, emphasizing assessing gender gaps in the agricultural and natural resource sectors. It further identifies gender-related issues related to the use of land, access to credit, and participation in decision-making processes. The gender gap index assessment is based on only available statistical information, observations and stakeholder consultations made during field visits in the project areas.

#### 315. Bolivia has a long history of progress towards greater equality in labour markets. According to the Gender Gap Index, Bolivia stands at 42% (data for 2020). However, some indicators show persistent differences between men and women with regard to access to productive assets, participation in decision-making processes, and natural resources. In rural Bolivia, women have less land ownership, responsibilities for agricultural work, and natural resource management. Both genders share some tasks, but women or men dominate other tasks. This results in unequal gender-based division of labour.

#### 316. The project's objective is to mainstream gender equality and promote a gender transformative path to increase climate resilience. The project will target 30-50% participation by women in all activities and access to assets and financing. To assure better implementation, the project will:

* Adopt a community-based human rights approach, which strongly depends on equal and meaningful participation both by men and women.
* Seek input regarding planning, implementation and priorities from women community leaders and partners who are responsible for implementing the project.
* Plan activities to meet women's time availability; location, resources, and specific priorities.

#### 317. The Gender Action Plan has been developed with the following objectives: (i) To mainstream gender equality in all components of the project; (ii) To ensure that all project activities are implemented in an equitable and gender-sensitive manner. It will further contribute to reducing climate change-induced social, economic, and environmental inequalities.

#### 318. The Gender Action Plan is structured around three main pillars: (i) Strengthening women's participation in the project; (ii) Ensuring that all project activities are implemented in an equitable and gender-sensitive manner; and (iii) Monitoring indicators and achievement against all project activities and outputs that contribute to the achievement of project results, components, and outputs.

#### 319. The detailed Gender Assessment and Action Plan is provided in Annex 8.

## G.3 Financial management and procurement (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 320. Financial control and procurement activities will be carried out by the FAD Bolivia office, which were classified as acceptable to the GCF in the FAD accreditation process. FAD has an Administrative Manual organized across various Clusters/Departments that provides guidance on financial management and procurement procedures. The FAD Intrant provides access to this Manual and to other procedures, information and guidance via the "FAO Handbook".

### 321. FAO's Global Resource Management System (GRMS) is a web-based system used by all FAO field offices, which provides all FAD employees in all locations globally with travel, human resource, procurement and finance functionalities. Using GRMS improves the flow of financial information, supports financial monitoring and reporting, increases transparency and accountability, and ensures that all transactions are recorded in accordance with the rules of the organization and that allows for a separation of income and expenditure by donor and project and support and provides

## G.A. Disclosure of funding proposal

The Green Climate Fund Policy (GFP) provides that the GCF will apply a presumption in favour of disclosure for all information and documents relating to the GCF and its funding activities. Under the GFP, prior approval from the Board is required before any document containing confidential portions may be disclosed by the Board; subject to the redaction of any information that may not be disclosed pursuant to the GFP. Information provided in confidence will only be released if it is determined that there are no confidentiality concerns and the document itself contains specific, segregable portions that can be disclosed without prejudice or harm.

No confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full by the GCF. No information is being provided in confidence.

With confidential information: The accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full by the GCF as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the annexed copy of this document contains a redacted version of the funding proposal and its annexes.

* **full copy for internal use:** of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanatory note regarding the said portions and the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity's policy.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP202

## Proposal name: Upcaling Ecosystem Based Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Rural Communities in the Valles Macro-region of the Plurinational State of Bolivia (RECEM-Valles)

### Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)
### Country/ies: Plurinational State of Bolivia
### Project/programme size: Medium

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

### Strengths

- The project is a scale-up from the Bolivian government’s existing initiative Mi Bingo to support expansion of its reach to the most vulnerable rural communities.
- The project will provide synergies with the long-term governance and development strategy, drawing on lessons learned from the project and expand proven approaches in other areas.
- The project has an emphasis on ecosystem-based adaptation approaches.

### Points of caution

- The project’s strategy relies on the government’s commitment to provide support for replication in other regions and additional financial resources may not be provided during the project implementation. It is uncertain whether these resources will be available, as they are dependent on the project activities and may decrease interest from local financial institutions.
- The project will promote dialogue and consensus-building processes through consultation and inclusive decision-making and design solutions that address the complex trade-offs and risks related to increased competition over land and water use in the proposed participatory approach. Nevertheless, the project needs to ensure conflict sensitivity when implementing its activities to be able to strategically address local issues.

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions set out on the short sheet and Addendum D, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations".


---
**Page 132**
---

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### Project background

The Plurinational State of Bolivia (Bolivia) is a landlocked country located in western-central South America. The Los Valles Macro-region, the project site, is located on the eastern side of the Cordillera. The project intervention area covers multiple ecological zones: cloud forests, a mountainous region with an average altitude of 3,000 metres above sea level; and lowland areas at around 2,800 metres above sea level. While the project primarily targets the rural area in Los Valles, the macro-region is also home to indigenous communities that depend on water resources for their livelihoods.

As temperatures rise, native tree species are predicted to shift their geographical distribution towards higher altitudes and steeper slopes. This will alter the composition of the mountain forests and as a result the water regulation capacity provided by these forests may be reduced. The project area is located in the upper basin of the water supply comes from precipitation.

The climate vulnerability analysis conducted by the AE shows how temperature increases are expected to lead to more intense rainfall events, as well as an increased precipitation variability. For example, climate projections show an increase in precipitation in some regions, such as Cochabamba and Chuquisaca, while other areas may experience a decrease in precipitation. These changes in temperature and precipitation may lead to delays in the onset of dry and wet seasons; reduced snow cover on mountain peaks; and increased evaporation rates. This has led to evaporatranspiration has reduced in Los Valles, with implications for a sustained supply of water, affecting rainfed as well as irrigated agriculture.

In addition, the region has experienced an increased frequency and intensity of frosts and hail events in the winter seasons. Based on the climate vulnerability assessment, the municipalities most vulnerable to these changes are those located at lower altitudes; frosts are a recurrent phenomenon and can create losses of up to 50 per cent of the crop production. In recent years, there has been an increase in the frequency of hail events in the project site area under high and very high risk of hail, including some inter-Ardenne valleys and headwaters. Rainstorms have become more frequent and unpredictable in the last 10 years.

The vulnerability assessment also shows that livestock production systems are highly climate-sensitive, with impacts being high, indicating that the crop and livestock sectors will be among the most affected. Floods and droughts are expected to increase in frequency and intensity; smallholder farmers are particularly vulnerable as their current crop yields are already very low. Poor land management and unsustainable land-use change exacerbate these impacts.

The Secretariat has used the Water Evaluation and Planning (WEAP) model to demonstrate the alteration of climate change to the observed impacts. The most considerable changes in water supply have been modeled considering changes in land use and land cover (2010–2020) for the climate of 1980-2015, which includes deforestation and burning coverages, amongst others. Changes in precipitation are also considered as well as other factors such as population growth, and other factors. The greatest disaggregation possible was used with the available data (4-km resolution). It is important to note that the changes in discharge modeled in the climate change scenarios can be mostly attributed to climate change.

The AE has identified the technical barriers for the smallholder farmers as follows:


---
**Page 133**
---

### Limited technical support to apply the appropriate technological know-how and management of traditional agroecosystem management to increased climate variability.

- Lack of appropriate and efficient on-farm water management practices and infrastructure to adapt to climate change.
- Additionally, financial barriers exist for smallholder farmers, including the following:
  - Limited access to financing, including credit and insurance, to build resilience;
  - Lack of support for marketing and selling opportunities for their agricultural produce;
  - Institutional barriers identified by the GCF as follows: (i) Inter-institutional coordination at regional level, without addressing the needs of smallholder farmers and producer associations; (ii) Local institutions lacking strong governance mechanisms for climate change and natural resource management; and (iii) Lack of integral and participatory micro-watershed management plans to guide climate resilient watershed restoration and conservation practices.

### The project aims to increase the resilience of smallholder farmers in Los Valles to climate change by implementing an integral ecosystem-based adaptation approach, which includes:

- Strengthening the capacity of local institutions and producer associations to manage their agroecosystems sustainably: (i) on-farm, climate-proofed irrigation systems; (ii) participatory and integral watershed management to restore ecosystem functions and services; (iii) strengthening the adaptive capacity of smallholder farmers through training and extension services; and (iv) strengthening the corresponding governance and institutional capacities at local level to support the implementation of these interventions.

### In terms of project design, the project is structured around four inter-related outcomes:

- Climate resilient agriculture for productivity increased.
- Climate proofed water management systems including supporting irrigation systems for increased agricultural productivity;
- Restoration and conservation of forest ecosystem covering 17,500 hectares; and
- Stronger institutional and territorial governance for climate risk management and disaster preparedness.

### A locally-informed vulnerability analysis was conducted as part of project formulation, and a suite of interventions have been proposed based on the climate hazards and impacts identified. The project will be implemented in four villages where the majority of smallholder farmers in Los Valles who are the final beneficiaries live. In addition to the concrete interventions described above, the project will also aim at increasing access to and engaging public institutions to diversify the supply of financial instruments. The socioeconomic and environmental systems analysis of the farmers' inform us detail on the farmers and production systems typologies.

### With regard to creating a dependency model, the critical role of social protection has been well identified in the context of climate change adaptation. The project will focus on:

- (i) equipping households with resources needed to overcome liquidity constraints and cope with market shocks; (ii) providing insurance against weather-related stressors, thus allowing them to reinvest in their livelihood activities and enable better access to risk management where insurance and financial markets are not sufficiently available [Component 1]; (iii) stimulating local economic development with positive feedback loops on poverty


---
**Page 134**
---

reduction (Component 2): (ii) supporting sustainable management of natural resources to strengthen resilient livelihoods (Component 3).

### This project proposal is based on a national flagship programme called Mi Riego, implemented since 2015 with public funding support, through the Ministerio de Medio Ambiente y Recursos Naturales (MMAyR) and the Federación de Municipios del Ecuador. Financing from organizations such as the Inter-American Development Bank (IADB) and the Development Bank of Latin America (BID) has also been received. The lessons learned from these investments are taken into account in the design of the RECCY-Valles program. The lessons learned have shown the importance of making investments in irrigation systems on the farm irrigation systems and following an integrated, ecosystem-based approach at basin level.

#### Lessons learned include:

(a) Not considering a watershed approach in new irrigation investments can reduce water availability.
(b) Investments in irrigation did not reach on-farm irrigation systems;
(c) The investments in irrigation must be in places where there is a willingness to organize around the irrigation system and these types of investments should consider those with easier mechanisms to maintain the irrigations systems.

#### In response, the project requests for GCF funding of USD 23.2 million in the form of grants with a co-financing of USD 30 million from the Government of Bolivia through MMAyR and the Federation of Municipalities.


### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

**Component 1: Strengthened food and income security in changing climate through climate resilient agricultural systems (total cost: USD 15.8 million; GCF cost: USD 15.8 million)**

The component aims to develop and strengthen the capacity of smallholder farmers, including women and youth, to adapt their livelihoods and agriculture as a means of adaptation to the variability of temperatures, rains and droughts. The component targets an increase in food security and income for at least 20% of the population served by the end of the project. Component 1 is structured in two main output or project results:

(a) Output 1.1. Climate resilient agriculture implemented and managed by smallholders to increase their access to climate-resilient agricultural products.
(b) Output 1.2. Increased market access to climate resilient agricultural products.

The results of studies on irrigation projects in the 1990s (GCRB, 1991), reported that the provision of Technical Assistance (TA) for the design and implementation of irrigation systems was often partial or simply not enough; because the projects had no overall technical approach to ensure the delivery of equipment and services. The studies also highlighted that the lack of follow-up and maintenance of the infrastructure, as well as the lack of institutional capacity in the municipalities to monitor the operation and maintenance in the medium term (5 years). A PROGRESI sustainability and self-management model was developed for irrigation schemes that after 3 years, only 20% of the irrigation systems presented problems for continuity and sustainability.


---
**Page 135**
---

21. The second component focuses on revitalizing and optimizing the existing irrigation systems for efficient water use, reduced risk of droughts due to the variability of temperatures, prolonged dry seasons, and increased frequency of extreme weather events, as well as the restoration or re-on-farm irrigation systems in at least 4,448 hectares of farmland by the end of the project.

22. Restored and conserved micro-watersheds and ecosystem functions and services (total cost: USD 11.3 million; GCF cost: USD 6.7 million)

23. The third component aims to ensure long-term improvement in the communities' water security to guarantee the sustainability of their climate resilient livelihoods, based on prioritizing micro-basins and the preservation and restoration of environmental functions using the participatory approach described above. By the end of the project, the component target is at setting or restoring the environmental functions of at least 17,520 hectares of farmland.

24. Enabling conditions created to implement and scale up climate resilient agroecological management (climate-informed integrated micro-watershed management, and access facilitation for smallholder farmers) (total cost: USD 3 million; GCF cost: USD 1.8 million)

25. The fourth component aims to strengthen public and community institutional capacities to manage climate risk by small farmers and communities. The component will facilitate one government institution per country to provide technical assistance, training, and local financial mobilization.

26. Interventions and concrete measures proposed under Component 1 are linked to specific hazards that overlap with the climate hazards addressed through interventions in Component 3.

27. These interventions are not newly introduced in Bolivia but are proven technologies that are ready for upscaling. They have been extensively studied and validated by the Bolivian Research Institute (IBR).

28. The proposed financial mechanisms will (i) ensure long-term financial sustainability via channeling funds to support the implementation of climate resilient agricultural practices, building resilient infrastructure and re-investments in climate resilient agricultural practices; and (ii) foster upscaling of the climate resilient project interventions.

29. Without a clear pathway for scaling up these interventions at household level, which in turn can increase climate resilience, it would be difficult to leverage funds for continuous support of climate resilient agriculture, and therefore to ensure food and water security.

best restoration should always be to restore natural ecosystems and to ensure a comprehensive understanding of the relationship between these ecosystems for water supply used on restored functions. This relates to socioecological behaviours, where practices promoted by the project can have an impact.

The project is based on a very good understanding of the sociocoeconomic interlinkages and constraints. This allows the possibility to explore the complex links between people and resources in the context of climate change and its impacts. The project will also consider the use of resource use, including outcomes related to climate resilience increase. Not only is it important to have a good understanding of the direct effects of climate change on water supply used for climate change stresses, but this also needs to be complemented with the social aspects that tries to understand the processes, governance structures and institutional arrangements used to manage resources in the context of climate change.

The project will consider how these processes are affected by these interactions and outcomes, as well as the possible effects of external or exogenous influences on them. The project will also consider how these processes can be adapted to new conditions.

Insurance: One of the key challenges in this region is that agricultural insurance is either not adopted due to low awareness or is not available in the areas of the project. Therefore, the project will support the development and implementation of a micro-insurance scheme based but also on micro-insurance.

The project was looking at covering partial premium subsidies for eligible beneficiaries, and will also therefore review specific activities, cost co-and financing options together with FAO. The long-term view is that the subsidy will be reduced gradually to enable farmers to access insurance products independently. This will also include a gradual increase in coverage for additional crops, including some forestry species.

Insurance: One of the key challenges in this region is that many farmers do not protect their investments in productive activities through either conventional indemnity-based agricultural insurance or innovative index-based insurance products due to the lack of an adequate micro-insurance scheme. The project will support the development and tailoring of an index-based insurance product for the target area.

A particular focus would be appropriate coverage for the production of fresh produce together with farmers' other needs, establishing triggers for the insurance pay-out and windows of protection. It will raise awareness and improve vulnerable farmers' access to insurance products.

For these farmers, the insurance premiums would be initially subsidized by the project. The subsidy will be reduced gradually to enable farmers to transition in time to insurance themselves. This will also include a gradual increase in coverage for additional crops, including some forestry species.

The project will work with the National Institute of Agricultural Insurance (INSA), in order to coordinate their approaches within the framework of the insurances that the institution handles in Bolivia. Currently there are two public-liability insurance mechanisms: (i) the Pachamanca and (ii) the Rural Credit Program, which is managed by the Bolivian Agrarian Insurance Program, which focuses more on municipalities.

Limited experience exists with index-based agricultural insurance benefits because coverage is limited to specific crops. The idea of this activity is precisely to explore the economic and financial feasibility of expanding coverage.

The project will also strengthen the National Service for Meteorology & Hydrology of Bolivia (SENAMHI) and Ministry of Rural Development and Land will be strengthened to ensure the generation and timely dissemination of locally adapted information on meteorological/weather and hydrological issues, as well as on hotspots and forest fires.

## Assessment of performance against investment criteria

### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium to high

- The project will directly benefit 81,551 heads of households, which accounts for 0.7 per cent of the total population of the country and 2.5 per cent of the population in Villes maire region.
- A total of 17,510 hectares of natural resource areas will be managed under improved low emission agricultural practices, to promote practices that will enable to establish 1,000 community reservoirs and 5,000 new farm reservoirs to support water security and reduce risks from droughts and low rainfall.

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium to high

- The project takes in lessons learned from the previous/existing initiatives in the country that are ready for scale-up.
- The project design takes into account both the lessons and the gaps identified, ensuring a more sustainable approach by people and communities.
- The project also has a strong knowledge management component to ensure knowledge-sharing.


---
**Page 138**
---

information and long-term monitoring tool that will provide up-to-date information on various indicators, such as water resources and climatic and weather conditions. This tool will be an annex to the existing MMYa portal and will be incorporated in the National Early Warning System.

* The project will address financial barriers for the smallholder farmers who tend to have limited access to credit and financial services, including non-conventional insurance products, and consequently are not able to manage risks effectively. It proposes the establishment of replicable financial mechanisms, including insurance; recommendations refer to the development of indices that can be used in combination with weather stations to monitor temperature and air temperature, which will make it possible to foresee the potential occurrence of catastrophes and eliminate the need for field verification with adjusters, in addition to paying indemnity directly from the insurance company.

* The project proposes different financing and risk-sharing alternatives, basically relying on existing public structures.


### Strengthening the National Agrarian Insurance Institute

The project is expected to provide technical assistance to the National Agrarian Insurance Institute (INSA) for developing applications of premium-sharing arrangements. INSA currently manages two insurance schemes (for smallholders and municipalities) and this proposed project will support the development of pilot projects in collaboration with existing public structures to the extent possible. To develop these pilots, INSA would require strengthening its capacity; therefore the project will support the partnership through the National Climate Service.

Regarding micro-insurance schemes, local producers currently face challenges with national insurance companies for developing applications that cover specific crops and specific agricultural crops. The risk-indexed micro-insurance being proposed would benefit the most vulnerable smallholder farmers in the region (Outcome 1). This project proposes to encourage small producers to make investments to support climate resilient agricultural activities that are the primary source of family income in the region (Outcome 1).


### Green and concessional credit lines

The project will work with local and national financial institutions, such as the Provincial Development Bank of Santa Cruz, to develop the micro-credit portfolio for climate-resilient agriculture and irrigation. In the case of FINMURAL (Association of Development Finance Institutions), the project proposes to support the design and implementation of a credit line for the Private Sector). The project will support the design and implementation of credit lines with additional technical assistance.

Green credits and concessional credit lines can be used to support the municipalities in implementing climate-resilient irrigation systems, and to support the management and administration of water funds at the community level, as well as to climate-proof the irrigation systems under Outcome 2.


### Design and establishment of water funds

Two revolving water funds are under design in the project area: Tarija and Santa Cruz. These will be used for financing small-scale projects that promote environmental activities upstream. While the funds are expected to be structured as Payment for Ecosystem Services Contracts, BIDF is considering accreditation as a GEF Direct Access Entity for Bolivia and could potentially be a partner in support of the capacity strengthening efforts.


---

scheme, it is worth noting that Bolivia rejects any kind of commercial transaction for ecosystem services, therefore, the participation of private companies in water regulation services payment schemes is limited to the public water companies of the municipalities and the national hydroelectric company.

The GCF funding will support the Fondo Nacional de Desarrollo Forestal (Fonafifo) to elaborate a financial assistance plan with participation of the private sector and the banking sector to finance projects that are aligned with the National Forest Strategy, which comprises a public fund, to manage new and additional climate-related funding (Outcome 3).

### Sustainable development potential
**Scale: High**

- The project expects 40.19 million tonnes of avoided deforestation and to reduce 78.000 tCO2eq over 20 years from improved crop and forest management. The AE has evaluated the impacts on greenhouse gas emissions (GHG) from project interventions using Ex-Ante Carbon Balance Tool (Ex-ACBT).
- The project recognizes the important roles that women play in agriculture production systems in Bolivia, representing 42 per cent of the economically active population as well as managing 40 per cent of agricultural production systems. The proposal aims to remove barriers that women face and to promote their participation in decision-making processes, empowering and support women's growing role in production.

### Needs of the recipients
**Scale: High**

- A multicriteria analysis was used to identify and prioritize project beneficiaries and the interventions will be implemented according to climate vulnerability assessment (poverty, food insecurity).
- The area most vulnerable to hydrological drought was identified, including 2.3 million people, out of which 10 per cent are in extreme poverty.
- Communities and small-scale farmers will apply climate resilient agricultural practices, based on the revitalization and expanded coverage of efficient irrigation systems, which will be complemented by the implementation of agroforestry activities with the objective of optimizing water recharge and supply cycles, reducing erosion and risk of soil degradation.

### Local financial intermediation in Bolivia
- Local financial intermediation in Bolivia does not always consider credit lines with unconventional guarantees or specific credit conditions linked to climate risks. Financial services are usually provided by commercial banks, which do not have the capacity for the deployment of microfinance products for adaptation are: (i) lack of knowledge and microfinance institutions' experience; (ii) lack of financial instruments and mechanisms in terms of maturity and interest rates both for the clients and the microfinance institution; (iii) microfinance institution risk perception of adaptation finance; and (iv) regulatory impacts of the new climate change law.

- Some municipal governments have long experience of self-government but local administration is generally weak. Municipal governments have four main types of resources:

70 To justify the need for concessionality, the AE performed a financial analysis including both a scenario using grants as financing sources and an alternative using loans to assess the relative attractiveness of both options.
71 The results of this analysis depend on the GCF loan being negative (here the assumption is made that concessional loans will reach the end beneficiary with an interest rate of 5 per cent and 5 years at a grace period of five years). For the agricultural sector, the AE concludes that there are no sustainable agroforestry, and thermal blankets. For other components, the activities are probably not financially viable without grants. This analysis provides additional evidence that suggests significant barriers to access finance in the Valle region.
72 The need for concessionality in the case of this project is not dictated by a lack of profitability but rather by the fact that private companies have been unable to overcome communities' lack of access to finance for the interventions. According to the AE, the targeted region of Bolivia has a very low level of financial inclusion (only 10 per cent of the population are clients of an APFU). However, 7 per cent of all total commercial credit granted to the agricultural and livestock sector in 2017. Private companies account for only 4 per cent of the APFU in the Valle region where this project is implemented. The AE concludes that the majority of the credits provided to APFU requires collateral. Out of all the loans requested for APFU purposes in 2017, 29 per cent were not granted, and specifically, 23.5 per cent were not granted because of lack of collateral.
73 Grants seem to be the financial tool that allows maximum flexibility and safeguards the project's viability by reducing the need for concessionality.
74 Sustainability in the long run and potential to catalyse private investment is high for most of the components included in this project. The only exception is the agricultural component, which includes components that will address risk perception from private investors. Indeed, the AE mentions that the government's decision to cap microcredit interest rates at 13.5 per cent contributes to reducing the attractiveness of this financial instrument and thus reduces its sustainability.
75 In addition, the adoption of agricultural insurance is only 3 per cent nationwide and less than 1 per cent in some regions. The AE concludes that the use of microfinance instruments, innovative financial instruments and scaling up the use of agricultural insurance are expected to contribute to stabilizing the project's long-term viability. The AE also mentions that the engagement of financial intermediaries is expected to strengthen the mainstreaming of climate in the agri-food systems and adoption of climate resilient interventions in Bolivia.

76 Overview: The project is a climate adaptation investment aiming to enhance the resilience of livelihoods, ecosystems, infrastructure and food security in the Valle Macro Region of Bolivia, to face the increasing hazards of climate change. The project is also expected to create opportunities for income generation through diversification of livelihoods.

migration more effectively and reducing the risks associated with drought. In terms of pollution, it is expected that the project will reduce the amount of chemical fertilizers used during construction or during operation of project-built facilities. Small-scale work at communal irrigation schemes will be undertaken by local workers who are expected to come from within the host communities.
The ESMF sets out the procedures and requirements for assessing and managing environmental
and social impacts of the proposed interventions and community site-specific activities.

### GH (Community Health, Safety and Security)
Risks and impacts on community health and safety are expected to be negligible during construction. However, interventions in the farming systems and micro-watersheds may have impacts that may increase the risk to communities of vector-borne diseases. The project will not support any activities that promote flood regimes in the irrigation system, and micro-watersheds may disrupt the ecological balance of the area. The change could promote increased prevalence of certain disease vectors increasing the risk for malaria, dengue fever, and other vector-borne diseases. Areas with high rainfall are susceptible to forest fires during drought seasons. Invasive species and pests may also emerge when conditions become favorable. The project will not support any activities that are expected to lead to low soil fertility in the new farming systems could expose farmers and members of their communities to increased risk of food insecurity.

The possibility of use of pesticides in the new farming systems could expose farmers and members of their communities to increased risk of pesticide poisoning. If exposure is expected to be low as the project will not support activities that violate the Pesticide Code of Conduct, and procurement of pesticides will only be allowed for those products which are locally validated or internationally regulated, will not be supported. Influx of non-resident workers into the communities could also increase the risk of vector-borne diseases.

### ESSS (Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement)
The project is not expected to result in any physical or economic displacement. The AE's Screening Checklist also assessed that there would be no significant adverse effects on the rights, livelihoods, or resources of individuals, communities or others to land, fishery and forest resources. The ESMP also has an Exclusion List which identifies activities that will not be supported by the project. As per the list, the project will not fund any activities that result in "changes in land tenure or displacement of people from their homes, lands, farms, other places or work of subsistence, or restrict their access to them".

### ESSS (Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources)
The project is expected to have positive impacts on biodiversity. Changes in farming systems could inadvertently create ecological imbalance, including increased species emergence of new pests and diseases. The new production system may also encourage increased use of pesticides and herbicides which could lead to the loss of non-viable activities located in protected areas and their buffer zones and will adhere to Bolivian laws on prevention of deforestation and degradation of natural resources. Activities that cause degradation (biological or physical) of the soil and water, use of genetically modified organisms, introduction of new species of animals and plants, commercial crop introduction of crops and varieties that previously did not grow in the implementation areas, including those that are invasive, will be prohibited. The project will support the functioning of ecosystems, and introduction of new invasive alien species; activities that affect gene flows and biological corridors; and collection of wild genetic resources.

### GH (Community Health, Safety and Security)
The area of project focus includes indigenous peoples in the Vallejos Marco region of Bolivia. While the AE has noted there is a lack of information on the health status of these communities, the ESMP was prepared as part of the annex to the ESMP. A free, prior, and informed consent process was conducted as part of the development of the IPP. The IPP identifies that consultation, consent participation and engagement with indigenous peoples will be carried out through the following organizations: the Qhara, Chowis, Chichas, Karangas and Charkas in the municipalities of

### Project Description

The project will be directed at the highest level by a Project Coordinator (PC), who is responsible for overall coordination of the project activities and will work closely with the GCF Secretariat to coordinate activities directly with local stakeholders. The PC will also liaise with other government agencies and supported by a Technical Committee. There will be a Project Management Unit (PMU) which will be responsible for overall coordination of the project activities, including implementation of the Safeguards Policy. The PMU will have technical officers (TOOs) who will coordinate activities directly with local stakeholders. The TOOs will have safeguards' specialists as well.

### Implementation Arrangements

The project will be directed at the highest level by a Project Coordinator (PC), who is responsible for overall coordination of the project activities and will work closely with the GCF Secretariat to coordinate activities directly with local stakeholders. The PC will also liaise with other government agencies and supported by a Technical Committee. There will be a Project Management Unit (PMU) which will be responsible for overall coordination of the project activities, including implementation of the Safeguards Policy. The PMU will have technical officers (TOOs) who will coordinate activities directly with local stakeholders. The TOOs will have safeguards' specialists as well.

### Cultural Heritage

The project is not expected to affect any cultural heritage sites or projects. Activities in areas with cultural, historical, or transcendental values for individuals and communities are subject to the GCF Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) and the ESS Precautionary Approach. A Detailed Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (DEISA) and a Cultural Heritage Impact Assessment (CHIA) have been prepared as part of the Project Preparation and Appraisal Document (PPAD). The PPAD has also been submitted to the GCF Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group for review.

### Sexual Exploitation, Sexual Abuse, and Sexual Harassment

Sexual exploitation, sexual abuse, and sexual harassment (SEAH) safeguarding. Awareness raising and prevention of gender-based violence (GBV) and SEAH is incorporated in the overall project design and implementation. The ESMP will include a specific module on GBV and SEAH through triggering the EAS & ES guidelines on gender equality and prevention of GBV. The ESMP identifies key stakeholders, including women's groups, community leaders, religious leaders, and employees of contractors and subcontractors who may request sexual favours as a pre-requisite for the technologies provided by the project. Workers and other project stakeholders could be engaged in activities that put them at risk of exposure to GBV or SEAH, particularly women and other vulnerable groups, given the possible influx of workers from outside the community. SEAH risks are also identified through the ESMP and will be addressed as part of the overall safeguarding strategy.

### Community Engagement

The project will ensure that all communities affected by the project have an opportunity to participate in the project, or from refusal to give up earnings to their husbands and/or other family members. The project will provide training for women and young mothers to participate in the project may also be subject to GBV and SEAH incidents. The ESMP also identifies key stakeholders who can support the project in addressing these risks.

### Project Inception

The project inception, sensitizing project workers and persons working or living in the immediate project area and other vulnerable groups on the risks of GBV and SEAH is included as part of the project activities. The ESMP will identify specific training needs for project staff, including gender-sensitive projects and clinics within the project area; contractually requiring mandatory GBV training of the project workforce and compliance with relevant codes of conduct; and a communications strategy to raise awareness on GBV and SEAH.

### Specific Activities

As specific sub-activities are identified during project implementation, the ESMP will also identify specific training needs for project staff, including gender-sensitive projects and clinics within the project area. The ESMP will be screened and mitigated as part of the ESMP at sub-activity level. As part of the project-level grievance mechanism, the ESMP will include a specific module on GBV and SEAH in an outcome-centred and gender-responsive approach, including confidential reporting and mandatory investigation of all complaints received.

### Reporting

In case of any incident related to GBV or SEAH, the reporting party will be immediately directed to the appropriate GBV referral pathway by the GBR personnel, and directed as necessary to medical care, psychosocial support, legal support, community-based support services, and other relevant services.

### 4.2 Gender policy

The AF has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan (GAP) and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Updated Gender Policy.

#### Background

Indonesia has enacted laws that promote gender equality, such as Law 24/1 against political harassment and Law 39/2007 on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women. The institutional framework for promoting gender equality includes a National Action Plan (NAP) to eliminate violence and dismantling patriarchy which was established by decree to monitor, follow up and evaluate the implementation of the NAP. The NAP is also aligned with the UN Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW). In addition, an Equal Rights Vice Ministry also exists for equal opportunities for women and men, age groups and disabilities. Challenges exist as lack of means and institutional capacity for policy implementation, especially in remote areas and among indigenous peoples and women living in rural areas.

#### Gender assessment

The gender assessment is based on a review of available information sources providing a general context of gender issues in Bali. Consultations with stakeholders that were undertaken in 2018 also provide a perspective on the gender issues of local communities in project areas. The results of these consultations have been used to update and refine the assessment in order to update information from stakeholders who include local communities and producer associations. The following sections present an overview of key findings from the assessment based on inputs received during stakeholder consultations.

#### Social norms

Social norms perpetuate the allocation of tasks according to gender such as care and domestic work, which are predominantly performed by women. Women in Bali are also household heads due to male migration from rural areas. This also puts an increasing number of women in charge of managing their households and providing for their families while still working outside their workplace. Time constraints are a significant barrier to promoting female participation in training activities as women have less time available than men, especially during the dry season which is particularly detrimental to over 30 per cent of rural women heading households as they depend more on natural resources and agriculture. Women are the main food producers. In addition,

### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Project risk assessment (medium risk)

GCIF is requested to provide USD 5 million grant to invest in ecosystem services (such as water and/or ecological resilience (such as food security) in the Valles magicus region of Bolivia. The Ministry of Environment and Water (MMAyH) and Federation of Municipalities (FAMU), are providing an in-kind contribution equivalent to USD 270 million and USD 1 billion respectively.

The Secretariat has three key recommendations for the AE: (i) improving its targeting of female-headed households given their high numbers, especially in rural areas; by including activities that specifically target women and girls as well as men and boys; (ii) reporting the results on training of the use of water resources; the AE should monitor results by including qualitative indicators to assess whether women are more likely than men to participate in training sessions and men; and (iii) given that the assessment identified access to finance as a barrier for women, the AE should specifically earmark a budget for women as beneficiaries under activity 4.2.1 and include related indicators and targets in the GAF.

### 4.3.2. Executing entity/Executing entity’s capability to execute the current project (execution risk)

100. FAO will be acting as both AE and co EE for the project. All GCF proceeds will be executed by FAO. FAO Bolivia has experience implementing mostly medium- size projects not only regional or national in scope. The average size of FAO Bolivia projects over the past five years, the average size of FAO Bolivia projects has been approximately USD 6 million annually. These projects were implemented under direct execution modality with different governmental counterparts.

101. MMDAs and FAO will be co-EEs for the project. They will be responsible for the implementation of funded activities by their co-financing resources only.

### 4.3.3. Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

102. Misalignment of interest with financial institutions, activities under Component 4 aim to improve access to credit and finance for smallholder agricultural production and irrigation systems to mobilize increased finance for farmers by working with financial institutions. The AE will need to ensure that the financial institutions are willing to offer their interest is not aligned with GCF and they have a lack of incentives to roll-out such mechanisms. The active engagement of the financial institution in the project will be critical, and those willing to engage in the project and the enabling policy and legal framework will be present.

103. Willingness of the government: activities under Component 3 will require an endorsement from the Ministry of Finance. Any delay or lack of information or communication or government priorities may delay the process or the plans might not be endorsed. The AE also identifies that there is a risk that the project will not be fully implemented as it has been derived from the government's commitment to the nationally determined contributions and other frameworks, and close collaboration between the AE and the government.

104. Financial sustainability: the project is expected to generate income such as collection of fees from the collection and marketing centres and voluntary contributions from farmers. The financial analysis shows that the project does not generate sufficient financial returns during the five-year implementation period. Therefore, the AE requests full grant support. On the other hand, there are no results in positive ROR considering marketable and non- marketable benefits.

### 4.3.4. GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

105. In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of geographical or thematic focus is not material.

### 4.3.5. Compliance risk (medium risk)

The AE, through its country office in Bolivia, will ensure compliance with the relevant national implementing entities - the Ministry of Environment and Water and the Federation of Associations of Municipalities of Bolivia (FAMABOL) - as well as with the rules for the use of GCF designated funds. The AE has assessed money laundering, terrorist financing, prohibited practices and sanctions risks in relation to project activities and its counterparties. In addition, the AE will – as per the usual practice – implement a set of standard controls to manage fiduciary risks in the funded activity, inclusive of providing an avenue for reporting any irregularities which

108. MAM will serve as both accredited entity (AE) and executing entity (EE) (GCF proceeds) for this project. The Government of Bolivia, acting through the MMAyA, and the GWF will also be executing entities for the activities that are funded with their own resources.

The EE will be responsible for the following: (i) implementing activities according to outlined in GCF policies as well as the accreditation master agreement (AMA) and funded activity plan; (ii) ensuring that all financial transactions are carried out in accordance with supervision team consisting relevant staff from the FAO Country Office in Bolivia, the FAO Regional Office for Latin America and the Caribbean in Santiago (Chile), and FAO headquarters in Rome. Financial management and reporting will be supervised by the GWF, which is also performed by FAO.

111. Assurance of project financial resources will be facilitated through the review of annual and biannual project reports, as well as through audit and monitoring reports. Financial control and procurement processes will be implemented in per FAO rules and regulations. The project will be subject to the audit regime of FAO, including the external audit and internal audit functions.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP202

## Proposal name:
Upscaling Ecosystem Based Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Rural Communities in the Valles Macro-region of the Plurinational State of Bolivia (RECEM-Valles)

## Accredited entity: Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO)
## Country/(un): Plurinational State of Bolivia
## Project/programme size: Medium

### I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel (based on the resubmitted funding proposal dated 12 December 2022)

#### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: High

##### Background:
The project "Upscaling Ecosystem Based Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Rural Communities in the Valles Macro-region of the Plurinational State of Bolivia" (RECEM-Valles) is a climate-resilient rural development initiative that targets farmers, their communities and livelihoods from crops to processed food products, livestock production, agroforestry systems, and markets), ecosystems (i.e. water, forests, land, soil), built infrastructure (including water irrigation), and food security in the Valles Macro-region of the Plurinational State of Bolivia. The project is designed to address climate change impacts such as increased frequency and intensity of extreme temperature and precipitation) and extreme weather events (drought, hail, floods), which are exacerbated by deforestation and land degradation. The project aims to benefit 12,503 households (64% female, 36% male), 1,251,769 indirect beneficiaries (of which 86 per cent are women and 200 are indigenous peoples), and 1,251,769 direct beneficiaries. The total project cost is USD 63.3 million with GEF contribution of USD 40.5 million. The co-financing from the Ministry of Environment and Water (Ministerio de Medio Ambiente y Agua [MMAyA]) is USD 29.5 million, and the Federation of Municipalities (FAM) is USD 6.5 million.

The project area consists of six municipalities covering an area of 83,800 km² situated at an altitudinal gradient ranging from 140 to 4,700 m above sea level. The average annual rainfall in the region is 920 mm and the population density in the project area is 27 inhabitants per km². The Valles Macro-region

The region is characterized by subsistence farming in low-productivity agricultural systems; however, the irrigated farms are responsible for 60 per cent of the total production of potatoes and 85% of the maize produced in Bolivia.

1. Agricultural products are mostly sold at local markets. Farmers sell on the streets and/or directly at local community fairs (in peri-urban areas via mobile food, fruit or vegetable crop trucks) and municipal markets, or indirectly through traders or aggregators for wholesale supply to supermarkets and other retail outlets.
2. Smallholder farmers in the Valle Macro-region are often far away from the farms and connected by roads in poor condition, and thus relatively costly to the farmers.
3. **Project Target Group:** A total of 81,551 farmers, known as heads of agricultural production units, will be identified and selected, with women given priority.* The project targets smallholder farmers who are landowners or tenants on farms that have less than 0.5 ha titled land (as certified by the National Institute of Agrarian Reform), relying mainly on agriculture and producing cash crops for their own consumption and for market (e.g., maize, vegetables, fruits, coffee, cocoa, etc.). These smallholder farmers are characterized as having limited access to resources (e.g., money, skills, knowledge, farm tools and equipment) and relying on subsistence farming. Smallholder farmers in the Valle Macro-region who are women farmers are at great risk of being pushed into conditions of extreme food insecurity as a result of climate change and other factors such as poverty, lack of education, and gender inequality.
4. Smallerholder farmers in the Valle Macro-region have weak adaptive capacities. They have limited access to resources (e.g., money, skills, knowledge) and rely on subsistence farming from agricultural activities due to poor crop production. Limited access to agrimercos owing to minimal infrastructure (poor road quality, high tolling costs etc.) (e.g. transportation) and few skills and no access to modern technologies, all of which factors contribute to the smallerholder farmers’ capacity to invest in the technologies and inputs they need to plan for anti-pollution measures and climate change adaptation strategies, leading to food insecurity and poverty.
5. **Impacts of current and projected climate change:** As per the funding proposal, the Plurinational State of Bolivia is highly vulnerable to the effects of climate change, such as the increase in extreme events, which are affecting waterbodies (hydrological drought, frost, hail), with the latter being a major concern for the country. The country is already experiencing a range of climate change-related impacts, including an increase in the occurrence and intensity of extreme weather events, and an increase in the frequency of increase of 0.32°C to 0.84°C in the last 25 years.* The country is also experiencing rainfall variability and changes in precipitation patterns. Climate model projections indicate that climate change will continue to degrade areas of the Plurinational State of Bolivia’s agricultural lands, with a projected decrease in crop yields by up to 30% under Representative Climate Change scenarios (Representative Concentration Pathway (RCP) 4.5 and RCP8.5) for 2050 in the Valle Macro-region project an increase in temperature from +2.7°C to +3.4°C. This change will have a significant impact on the country’s agricultural sector, leading to food security loss through evaporation from soils and transpiration of vegetation.*
