## Development barriers

Among the barriers identified are: (i) limited technical support for smallholder farmers in applying the appropriate technical know-how and innovation to adapt traditional agroecosystems to increasing climatic variability; (ii) lack of support for smallholder farmers to access markets, credit, and insurance; (iii) limited opportunities to participate in other stages of the agricultural value chain; (iv) lack of appropriate institutional frameworks and policies that are conducive to adaptation to climate change; (v) limited access of smallholder farmers to financing to build resilience; (vi) inter-institutional coordination taking place at the regional level, without addressing the needs of smallholder farmers in the municipalities; (vii) lack of adequate information on climate monitoring and evaluation processes, climate data and best practices in adaptation; (viii) lack of integral water management systems that are able to provide a sustainable supply of water for climate-resilient watershed restoration and conservation practices; and (ix) local institutions and communities have limited access to financial resources due to technical and coordination constraints.

### Summary of development problems

The above-mentioned narratives described in the funding proposal seek to address the following: region, along with its peoples, communities, ecosystem resources, built infrastructures, both soft (finance, markets) and hard (water, irrigation systems), are highly vulnerable to climate change due to high exposure to climatic variability and extreme events due to its very high exposure and sensitivity and very low adaptive capacity. The region is located in the Bolivian part of the Valles Macro-region and limited availability of water resources during the dry season. This represents a high territorial risk considering that in 2019, agriculture consumed approximately for per cent of all available water resources in Bolivia. The region has an agricultural intensity of 15.3 per cent of the national gross domestic product and producing more than 90 per cent of the country's soybeans.

### RECOM-Valles project (funding proposal)

The funding proposal seeks the transformation of the Puno-Malvin State of Bolivia in line with its country’s nationally determined contribution (NDC) under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC). The project will be implemented through the Forest Mitigation and Adaptation Mechanism for the Integral and Sustainable Management of Forests and Moist Ecosystems (FIMMA), which is a mechanism that allows smallholder farmers through transformed land management practices, enhanced climate-resilient ecosystems, and increased income security. This project seeks to implement the Bolivian government’s theory of change: “If vulnerable smallholder farmers in the Valles Macro-region of Bolivia implement climate-resilient agricultural systems, then their vulnerability will be reduced.” The RECOM-Valles project will contribute to the implementation of the Joint Mechanism for Adaptation and Mitigation (JAM) as a means by which smallholder farmers in BOLIVIA create climate resilience of smallholder farmers and an increase by transforming land management practices into climate-resilient ecosystems and strengthened governance capacity.”


---
**Page 155**
---

15. Component 1 of the project focuses on climate resilient agriculture implemented and managed by farmers for increasing the productivity and resilience of their agroecosystems. The resilient agricultural management will enable farmers to adapt to the main climate risks the project covers: increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events, including droughts and rainfall. These practices include a shift to producing crops with greater resilience to higher temperatures and drier conditions. This component is also based on supporting farmers with the modernization of irrigation systems and water management in order to increase their water use efficiency.

16. Component 2 complements Component 1 by promoting activities which seek to increase the resilience of smallholder farmers, livestock keepers and other vulnerable groups (e.g., women) against climate risks through improved access to water resources and services. Activities promoted under Component 2 include (but are not limited to): harvesting rainwater and improving the household irrigation systems with drip irrigation; construction of water reservoirs for livestock watering and revitalization of existing irrigation systems, increasing their resilience to climate variability and reducing their vulnerability to droughts; and the implementation of climate-resilient agricultural practices. Activities promoted under Component 2 include the implementation of technological innovation (irrigation water management, soil moisture monitoring, etc.), including the use of drip irrigation and micro-irrigation systems, drip irrigation, construction of water harvesting reservoirs, among others. These activities are implemented in areas with a high risk of droughts as identified by hydrological modelling (see funding proposal paragraph 41 onwards), which identifies the areas with a high risk of droughts as those where there is no water deficit and there is sufficient surface water that allows farmers to connect to the existing larger irrigation networks.

17. Component 3 seeks to ensure that all water resources within the project area can be restored and conserved, ensuring availability not only for the water users within the project (especially linked to Component 2) but also for the ecosystem functions. Component 3 seeks to ensure the sustainability of the water resources in the long term by enabling the irrigation domains as implemented in Component 2.

18. Component 4 seeks institutional strengthening and enhanced access to climate finance to sustain and strengthens the Components 1, 2 and 3.


### Adaptation impacts

As an integrated and holistic approach in increasing the resilience of smallholder farmers, water and irrigation facilities, water and watershed resources, agricultural livelihood and business value chains, the project will ensure that all adaptation measures are “no regrets” solutions, if implemented correctly.

The multi-pronged approach of the activities and outputs, as presented in the funding proposal, ensures a combination of short-term and good and reasonable work, with one activity synergistically building on the success of another. The first set of combinations of key activities can be defined as follows: (i) water resources management; (ii) climate-smart agriculture increasing the adaptive capacities of CFS farmers through the acquisition of new/upgraded skills and training in climate-resilient agricultural practices, including the use of climate-smart technologies (e.g. drip irrigation) and information (e.g. agrometeorological advisory services); and (iii) water and irrigation facilities via upgrading water tanks, reservoirs and irrigation systems. These measures will be implemented to enhance the transformative capacities of farmers, agrobusinesses and business value chains; these resilience measures will also contribute to the development of a climate-smart agricultural sector (e.g., focus on agribusiness, finance and markets), the establishment of climate-smart production facilities (e.g. cold chain logistics, agribusiness centres), the provision of business advisory services (e.g


---
**Page 156**
---

organic fertilization, incubation of community productive ventures, seedling business diversification, facilitating agriculture loans), to institutionalizing governance measures for a sustainable agroecosystem (e.g., mainstreaming climate in operation and maintenance (O&M) plans, developing climate-resilient products and services such as green credit, parametric insurance) [output 4.2].

If the project is implemented correctly, it is expected to directly benefit 81,553 heads of households living in vulnerable areas (e.g., smallholder farmers, pastoralists, and indigenous populations of the Valles Marce region), including 2,000 indigenous peoples; it will impact 1,251,769 indirect beneficiaries (i.e., 4.8% of the total population and 3.5% of the total macro-region population). It will enhance the resilience of 3,240 agricultural management areas, 4,494 ha agricultural lands and 17,510 ha prioritized micro-watershed areas.

### Risks to adaptation

In the previous submission of the funding proposal at the thirty-fourth meeting of the Board (B34.1), the independent Technical Advisory Panel (independent TAP) raised the issue that if the project is implemented as proposed, it could lead to maladaptation and other potential unintended consequences that may lead to maladaptation, thus undermining the overall impact of the funding proposal. The risks identified by the independent TAP include: [i] increased emissions resulting from the increased economic activities as the funding proposal lacked greenhouse gas (GHG) assessment; [ii] risk of further exclusion due to an inadequate selection process for project beneficiaries; [iii] limited access to financial services and lack of innovative finance products (output 4.2); [iv] limited private sector participation (output 4.2); [v] limited capacity building for the project team, including the lack of a more in-depth assessment of a farmer's ability to adapt to climate change; [vi] risk of over-reliance on short-term resilience measures for farmers and agroecosystems due to lack of a more in-depth assessment of how these measures would be sustained beyond the project period (output 4.2); and [vii] weak baseline assessment on agroecosystem resources due to weak demonstration of how the interplay of climatic and non-climatic factors impacts the agroecosystem (e.g., forests and water).

Following the request for B34.1 for clarification and discussion with the independent TAP, the project team has reviewed the issues raised by the independent TAP and reasonably addressed the aforementioned issues and submits this new funding proposal for B35.

Risk of increased GHG emissions: In the previous submission of the funding proposal to B34.1, the project team acknowledged that increased economic activity in the agroecosystem naturally lead to an increase in economic activity. The potential risk of increased GHG emissions may come from the use of fossil fuels for transportation and energy generation, as well as from the use of livestock manure and other organic fertilizers. The use of diesel-powered vehicles (e.g., tractors) for agricultural activities such as plowing, planting, and harvesting would surely increase carbon dioxide (CO2) emission intensity (tonne of carbon dioxide/diesel/hour-hour (tCO2d/hh)), depending on the source of power. The increased use of vehicles in the project area could lead to an increase in CO2 emissions from transport. This would also lead to an increase in the carbon footprint of cargo per tonne-kilometre and per passenger-kilometre, as well as the amount of fossil fuel used for transportation. Waste that is either burned or left to rot as waste, thus creating another source of GHG emissions. The risk of increased GHG emissions may come from the use of livestock manure and other organic fertilizers. The use of diesel-powered vehicles (e.g., tractors) for agricultural activities such as plowing, planting, and harvesting would surely increase carbon dioxide (CO2) emission intensity (tonne of carbon dioxide/diesel/hour-hour (tCO2d/hh)), depending on the source of power.

The project team has conducted a baseline assessment for the targeted project area; without this baseline assessment, it was difficult to determine

**Page 157**

whether or not the proposed activities would be potential sources of GHG emissions, and whether or not the planned agroforestry and/or waterbody restoration (e.g., reforestation) activities were enough to sequester and store carbon off to reduce the project's emissions. Thus there was a need to assess the overall impact on the project's emissions in terms of its agricultural value chains from production to the markets, taking into consideration that the funding proposal was also looking at increasing the resilience of the whole agroecosystem, and across the different components of the project. This would help to better understand the risk of maladaptation (i.e., increased emissions due to the project). As cited in the text memo annex 3, Working Group 1 had already identified a number of key assumptions and/or potential impacts for the project if a component were likely to lead to a significant increase in GHG emissions.

When the independent expert was asked to clarify this risk during the first submission for B.14.02, he mentioned that "a detailed GWP analysis" would be required, but the project interventions are assumed not to lead to increased GHG emissions on the contrary they will reduce them. The project is expected to increase soil organic matter content by healthy soils, reduced emissions and increased GHG sequestration availability of water."14 The AE further noted that "the project is expected to reduce emissions from livestock by producing wanted product that is sent to landfill. These emission reductions would be balanced against increased emissions due to the production of feed for livestock and the use of fertilizers, and that B2A cold chains appear to be approximately neutral in terms of their climate impact. The project could also lead to increased emissions if it were to promote poor agricultural practice technology in terms of energy efficiency and use of low-GHG emission refrigeration equipment. The refrigeration equipment will not use HFCs (hydrofluorocarbons). The national GHG monitoring will contribute to the assessment.

With the funding proposal resubmission for B15, FAD addresses this issue. An EX-ACT analysis15 was conducted to assess emissions and reductions related to key elements of the value chains affected by the project activities, including inputs and construction of irrigation and rainwater harvesting reservoirs, livestock feed production, land management (reduced tillage), fertilizer use, refrigerant leakage, and forest management. The evaluation assessed emissions compared to the baseline scenario for each activity. The results show that the total GHG emissions from the EX-ACT can be disaggregated by components, each reflecting a different activity (see EX-ACT calculation appendix). The baseline scenario is defined as the current situation in 2015. The baseline appears in the cropland module, in the section on annals. Given the computation of data (detailed in the annex), the total GHG emissions from the baseline scenario for this activity is equal to -162,944 Co2 eq. This is the net difference between the carbon balance from the baseline scenario (273,072 Co2 eq) and the carbon balance of the "with project" scenario (435,016 Co2 eq). The main activities that contribute to these emissions are reduced tillage), and residue management are the main improvements; irrigation and manure application are also important. The baseline scenario includes all other GHG emissions from project inputs, such as the construction of rainwater harvesting reservoirs, irrigation systems, collection and distribution of water, livestock feed production, and forest management. These activities emits 781,566 Co2 eq over a year-20 period. Finally, activity 3.1.2 aims for reforestation and agroforestry in the project area. The baseline scenario includes all other GHG emissions from this activity which generates the highest potential carbon sink as -994,119 Co2 eq over 20 years. Overall, results show a positive environmental impact due to the implementation of the project activities. However, if we consider that the project is implemented in the whole area under focus, this would amount to a carbon balance of minus 0.6 Co2 eq per hectare by year (paragraphs 224 and 225 in the funding proposal). ### Risk of farmer exclusion

In the first submission for B34, the independent TAP raised the issue that there was a risk of exclusion for a group of farmer beneficiaries. There was uncertainty about whether individual farmers would be able to access the funding. Individual farmers may be excluded from some of the key transformative activities, for example, enhancing the resilience of farmers and crops (output 1.1) and agricultural livelihoods (output 1.2); climate-smart agriculture (output 4.1), and accessing financial services and savings accounts to finance output 4.2.

#### General selection criteria

The general criteria for the selection category of a farmer beneficiary were as follows: (i) residence within the project municipalities; (ii) high dependence on agriculture and/or natural resources; (iii) small-scale family farmers; (iv) those who are not able to access or benefit from other public services due to exposure to environmental and climate change risk; (v) household with five or more members; (vi) agricultural production is the main source of income for the household, and willingness to implement project management practices. The criteria seem fair and reasonable.

#### Specific project activities

When it came to specific project activities under the project outputs, the farmer beneficiaries would be different from those in current context 1. In that case, there will be a need to ensure that the distribution of climate-smart equipment the target farmers beneficiaries will be the same as those who are currently benefiting from the project. This is because the majority of smallholder farmers, frost and drought, which undoubtedly generate greater poverty, food insecurity and migration. The beneficiaries would also be different for output 4.1, which is the distribution of horticultural, fruit and livestock, and agricultural production, so the beneficiaries will be members of communities or irrigation associations that have been affected by drought and extreme phenomena of increased temperature and reduced rainfall.

#### Funding proposal

As per the funding proposal therefore, the selected farmers for outputs 1.1 and 1.2 would be benefiting from activities under output 4.1. The farmers who are currently benefiting from outputs 1.3 and 1.2 also would be benefiting from the activities under output 4.2. From the description, there was also a distinction between farmers who are organized (for output 1.1) or not organized (for output 1.2).

#### Additional criteria

However, based on the funding proposal (tables of indicators and criteria) and annex 2 (indicators and criteria), in addition to the general criteria, there were other more specific criteria that included the following: (i) farmers who are members of producer associations with availability of counterpart in-kind or financial resources; (ii) producers associations with at least two years’ experience in implementing climate-smart agriculture practices as means of maintenance of the equipment for output 1.1 activity 1.1.13); (iii) producer associations with at least five years' experience in implementing climate-smart agriculture practices and activities; (iv) areas close to urban and/or peri-urban cities with high potential for the consolidation and/or diversification of agricultural production, where there is a need for the establishment of producers with possibilities of counterpart in-kind and/or financial resources (activity 1.2.3); and (v) family farmers with investment capacity as counterpart (activities 2.1.1, 2.1.2, 3.1.3 and 3.1.2).

With these additional considerations, the independent TAP is of the view that the funding proposal submitted for SB.34 generated a risk of excluding the most poor and vulnerable, non-organized or newly organized, remotely located farmers. The previous funding proposal created an opportunity to include these farmers in the project, as their existing financial capacities were likely to be left out as potential (project) counterparts in outputs 1.1.1 and 4.2, which include important resilience initiatives of the project. One advantage of preferring the organized farmers is that they are more likely to have access to a broader range of private sector prospect for the proposed innovative financial products and services of the financial institutions under consideration. This would also be in line with the TAP’s view on the importance of financial institutions’ lending activities instead of re-directing the vulnerable farmer’s activities and income.

With the funding proposal revision for SB.35, FAO addresses this issue. FAO has revised the criterion regarding investment capacity and financial resources in activities 2.1.1., 2.1.2 and 4.2 to ensure that all farmers will be included as potential project counterparts. The criterion that beneficiaries must be in areas close to urban or peri-urban centres has been removed from these activities, which will promote associativity to foster inclusiveness. This action will facilitate participation in producers’ associations and other forms of collective organization (see the revised funding proposal, page 131). The producer associations will become a means to help the most vulnerable farmers to access financial services and to be included as potential project counterparts. Activities 1.1.1., 1.1.2., 1.2.1. and 4.2 have been revised to employ in-kind contributions (such as labour) to ensure no one will be excluded left behind. The above adjustments were more reflective of the TAP’s views on the inclusion of all farmers as potential project counterparts.

Risk of ineffective financial products, services and components of innovative financing instruments (1.3.2). In the previous TAP revision, the proposed TAP raised this issue. One major impediment to the design of new innovative financial products was the impact of the Financial Services Law No. 98/2014 on the agricultural sector. There is little to no information on how this Financial Services Law has impacted the agribusiness markets in general and the agricultural credit market specifically, especially for the smallholder sector; the success of a new financial product or service remained to be seen, thus potentially undermining the TAP’s confidence in the proposed innovative financing instruments. The AED needs to assess the prevailing sources of formal and informal financing and the actual barriers among the farmers, including those who are most vulnerable, to inform the design of these products.

#FAO Matrix with revisions made after independent TAP review 29 November 10 (CRITERIA FOR SELECTING TARGET GROUPS AND PROJECT COUNTERPARTS)

The Government of the Plurinational State of Bolivia issued in August 2013 a new Financial Services Law (No. 98/2013) aimed at regulating financial services and activities, especially those related to credit, savings, insurance, leasing, and other financial services, especially for the farming segment, fishers, timber producers, artisans and small, medium-and micro-enterprises. The law also aims at improving access to finance by the most vulnerable groups in Bolivia, including women, indigenous people, and rural communities. It is expected that this law will contribute to the development of a more inclusive financial sector and segmentation of economic segment and potentially establish caps on interest rates but have been challenged by microfinance institutions. The law also aims at improving access to finance for the most vulnerable groups in Bolivia, including women, indigenous people, and rural communities. With the funding proposal resubmission for R35, FAD addresses this issue and has completed an in-depth agrifinance assessment.

(a) The market analysis covers the following areas: an overview of the financial system in Bolivia; a review of current fiscal/banking regulations related to the agricultural sector; an overview of the main actors involved in the financing of the productive agricultural sector and impact of the financial services law; an overview of the state of agriculture in Bolivia, including the role of credit in the production process from the perspective of the borrower and the lender; and a brief discussion of the state of agrifinance in Bolivia.

(b) The agrifinance market study (Feasibility study, pages 16-18) confirms the previously stated finding that the interest rate cap discourages both traditional commercial banks and local financial intermediaries from investing in the productive agricultural sector. It also shows that there are three categories of productive credit vehicles; and given fixed interest rates, these are more profitable for lenders than loans provided by financial intermediaries than for rural farmers. The agro-market function, in addition to the normal market functions, is affected by the impact of climate change on the productivity of climate change, which impacts productive loan repayment reduces the performance of the loan portfolio and increases the risk of default (as defined by the Financial system supervisory authority). Similarly, climatic effects have adverse effects on customers in the productive sector (especially agriculture), since their expected yields may be reduced.

On the demand side, the agrifinance market study indicates that, in rural areas, the main demands for credit are to finance agricultural inputs and equipment. The role of financial intermediation (since 2013) and others at third place with an average of 15 per cent per year (Feasibility study page 19).

# Risk of ineffective selected adaptation and resilience measures for farmers and agroecosystems

Paragraph 114 of the funding proposal has been updated to note that "RIO" is considering accreditation as a GCF direct access entity for Bolivia and could potentially be a partner in supporting these capacity strengthening efforts within other government programs.

## Risk of ineffective selected adaptation and resilience measures for farmers and agroecosystems

The proposed project design is based on lessons learned from past irrigation projects, particularly those implemented by the World Bank and by establishing an O&M program that reflects best practices developed over the past several decades (see funding proposal paragraphs 113-114). The analysis led to GMR, a PROA report, which showed that 20% of irrigation systems from 2020 showed that after three years, only 20 per cent of the irrigation systems presented problems with management and operation. This is in contrast to the situation in the 1990s (Center for Intercultural and Indigenous Studies). 1991 reported that the prevalence of irrigation projects in Bolivia was generally limited or non-existent.

## Risk of ineffective selected adaptation and resilience measures for farmers and agroecosystems

These lessons contributed directly to the design of the current project proposal, which includes a focus on strengthening the management and operation systems. Developing capacities and following up at least 2 years are essential to sustainability (funding proposal, paragraph 117), providing support and capacity development of smallholders for the correct operation and timely maintenance of irrigation systems.

The proposed drip and sprinkler technology system is considered to be easier to handle compared to other technologies such as industrial machinery, digital equipment, etc. There are also examples in the study area where farmers have successfully used these systems after having adopted this in different Macromissions with irrigation committees in place (see funding proposal paragraph 105; feasibility study, pages 48 and 96).

### Slash and burn
In the previous submission for B34, there was also the unresolved issue regarding slash-and-burn farming. This practice not only deforestation but also severe land degradation by affording gradual tropical erosion in the hilly and mountainous areas of the Valley Macromission. The slash-and-burn system has washed out sediments. These factors damaged the farm and irrigation equipment, lessened its total capacity, and reduced the productivity of the crops grown on these farms for poor farmers. The simultaneous operation of traditional-based slash and burn and a modern irrigation system appeared counter-productive to the overall improvement of farming practices, with slash-and-burn farming being abandoned.

With the funding proposal resubmission for B34, FAO agreed that "the project fully anticipates the need to address the issue of slash-and-burn farming by promoting more conventional practices to more sustainable farming techniques. Paragraph 100 in the funding proposal directly addresses the topic of slash-and-burn techniques and proposes, under activities 2.1 and 3.4, the promotion of alternative agricultural production practices (described on page 165 of the feasibility study). In addition, climate-resilient agriculture is a key component of the project's overall approach, which means that farmers will have to move towards more technological practices (see funding proposal paragraph 103).

### Missing focus on farmer health and productivity
In the first submission for B34, the indicators used in the baseline survey were not sufficiently comprehensive. Farmers are much more exposed to the impacts of climate change and its possible effects on their health and production capacity. For example, a prolonged drought or an unexpected extreme weather event could reduce farmers' effectiveness as well as their long-term interest in farming. The funding proposal did not cover an analysis of the impact of climate change on farmers' health and productivity. It also did not consider the farmers' ability to carry out agricultural crop farming or how the frequency and duration of extreme weather events (droughts, floods) and their impacts have forced some farmers to migrate out of the Valley Macromission. The funding proposal also did not mention the previously briefly mentioned health and productivity, focusing instead on the activities and crops without considering the farmer carrying out these activities.

In the first submission for U.S.FAF the baseline assessment was not comprehensive enough and only adequately described how the interplay of climatic and non-climatic factors impacts the agroecosystem (i.e. forests and water). This will include increased wood harvesting, slash-and-burn activities) The funding proposal needed to be further developed to provide a better understanding of climate change impacts on agroecosystems, especially on the degradation of forest and water essentially it asks to if these pressures are due to climate change more than the pressure to clear forests for food production or other uses.
### With the funding proposal resubmission for U.S. FAF addresses this issue FAO has adequately considered the impacts of climate change on agroecosystems and their contribution to the observed impacts This has been done with the support of data available and provided by national universities engaged in hydro-climate monitoring initiatives The WEAP (Water Evaluation And Planning System) model is used to simulate water balance under future climate variability, and anthropogenic activities on the hydrological cycle. The model considers the main factors that influence water availability and demand, including precipitation, snowmelt, modeled considering changes in land use and land cover (2018-2020) for the climate of 1960-2050. Other scenarios have been considered to assess the impact of climate change on water demand have been studied considering evapotranspiration population growth, and other factors. The WEAP model has been applied at different levels (i.e., basin, sub-basin) geographic units within the Macro-region. The conclusion from these analyses is that the changes in discharge modeled in the climate change scenarios can mostly attributed to climate change and not land use change. This conclusion is supported by the results of paragraph 50 reflecting the summary of this analysis. Feasibility study (pages 83-97) presents a detailed description of the WEAP model and its application. The study details the methodology specifying the model used to calculate evapotranspiration. The increase in average temperature has led to an increase in evapotranspiration, which is associated with prolonged droughts in the Chiquitano-I dry forest over the last 39 years and supports the hypothesis that climate change is a major driver of these changes in water availability. However there is not sufficient data to estimate the future loss of forest area due to fires forest fires by climate change Reference evapotranspiration is directly related to increase in temperature and changes in precipitation patterns. The funding proposal has treated the climate mitigation component of the project as a co-benefit; as per the RF and funding proposal, the main objective of the RECBM-Valley project is adaptation to climate change. However, the baseline scenario used in the analysis is based on the FAO Natura2000 (FAN) 2021 using WEAP compares the hydrological demand and supply for the period 2015-2065 with the climate change scenarios: MIROC RCP 4.5 and MIROC RCP 8.5, as well as the socio-economic development pathways to 2025 (Dewberry et al., 2013). The difference between the two scenarios for the period 2015-2065 is less than 1% in terms of water demand and supply on a monthly basis (figure 34 of annex 2), the difference between RCP 8.5 and RCP 4.5 is barely visible, and water demand decreases marginally. September and October are the months with the highest water demand. Based on this finding, the WEAP tool was adequately used and the need for better water management systems (as stated in the TAP questions) is clearly shown.

### Risk of maladaptation

Risk of maladaptation is the first sub-issue for 5.3. In the independent TAF raised this issue. If not fully grounded on farm-ecological issues, due to the weak farmer baseline assessments, the proposed adaptation and resilience in the funding proposal may lead to unintended consequences. For example, if the project proposes that farmers should plant more trees as a result of the availability of water resources and modern irrigation equipment, deforestation is entirely against the GCF mandate. New access from informal sources to financial slime and slash burning facilities could lead to increased unsustainable farming practices. Similarly, if the project proposes that farmers should use improved irrigation facilities and equipment close to a forest patch may lead to increased unsustainable farming practices. The FAF also notes that the proposed adaptation strategy of providing credit as a result of the availability of water resources and modern irrigation equipment deforestation is entirely against the GCF mandate. New access from informal sources to financial slime and slash burning facilities could lead to increased unsustainable farming practices. Similarly, if the project proposes that farmers should use improved irrigation facilities and equipment close to a forest patch may lead to increased unsustainable farming practices.

Then there are other issues related to the proposed adaptation strategy of providing credit as a result of the availability of water resources and modern irrigation equipment deforestation is entirely against the GCF mandate. New access from informal sources to financial slime and slash burning facilities could lead to increased unsustainable farming practices. Similarly, if the project proposes that farmers should use improved irrigation facilities and equipment close to a forest patch may lead to increased unsustainable farming practices.

On with the funding proposal response for 5.3. FAF addresses this issue: FAO has added a section on adaptation related to maladaptation in Section 4 of the Funding Proposal. The FAF further confirms that the project does not (i) promote short-term adaptations that decrease the resilience of the farming system; (ii) transfer resources from one vulnerable group to another, or one area to another; (iii) ensure sustainable development through adaptation strategies that are not based on a thorough understanding of the social, environmental and economic values. It also states that maladaptation can be avoided by flexible, multifunctional, inclusive and long-term planning and implementation processes. As noted in the funding proposal feasibility study and environmental and social management framework, the project's

### Sustainable development potential

#### Scale: High

1. **Economic co-benefits:** If implemented correctly, the RECEM-Vales integrative approach will improve and transform the traditional agriculture-based economy into a climate-smart agricultural system. The economic co-benefits of this transformation are expected to be significant as the agroecosystems will surely increase economic activities as presented in the economic and financial analysis (see paragraph 70). These economic benefits will directly arise from the de-risking of the agricultural activities for farmers, especially in the last mile segments, focusing on improved access to major markets, access to finance and to skills and knowledge. The economic co-benefits are expected to be significant as these will arise from the de-risking of the financial transactions through the issuance of new innovative growth capital instruments (see paragraph 70). These economic benefits will also arise from the success of such de-risking activities and replication in the sector in other regions, especially in the context of the Brazilian economy's recovery from the 2019 coronavirus disease pandemic and the impacts of global economic shocks on the agricultural sector.

2. **Gender mainstreaming:** The funding proposal has a gender assessment and gender action plan, and gender has been fully mainstreamed in each of the four funding activities. Women are expected to be beneficiaries of all four funding activities as they manage 68 per cent of the households and agriculture-related activities. Women are given access to economic and financial instruments, and in fact, priority is set on the project activities. Women are given access to

# Environmental co-benefits

If implemented correctly, the carbon sequestration through soil, reforestation and afforestation, increased vegetation and avoidance of forest degradation will be significant. The project is considered sustainable considering that the targeted areas are part of the endangered Amazon Rainforest. Forest management activities such as selective logging, silviculture, and fire prevention and eliminating deforestation in the project's intervention area. Starting with a forested area of 1,22 million ha, the business-as-usual scenario anticipates an annual deforestation rate of 0.34 per cent resulting in 587,950 tons CO₂ emissions annually. Project activities will reduce this deforestation and support restoration, resulting in incremental GHG emission savings of 2.5 million tons CO₂ equivalent (tCO₂e) over the project period. This will contribute to development and climate change is addressed, water resources will be protected.

## Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)

The project can be linked to several SDGs including: SDG 1 "No poverty" (target 1.2); SDG 3 "Good health and well-being" (targets 3.5 and 3.9); SDG 6 "Clean water and sanitation" (targets 6.4, 6.5 and 6.6); SDG 7 "Affordable and clean energy" (target 7.1); SDG 8 "Decent work and economic growth" (target 8.3); SDG 12 "Responsible consumption and production patterns" (targets 12.4, 12.5 and 12.9); SDG 13 "Climate action" (target 13.2); and SDG 15 "Life on land" (targets 15.1, 15.2, 15.3, 15.4 and 15.5).

### Risk to sustainable development

The project covers 68 municipalities in the Plurinational State of Bolivia. The proposed activities ought to lead to behavioral changes of people, and involve a multitude of farmers, farmer associations, indigenous peoples and women. In the first funding proposal submission for R$40 million, the project was linked to SDG 15 "Life on land" (targets 15.3, 15.4 and 15.5). The project goals to the funding proposal beneficiaries would have undermined the implementation of the proposed activities. In addition, the lack of a clear definition of the project's objectives, the weak monitoring of the smaller/nuisance farmer activities may lead to environmental degradation.

This risk is reduced in the present funding proposal submission for R$35 million.

Overall, based on the above, the sustainable development potential of the project is assessed as high.

# Needs of the recipient

There is a need to leapfrog towards climate-resilient agriculture. Smallholder farmers typically are engaged in subsistence farming on a piece of land (approximately 0.5 to 2.3 ha), on which they grow staple crops such as maize, beans, rice and cassava; root crops like sweet potatoes, maize and vegetables, and other complementary crops depending on the altitude, such as bananas or coffee. The crops are grown in fields that are subject to flooding because rain falls mainly via rainfall for irrigation using existing ponds and canals. The crops are increasingly exposed to climate change impacts such as droughts and floods. The proposed project addresses these needs. Component 1 seeks to increase farmer resilience towards climate change by providing them with the necessary knowledge to diversify agriculture from production to market. Component 2 seeks to increase the resilience of the watershed resources that support farming activities via restoration of the micro-ecosystem.


---
**Page 172**
---

### Component E: Enhances farmer resilience via enabling access to finance and improving governance of ecosystems

The holistic approach of the project, covering the whole business value chain from production to post-production and access to markets, along with the integration of climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices (e.g., soil management, collection centres, cold-chain logistics, governance on water, irrigation), leads to the transition towards resilience of the agroecosystems. The needs of the farmer recipients of the seven departments are clearly discussed with the target farmer beneficiaries based on the stakeholder consultations conducted in each department.

#### Component E: Enhances farmer resilience via enabling access to finance and improving governance of ecosystems

Component 1 complements Component B; Component 2 complements Component 1; Component 3 seeks to ensure the sustainable management of water resources, which is a key component of the project. Without this, unregulated use would risk inefficient use of water and watershed resources, a rise in territorial area and indigenous peoples conflicts, degradation of ecosystems, and increased competition for land by farmers or expansion needs.

Outputs 4.1 and 4.3 address these needs.


#### Need for financial inclusive products and services

Component 4 needs to be highlighted, mainly because of the urgent need to address access to finance of the smallholder farmers, which is a key component of the project. The de-risking of the former activities, trading between farmers and end-users (output 1) and financing of the latter (output 2), are two major components of the project. These are also major steps towards sustaining the financing needs and it is, hoped, sustainable, climate-friendly investments in the agricultural sector.

The financial institutions' learning-by-doing approach on climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices will ensure access to finance by smallholder farmers, and ensures that these farmers are part of the project. The financial institutions' learning-by-doing approach on climate-smart agriculture (CSA) practices will ensure access to finance by smallholder farmers and services for similar financial institutions catering to smallerholder farmers and MSMEs (output 4.2).

In the first submission for B3.5, the independent TAP raised this issue on the potential drawback of a learning-by-doing approach: the limited or poor assumptions of the baseline document on the impact of CSA practices on the transaction costs and the different rate caps on financial institutions, lessons learned on financing from informal sources may lead to insufficient de-risking of the former activities. The project should therefore be better focused on de-risking of the financial institutions rather than of the farmer transactions. Financial inclusion is a key component of the project and it will help to include more of the farmer population and avoid the poorest and vulnerable and higher risk profile farmers.

With the funding proposal submission for B3.5, FAO address this issue by completing the baseline document with the assumptions on transaction costs (output 4.1).


#### Need for governance measures for agroecosystem services

Water and watershed resources are first and foremost the most important resource in the RECCEM-V project. The project will contribute to the implementation of a governance mechanism (e.g., PTF), territorial platforms for management of water, watershed resources, and land use planning. This is a key component of the project. Without this, unregulated use would risk inefficient use of water and watershed resources, a rise in territorial area and indigenous peoples conflicts, degradation of ecosystems, and increased competition by farmers for survival or expansion needs. Outputs 4.1 and 4.3 address these needs.


---
**Page 173**
---

### Risks to needs of recipients

If the funding proposal is not carried out properly owing to poor communications and execution of the project components' activities and subactivities, and most of all, a delay in the development of effective financing products/services support as envisioned under the funding proposal, there will be a risk that the project will not meet its objectives and expectations on the stakeholders. Thus the risk of a return to a BAU situation is likely, especially for the most vulnerable and resource-poor farmers. One mitigating factor for this is in the availability of funding from other sources, such as the National Fund for Rural Development (FONAR), which are obliged by Law N° 777 of the Integral Planning System of the State, to elaborate an annual Territorial Project for the Transfer of Technology (PTTD). This plan includes a budget, where funds are allocated by the Ministry of Planning.15

Overall, based on the above, the needs of the recipients are potentially high.

## Country ownership

### 1.5 Country ownership

The RECCM-Valles project is fully aligned with the country's NDGs. The funding proposal supports the implementation of the Joint Mitigation and Adaptation Mechanism for the Integral Territorial Project for the Transfer of Technology (PTTD) in line with climate change initiatives and policies include the Law on Decade of Transition 2015–2025, Plurinational Political Constitution (2019), National Biodiversity Strategy and Action Plan 2018-2030, Framework of Biodiversity 2019-2030 and Law 300 – Framework on Mother Earth and Integral Development for Living Well.

The funding proposal has strong government support and representation. The co-financing letters from the key government stakeholders show strong ownership of the projects. MAFIA (Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry Industry and Animal Husbandry) is the lead agency in the programme (USD 29.5 million sourced from budget resources). Government financial institutions such as FONAR (National Fund for Rural Development), Fondo de Agua Potable y Saneamiento (Water Supply and Sanitation Fund) (Tarita Water Fund and Santa Cruz Water Fund) are also directly part of the project.

### Ownership and decision-making in the Plurinational State of Bolivia

The ownership structure is complex, as it depends on the perspective of the Bolivian institutional structure, where ownership is recognized by the three types of institutions: state (central government), local governments (departments, provinces, municipalities, associations,16 and union organizations). The State Political Constitution (2019) establishes that "Bolivia is organized territorially into departments, provinces, municipalities, communes, and communities. These territorial units are the basic levels of convergence in this form of territorial organization: autonomy and decentralization." The following entities, which represent the three types of institutions, have been identified as key stakeholders for the consultations in the formulation of the RECCM-Valles project: the Associations of Communities and/or the Autonomous Municipal Governments of Sapucay, Sucre, Potosi, Camargo, Tarixa.

16 Independent TAP support for Bolivia, FAD 14/05/2014.
17 See Theory of Change, paragraph 77 of the funding proposal.
18 The Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry Industry and Animal Husbandry departmental associations of municipalities in Beni (AMBIENDE), Cochabamba (AMBECO), Chapare (AMBACO), Santa Cruz (AMBCRUC), Oruro (AMBORU), Pando (AMPANDO), Tarija (AMBTAR), La Paz (AMBPAZ), and El Alto (AMBELA). The associations of municipalities are composed by Mayors and Councilors (CONMUNES), whose members are the vice departmental associations; and by the Bolivian Association of Municipalities (ABM) which represents the 34 departments, the 10 provinces, the 26 communes, the 5 capital cities and the city of El Alto (Law No° 548, 2014, Art. 2).
19 Bolivian Community System of Association represented by producers' associations, federations and confederations.

## 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: High

The economic analysis shows that without the GCF grant component, the project does not generate sufficient financial returns for farmers over the Lyapu implementation period.

With GCF participation, the financial viability hurdle is surpassed; the project generates robust economic returns for farmers over the Lyapu implementation period. These returns contribute to the long-term sustainability of rapidly deteriorating forests and wetlands in the Plantation State of Bolivia, and supports the GCF goal of low-carbon and climate-resilient development.

The results show that the project is economically viable without GCF support; however, it can be undertaken by farmers without GCF support, despite the significant positive externalities and public goods provided by the project.

### Economic and financial analysis

The project also demonstrates that a holistic intervention in the project area, from production to access to markets, from farming inputs to access to credit, is necessary for increasing the economic returns of farmers. This increases their resilience as well as the resilience of farmers' ecosystems, irrigation infrastructures, and MSMEs business value chains, greatly increasing the sustainability of the agriculture sector and enhancing food security.


---
**Page 176**
---

## As demonstrated by the economic analysis, given a discount rate of 12.7 per cent (as mandated by the government of Bolivia) and a carbon price of USD 40/CO2t, the RECF-Valles project shows a positive net present value under a 5-year horizon and USD 24,323 million over a 10-year horizon. However, for the 5-year horizon (which includes benefits only) only yield a negative net present value under a 5- and 10-year period (i.e. minus USD 41,694/ha/year 5 years and USD 22,895/ha/year 10 years). As highlighted by the FAO in more recent studies (e.g., see footnote 3), the project is not only a financial investment but also provides farmers' climate change-related losses and risk increase in financial returns for farmers over the medium to long term. The RECF-Valles project has been designed as an innovative financial incentive to continue the resilience-strengthening activities (including irrigation) and provide additional benefits to farmers, such as improved access to credit, increased agricultural productivity, improved and more predictable cash flows also increase their willingness to access productive credit, which is a key element for the success of the project. The RECF-Valles project will allow intermediaries to extend innovative lending and insurance products to support continued investment in irrigation systems.

# Introduction

Climate change impacts in Bolivia are not gender-neutral. The different roles, resources, opportunities and agency of women and men influence how climate change affects and is responded to locally.

Bolivia has achieved important progress towards gender parity in education and access to labour markets as reflected by the Gender Gap Index². Bolivia stands at 43% for this index in 2020; however some indicators show persistent disparities between men and women and in particular indigenous groups. The gender gap is widest for the economic participation and access to equitable financial resources.

The proposed project, “Strengthening Ecosystem Based Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Rural Communities in the Vailes-Maquis region of the Plurinational State of Bolivia (RECEEM-Valles)” aims to strengthen the resilience of agricultural producers in the Valles Macrorregion, located in the departments of Cochabamba, Chuquisaca, Potosí and Tarija. The vast majority of this area is rural. This region is inhabited by more than 2,308,746 people, and approximately half of them are women.

At the national level, the project will strengthen Institutions and governance mechanisms related to water management and climate-resilient agriculture to benefit vulnerable smallholders and their communities. Given the water scarcity in the project intervention area, the project will also focus on the development of water harvesting and irrigation conservation services to ensure water provision to rural communities under climate change scenarios. In parallel, the project will design climate-resilient agro-ecosystem technology packages and provide technical assistance for their implementation. The overall objective is that more sustainable and resilient agriculture will contribute to food and nutritional security, leading to climate resilient communities. Women empowered through more dynamic participation in agriculture would result in more gender-neutral food distribution within households and better health for both women and children.

The needs that differentiate women and men within the project are anchored in the access to resources and the low autonomy of women in terms of their personal priorities. The clear sexual division of labour, which is a key factor in this region, reinforces these differences. Social, cultural, economic and political constraints such as violence at different levels that restrict women's active participation in decision-making processes at family and community level. Furthermore, women are more vulnerable to climate change impacts and promote leadership in different livelihood systems across ecosystem and environmental functions.

The project will distribute the distribution of timely information on climate and agriculture as well as sharing of knowledge and skills obtained by farmers through Farmer Field Schools (FFS). Given that key gender gaps include education and access to financial assets, the project will seek

<sup>2</sup> http://www3.weforum.org/docs/WEF_GGGR_2020.pdf


---
**Page 188**
---

to address these disparities through gender transformative actions to ensure gender equality in the access to trainings, technology and financial services.

The project will also contribute to the implementation of specific contexts related to adaptation to climate change, including gender issues along the entire value chain – from policy, public administration, education to agricultural input, product, credit and insurance and consumption – so that adaptation to climate change by farmers will be socioeconomically feasible, and hence sustainable.

The objective of the Gender Assessment is to provide the foundation for effective gender mainstreaming in the Green Climate Fund (GCF) project, “Upcycling Ecosystem Based Climate Resilience in the Bolivian Amazon: The Case of the Upper Beni River Basin” (hereinafter referred to as Bolivia (RECAM Valles)”. The Analysis examines the socioeconomic conditions of women and men living in the upper Beni river basin. It provides a gender analysis of the project, thereby allowing a strategic approach to the integration of gender dimensions into the project, summarised as the Gender Action Plan (GAP). The underlying theory of change is: the project will create opportunities for women and men to participate more equitably and according to existing gender differences, which will allow communities in this Valles bioregion to adapt to climate change in a sustainable and equitable manner.

### 3. BACKGROUND

#### 3.1 Green Fund Policy on Gender Equality

Gender norms and related cultural rules are part of what structures the interactions and reactions to climate threats and opportunities in human systems by influencing roles, expectations and decision-making processes. Climate change affects women and girls, men and boys differently because of existing gender inequalities, gender discrimination and social exclusion. These inequalities create structural barriers that reinforce other barriers. These often restrict women’s and girls’ access to and control over resources, legal rights or political participation and decision-making, threaten their peace and security, and thereby undermine their ability to adapt to climate change. Women and men are also differently affected by climate change as the negative impacts are aggravated by existing gender inequality and systemic discrimination. This can lead to increased vulnerability for women which also reduce the effectiveness of sustainable development and poverty alleviation measures.

The Fund’s gender policy builds on the existing gender policies and gender action plans of other climate funds. It systematically integrates key principles elaborated in the Fund’s own environmental and social framework, including the need to ensure that projects are carried out with consideration of marginalized and vulnerable groups and of human rights. It highlights the principle that women and men have different needs and capacities for adaptation and their ability to attain through this process. It acknowledges and integrates the need to apply an intersectional analysis in addressing gender inequalities and recognizes the need and ability to reduce vulnerability and adapt to climate change impacts as a lens to understand the complexity and particularity of inequalities in the lives of women and girls, men and boys, including their interdependence and interconnectedness. The policy also takes into account the economic profile and societal structure of the country or subnational region, specific climate

2 ANNEX 4 to GPG: Gender policy and gender action plan (Updated version approved in March 2021). Initial GP and GAP approved in March 2020). Page 8


---
**Page 189**
---

Impacts, variety of livelihoods, a host of sociocultural factors such as class, age or race as well as other change processes in societies, such as those brought on by globalization, migration, urbanization and economic development

The Fund's gender policy is human rights-based and congruent with international instruments in acknowledging the centrality of women’s rights to universal human rights, in particular with all the United Nations’ human rights conventions. It is also aligned with the Convention on All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW), the International Labor Organization’s (ILO) core conventions, the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs) and the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and the 2030 Agenda for Sustainable Development. It supports the equal value of women and men in all areas and activities funded from the Fund's resources in order to increase their adaptive capacity and reduce their vulnerability to climate change impacts, facilitating a transition towards a gender transformative approach.

### 3.2 FAO Policy on Gender Equality

The goal of the FAO Policy on Gender Equality (PGE) is to achieve equality between women men in sustainable rural development and food security for the elimination of hunger and poverty In order to achieve this goal, FAO's work will be guided by the following four objectives, which highlight key dimensions to be addressed for promoting gender equality in agriculture, rural development and food security: increasing access and control over assets and resources; and services for all:

- Objective 1: Women and men have equal value and decision-making power on rural institutions and organizations to shape relevant legal frameworks, policies and programs
- Objective 2: Women and men have equal rights, access to and control over natural and productive resources, to contribute to and benefit from sustainable agriculture and rural development
- Objective 3: Women and men have equal rights and access to services, markets, and decent work in the agricultural sector and food security
- Objective 4: Women's work burden is reduced by enhancing their access to technologies, practices and infrastructure and by promoting an equitable distribution of responsibilities, including at household level

FAO Policy on Gender Equality Strategy and Approaches

The Organization will adopt a twin-tracked strategy for the promotion of gender equality and women’s empowerment:

- **Gender mainstreaming**: FAO will ensure that its normative and technical work systematically integrates a gender perspective and responds to the different needs, interests and capacities of women and men. This will be done by identifying and addressing the barriers which intersect with gender (such as age, marital status, disability status, religion, race, ethnicity, socioeconomic status, etc.) and can create and reinforce inequalities, not only between women and men but also within each group.
- **Targeted interventions**: When the gender gap is particularly wide, FAO will implement projects or programs that specifically target women and/or focus on the promotion of gender equality as their main objective.

<sup>2</sup>FAO. Policy on Gender Equality 2010–2030.


---
**Page 190**
---

In all cases, FAD will adopt integrated and gender transformation approaches that not only take into consideration the different needs of women and men but also actively seek to redress unequal power dynamics by challenging the discriminatory social norms, behaviours and attitudes that are at the root of persisting gender inequalities.

Through this Policy, FAD is committed to promote the gender equality objectives in all its mandate areas, including agriculture, fisheries, aquaculture, forestry and livestock; nutrition; natural resource management; climate-change adaptation and mitigation; emergency response; rural infrastructure building; markets; trade and value-chain development; decent employment and the elimination of child labour; social protection; and the empowerment of rural institutions.

### 4. DEFINITION OF TERMS

The Fund's gender policy makes reference to a number of key gender concepts. They underline and describe the ambition of the Fund's gender approach to achieve increasing levels of gender integration through continuous improvement with the ultimate goal of achieving gender equality in all its operations. Progress towards gender equality can be illustrated as a continuum of gender integration levels and approaches (figure 1).

Figure 1: Illustrating the Gender Integration Continuum

| Gender blind | Gender aware | Gender sensitive | Gender responsive | Gender transformative |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| GENDER | EQUITY | |

(a) Gender: refers to the roles, behaviors, activities, rights, and attributes that a given society at a given time deems appropriate for women and men and boys and girls. In addition to these social attributes, gender opportunities are defined by being male or female and the relationships between women and girls and men and boys; gender also refers to the relations between women and girls and men and boys. These attributes, opportunities, and relations are shaped by cultural norms and values as well as by socialization processes. They are context-time-specific and changeable. Gender determines what is expected, allowed and valued in a woman or a man in a given


---
**Page 191**
---

In most societies there are differences and inequalities between women and girls and men boys in responsibilities assigned activities undertaken access to and control over resources as well as decision-making opportunities Gender is part of the broader socio-cultural context and intersects with other important criteria for socio-cultural and economic development.
(b) Gender aware refers to the knowledge and recognition of the differences in socially assigned gender roles rights entitlements responsibilities and obligations while accommodating these differences in planning programming and implementation.
(c) Gender balance refers to the goal of having the same number of women and men in decision-making bodies and among staff in the different levels of organizational structures and institutions. It is a strategy for making the experiences and concerns of all people regardless of gender an integral part of the design, implementation monitoring and evaluation of interventions.
(d) Gender blind refers to the failure to recognize that the roles rights entitlements responsibilities and obligations of women girls men and boys are assigned to them in specific social cultural economic political and historical contexts and backgrounds.
(e) Gender equality refers to the equal rights responsibilities and opportunities access of all women men boys and girls and the equal distribution of their respective interests needs and priorities. As gender equality concerns and benefits men and women boys and girls not only women but also men and boys are required to fully engage in efforts for achieving gender equality. It is a human rights issue as well as a precondition for, and indicator of sustainable people-centred development.
(f) Gender sensitivity refers to the ability of individuals organizations and institutions to recognize differences between women and men girls and boys. It recognizes the need for potential differential treatment that is fair and positively addresses a bias or historical social disadvantage or power imbalance that is due to gender and/or other criteria such as age race ethnicity religion disability and so on. Treatment that takes into account the different needs of women girls men and boys cultural barriers and (past) discriminations of the specific group.
(g) Gender mainstreaming refers to the process of assessing the impact of a men's condition or position in role in society. It occurs inequalities terms their participation, their access to opportunities rights power influence make decision incomes benefits and so on.
(h) Gender identity refers to a person's innate deeply felt internal individual experience of gender.
(i) Gender mainstreaming refers to a globally supported strategy for promoting gender equality Mainstreaming involves the process of assessing the implications for women and girls men boys of any planned action including legislation policies or programmes in all areas and at all levels. It is the integration of an equal perspective into the design implementation monitoring and evaluation of interventions. It is a strategy for making the experiences and concerns of all people regardless of gender an integral part of the design, implementation monitoring and evaluation of interventions.
11


---
**Page 192**
---

that different gender groups benefit equally, and inequality is not perpetuated. The ultimate goal of mainstreaming is to achieve gender equality.

(i) Gender responsive: refers to the consideration of gender norms, roles and relations and to addressing inequality generated by unequal norms, roles and relations through changes in policies and practices.

(k) Gender sensitive: refers to the consideration of gender norms, roles and relations but does not necessarily address inequality generated by unequal norms, roles and relations through changes in policies and practices.

(l) Gender transformative: refers to approaches actively striving to examine, question, and change rigid social and gender norms, cultural values and to address power inequalities between men and women. This approach seeks to challenge stereotypes, discrimination and exclusion as well as seeking to redefine systems and institutions that create and perpetuate inequities. The goal of this approach is to transform adverse gender norms and power relations into positive ones, thus increasing achievement of gender equality.

(m) Intersectionality: refers to the interaction between social, political, economic factors such as race, ethnicity, migratory status, religion or belief, health, state of age, caste, orientation, gender identity, and inclusion and exclusion. Looking through the lens of intersectionality is critical for understanding the complexity and particularity of inequalities experienced by women.

(n) Women's empowerment: refers to the process by which women gain power and control over their own lives and acquire the ability to make strategic choices through an expansion of their options and opportunities, especially via participation and decision-making. It generally refers to differential or pro-active support to increase:

(i) women's awareness and sense of self-worth and rights;

(ii) women's access to resources;

(iii) women's right to have access to opportunities and resources;

(iv) women's right to have power to control their lives both within and outside the home.

(v) women's ability to influence the direction of social, political and economic change to create a more just social, political and economic order; nationally and internationally.


5. METHODOLOGY

The gender assessment draws on the information obtained through consultations with farmers and provincial government officials, gender analysis of the county recently produced, and other relevant information sources – such as research articles, policies and statistics on agriculture and rural development.

Workshops were held in different municipalities in the Vales Macomberang, where the project will intervene to assess the current situation on gender equality in the context of climate change. The workshops were centred on the gender roles in three main themes: 1) Sustainable Farming Systems; 2) Irrigation Systems; and 3) Sustainable Management of Water Sources.

Subsequently focus group discussions with female leaders took place to further specific aspects regarding the family economy, decision making, climate impacts and coping strategies.


---
**Page 193**
---

Likewise, semi-structured interviews were conducted with key actors from institutions that work in the area.

Finally, the project’s logical framework was validated by the same key actors, which has actions and indicators that guarantee gender and intergenerational equality throughout the design, implementation, and evaluation of the project. Additionally, the Gender Action Plan aims to address inequalities and promote gender and generational equality throughout all of the project's activities.

To the greatest extent possible, the assessment is based on official statistics and published research from the target provinces and municipalities. Where relevant information could not be found, it relies on that of the rural areas of the target provinces, or of the whole nation. Where no such information is available, the Assessment may refer to other sources.

### 5.1 Purpose of the Gender Assessment and Action Plan

Conduct a Gender Assessment to identify the main gender gaps affecting the municipalities with which the “Upgrading Ecosystem Based Climate Resilience of vulnerable rural communities in the Valley of the Confluence of the Plurinational State of Bolivia” project will be working, and then define a Gender Action Plan that enables this project to contribute to reducing the gender gap and inequalities.

### 5.2 Approaches

- **Gender equality**  
  Gender equality means that women and men enjoy the same conditions and opportunities to exercise their human rights in full and realize their potential to contribute to political, economic, social and cultural development and benefit from its results. It therefore means that society values both women’s and men’s contributions equally and that it is not biased with the changing roles that men and women play.

  This implies that the views, experiences, aspirations and needs of women and men have been considered, and that these have been valued equally and given the same priority.

  In short, the principle of gender equality seeks to ensure that the differences between men and women are recognized as a natural fact but not as an obstacle. Women and men should be everyone, regardless of their biology and the social roles that may be associated with them.⁴

- **Intersectionality**  
  In discussing intersectionality, the Committee on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women has explained that discrimination based sex and gender is intertwined with other factors that affect women, such as race or ethnicity, religion or belief, health, status, age, class, caste, sexual orientation and gender identity. Discrimination based on sex and gender may affect women from certain groups to a different extent or in different ways than men. Therefore, States Parties must legally recognize and prohibit these

## 5.5 Tools

The following tools were used:

- Contextual information, which provides an initial overview of the intervention area, using quantitative data to find out about the local reality and determine how gender gaps can be identified, taking account variables including education, health, employment and access to natural resources, among others.
- Group interviews, to gather qualitative information of the women. The aim of this was to obtain a gender and generational perspective that would enable gender gap and inequalities between men and women to be identified within their own context and living conditions.

## 6. INSTITUTIONAL FRAMEWORK

### 6.1 Plurilateral Service for Women and Dismantling Patriarchy (SEPMUD)

Supreme Decree No FP 3774 of 16 January 2019 mandates the creation of SEPMUD: Its purpose is to monitor, follow up on and evaluate the implementation of public policies aimed at dismantling patriarchy to promote the effective exercise of women's rights.

Its roles and responsibilities include: coordinating with head of sector institutions on the design and implementation of public policies that aim to provide women access employment, credit, land, technology, information, public services and other elements that help to ensure the


---
**Page 197**
---

**SEMAIS has developed a Consolidating Feminist and Decolonization Agenda 2020-2035, which includes seven main areas which will govern actions related to gender in Bolivia over the next ten years: (1) Political rights; (2) Economic rights and poverty eradication; (3) Social rights; (4) Rights of women against violence; (5) Rights of children and adolescents; (6) Discrimination and the right to information; and 7) Young women's rights.**

In taking forward the government agenda 2015 and the dismantling patriarchy agenda, the Alliance of Women’s Community Organizations for the Democratic and Cultural Revolution held a national meeting in La Paz on October 3rd, 2016, which approved the following plan: “12 proposals for dismantling patriarchy”, which covers the following key thematic areas: (1) For a life free from violence; (2) For women’s economic independence; (3) For women’s productive work, with the state and society sharing responsibility for the task of caring for life; (4) Effective access to health care for women; (5) Promotion of sexual rights and reproductive rights; (6) Redistributing power in the family; (7) Women’s right to education and knowledge production and representation; (8) Strengthening care for Mother Earth; (9) Production with women's participation, for a life free from violence; (10) For the expansion of the social housing programme and (12) Giving continuity to social programmes.

### 6.2 Vice-Ministry of Equal Opportunities (VIO)

This vice-ministry is part of the Ministry of Justice. The VIO has the responsibility for equal opportunities related its gender, age groups and disability.

The Vice-Ministry of Equal Opportunities is the head of sector institution for the design and implementation of public policies aimed at eliminating all forms of discrimination against women and girls in Bolivia. It works with other government institutions and agencies, including at the departmental level, through the Departmental Gender Offices or Departmental Gender Units (UDG). The VIO also provides support to the Social and Legal Services (SLLM) that provide support to victims of violence and the Child and Adolescent Defense Unit.

It has drawn up the “National Equal Opportunities Plan – Women Building the New Bolivia for Living Well” (PNQOL), which was approved by the Supreme Court in 2016. In this plan, it is recognized that women contribute disproportionately to the country’s development. This recognition is expressed in equal opportunities to access services, full participation of women and girls in decision-making processes, and the distribution of technological resources and wealth, thus creating the conditions for a life free from gender-based discrimination.

The PNQOL is organized around six thematic areas: (1) Economic, productive and labour issues; (2) Education; (3) Health; (4) Gender-based violence; (5) Citizenship and political participation; and (6) Institutional strengthening.


---
**Page 198**
---

### 6.3 Ministry of Rural Development and Lands (MDRyT)

The MDRyT has prepared the Plan for the Agricultural and Rural Sector with Integrated Development for the Long Term - PAGARD 2016-2020, which takes into account the gender for development approach with the objective of promoting gender equality and equal opportunities for men and women.

### 6.4 Ministry of the Environment and Water (MMMAA)

The MMMAA has drawn up a Gender Strategy for the purpose of achieving the involvement of women. The main objective of the strategy is to "Establish institutional directives to guide the work of all the actors involved in the original project cycle to maintain the gender perspective, with the aim of reducing the inequalities that exist between men and women." Its aim is to promote real and effective participation by women in all stages of the project cycle and as active agents in decision-making processes. The strategy also aims to provide training so as to encourage and motivate professionals who provide technical assistance and support services to adopt a gender perspective, with particular emphasis on attitude changes in families in order to enable men and women to participate under equal conditions.

Adult literacy rate
