Poverty rate

- In urban areas, poverty affects 9.5% of men and 14.7% of women; in rural areas it is 23.1%. However, the rate of extreme poverty in rural areas was 34.6%.
- Extreme poverty affects 0.4% of women and 0.8% of men. Four out of ten women live below the poverty line compared to only one in five men (gender statistics).
- With regard to the feminization of poverty, Bolivia had a score of 85.7 on this index for 2013. The average score for Latin America and the Caribbean was 94.6; the lowest score was 59.3% (ECLAC) and Gender Equality Observatory for Latin America and the Caribbean.

Participation in the workforce (sex-disaggregated)

- Participation in the workforce in urban areas is 82.8% for women and 66.9% for men.
- In rural areas, the rate is 32.5% for women and 75.2% for men.
- Distribution of time devoted to care work by women: 33.3% devote it to 4 hours or less; 18.0% spend between 4 and 6 hours (full-time care); 15.8% devote up to 10 hours, 13.2% devote it to 1 hour and 7 days do not carry out this type of work.
- Time devoted to paid or unpaid work: 90.8% of women are involved in paid work; 64.2% of men are involved in unpaid (reproductive) work caring for themselves and others, and working for the community. Women spend an average of 13 hours a day on care work, compared to 5 hours a day, completing a double or triple-day's work. This makes it impossible for them to carry out their own life plans and projects.
- Women also have time for their own life plans and projects, with the consequent stress, feelings of being overwhelmed, fatigue and lack of expectations.

Employment rate (sex-disaggregated)

- The employment rate is 59.8% for women and 69.9% for men, with an average of 59.5%. This shows that out of 100 women in the population, only 59 are employed.

According to FAO data, at the household level there is clear evidence that low levels of household income and wealth are clearly related to different forms of malnutrition. For example, the disparity between the richest and the poorest quintiles in Bolivia is more than fivefold.

With regard to the prevalence of undernourishment in the population as a whole, between 2016 and 2018 it stood at 17.5% in Bolivia. This is considerably less than in 2009 when it was 34%. The situation has improved significantly.

Focusing on children under five, the national level prevalence of emaciation in 2018 was 6%, and that for wasting was 10%. In 2018, 5.7% of Bolivian children under five are overweight. These figures are closely related to the quality of the diet among this population group.

The proportion of children aged one to four years old (1-4) in 2016 was 30.2%. This is a consequence of a poor quality diet starting in childhood and continuing into adulthood, which leads to obesity later on.

The prevalence of exclusive breastfeeding of babies until they are five months old was 53.8% in 2018. The effect of this low figure is the malnutrition that affects the population as a whole, especially women and children in Bolivia, which negatively impacts their subsequent growth and development.


Domestic violence

• Many women in rural areas are likely to experience violence throughout their relationships with a partner, exceeding the national average by eight percentage points. The prevalence of intimate partner violence experienced during the last twelve months exceeds the national average by three percentage points. The prevalence is 10% for women aged between 25 and 34 years old compared to seven percentage points for those aged over thirty-five.

• In urban areas, 71.3% of women who are married or in a common law partnership experience intimate partner violence throughout the relationship, which is three percentage points higher than the national average. The prevalence of intimate partner violence experienced during the last twelve months is 50%, compared to 42% for all women.

• By 2021, according to information from the Ministry of the Presidency, there are 36 cases of domestic violence against women (aged between 18 and 35 years old), placing the country as one of the most violent against women in Latin America. According to the Attorney General's Office, 34 cases of infanticide have been reported nationwide in 2021.

# Analysis of Gender Gaps at a National Level

## Women’s Legal Status

The text of the Constitution of the Plurinational State of Bolivia (2009) includes an entire section on Human Rights, which is based on international treaties and conventions on human rights and the rights of specific groups of people (indigenous peoples, women, older adults and people with disabilities), and gives their rights constitutional status.

The Political Constitution of the State integrates principles regarding the rights of women and men in the following articles:

- Article 11: Prohibition and punishment of all forms of discrimination.
- Article 14. I, II, III and IV: Inclusion without discrimination.
- Article 15: Equality between men and women.
- Article 147 and Article 210: Right to health and social security.
- Article 351: Right to social security and safe motherhood (I, II, III, IV and V. Right to reproductive rights).
- Article 61.1 and 6.2: Rights of the family. Article 638, Article 641 and II. Sexual and reproductive rights.
- Article 70: Equality in access to public services and public administration.
- Article 300, paragraph 30: Competences of municipal governments.
- Article 302, paragraph 30: Financial policy with equal opportunity criteria.
- Article 318. Paragraph 4: Equal opportunities for women and men.
- Article 338: Non-discrimination in access, tenure and inheritance of land.

Bolivia has enacted several laws that promote gender equality, such as Law 348, the comprehensive law to guarantee women a life free from violence; Law 243 against political harassment and discrimination towards women; Law 645 against racism and all forms of discrimination. Law 011, the framework law on decentralization and autonomy; and Law 263, the comprehensive law to combat human trafficking.

In 2010, five fundamental laws established in the Constitution of the Plurinational State were enacted: Law No 18 on the Plurinational Electoral Authority, enacted on 16 June 2010


---
**Page 205**
---

Law N°25 on the Judiciary, enacted on 24 June 2010

Law N° 26 on the Electoral System, enacted on 30 June 2010

Law N° 27 on the Constitutional Tribunal, enacted on 6 July 2010

Law N° 28 on the "Andris Balder" Framework Law on Autonomies and Decentralization, enacted on 19 July 2010

These five laws include the gender perspective, thus achieving significant progress towards guaranteeing women's rights, especially with regard to: (i) the principles of gender equality, parity and attention to the specific needs of women in all areas of public life; (ii) the strengthening of the role of state; (iii) the recognition of political harassment as a crime in election processes; (iv) the prohibition of domestic violence against women and children, including sexual violence; (v) the prevention of violence; (vi) acknowledgment of the importance of allocating sufficient government budgets for the implementation of gender equality policies at the autonomous territorial entities, allocating a minimum 10% to the budget of each autonomous entity at the municipal and regional level.

All these laws comply with the international human rights conventions and treaties to which Latvia is a signatory and are oriented towards achievement of the SDGs.

• In 2017 the Multisectoral Plan was prepared to further address gender gaps in the country and boost Women's Right to Live Well.
• In 2018, the 2020-2030 depopulationisation and decolonization agenda was developed and focused on: 1) Political rights; 2) Economic rights and the eradication of poverty; 3) Social rights; 4) Cultural Identity; 5) Rights to health care; 6) Education of violence; 7) Communication and Information Rights; and 7) Women's Rights.

Various instrument mechanisms and legal and technical regulations have been developed for the implementation of the SDGs. The programme "Food Production and Forest Restitution" aimed at achieving an accelerated increase in the coverage of services. It also contributes to increasing the income of farmers and forest owners, as well as improving their living conditions. This programme considers the general needs of family farming and economy. It does not systematically address gender equality issues. However, it is possible that some of these cross-cutting manner in the institutional principles to be projected in a natural way in the strategies and action plans. In this context, the following laws have been passed in favour of Food Sovereignty:

- Law 337 (2013): Law to Support Food Production and Forest Restitution: Establishes an economic and social framework for the development of food production and forest restitutions and whose beneficiaries benefit from the "Food Production and Forest Restitution Programme".

- Law N°300 (2012): Framework Law of Mother Earth and Integral Development for Living Well: Establishes the vision and foundations of integral development in harmony and balance with nature.

- Law N° 144 (2013) Law on the Community Agricultural Productive Revolution: Regulates general principles, objectives and tasks related to agricultural production and forestry, establishing the institutional foundations, policies, technical and financial mechanisms for the production, transformation and commercialisation of agricultural and forestry products.

- Law N° 071 (2010): Law on the Rights of Mother Earth recognises the rights of Mother Earth, which are enshrined in the constitutions of the national State and aims to guarantee respect for these rights.

- Law N° 035 (2010): Framework Law on Autonomies and Decentralization Model: It regulates the autonomy regime by mandate of Article 271 of the Political Constitution


---
**Page 206**
---

of the State and the basis of the territorial organization of the State. It establishes the policy of integral rural development and food sovereignty in coordination with the autonomous territorial entities as a competence of the Central Government.

Law No. 1705 (2004): Law on the Promotion of Ecological Non-wood Agricultural Production that regulates, promotes and strengthens the sustainable diversification of agricultural production systems through the creation of the National Council for Ecological Production (CNACE, acronym in Spanish) responsible for planning, promoting, regulating, managing and supporting the implementation of programmes and projects, providing technical assistance and the development of ecological agriculture.

Law No. 1706 (2005): Forestry Law that regulates the sustainable use and protection of forests, ensuring their conservation for future generations, harmonising the social, economic and ecological interest of the country.

There is a law that specifically mentions gender and generational equality in its principles:

- **Law No. 338 The Law on Rural Native Indigenous Organizations and Community Economic Organizations for the integration of Sustainable Family Farming and Food Sovereignty** mentions gender and generational equality in its principles.

    - Article 1 Clause 2: Gender Equality. The obligation to take forward actions that guarantee gender equality, where the family made up of men, women, young people and older adults takes priority, is hereby accepted. The differences and complementary roles between men and women are recognized as well as their equal participation in and contribution to social, economic and productive development for Living Well.

    - Article 1 Clause 3: Inclusion of Rural Young People. Actions are to be taken forward to promote the comprehensive development of the capacities of young women and men in rural areas, encouraging the transmission of the knowledge and skills held by parents and grandparents to their children and grandchildren. The inclusion of rural areas to the city shall be developed, recognizing that young women and men are essential to the development of sustainable family farming in rural communities.

    - Article 1 Clause 5: Recognition of Rural Women's Contribution to Production. The principle is hereby accepted that the role played by rural women in contributing to the Bolivian women to sustainable family farming producing food for consumption within the community, as well as the production of a surplus and the development of the productive sector, is hereby recognized.

### 8.2 The most common beliefs, perceptions and stereotypes that exist concerning gender

- There have been important legal advances about gender equality in Bolivia since 2009. However, when applying public policies (especially in rural areas), there are still social barriers, stereotypes and beliefs that have made it impossible to advance as desired. In addition to the fact that these barriers are not only present at the level of policy formulation but also during implementation.


---
**Page 207**
---

• The role of a woman as a food producer, their contribution to the economy of their family and country, and all related roles relevant to food production, remain undervalued. Additionally, women's unpaid work in agriculture is not recognized by the state, and this remains unremunerated without reducing their domestic workload, still goes unrecognized by family members.

• Unpaid work, i.e., care work and domestic work, is mainly women’s responsibility. This government policy has been criticized for failing to recognize the value of unpaid work for this type of work, which has not changed even after the inclusion of women into the labour market in 2013.

• Women are usually in charge of activities in the rural sectors, especially in the highlands and the valleys, due to temporary migration of men or the strategy of combining family economic activities with other income-generating areas. Family violence persists despite support services being offered; the number of violence incidents is increasing.


### 8.3 The labour division between women and men

• In rural areas, the tasks fall almost entirely for the female population are care work and domestic work. Women dedicate an average of four times more than men to housework in addition to nearly 38 hours to agricultural work or the labour market, while men dedicate 47 hours (including paid work).

• There is a higher tendency for men to migrate and look for salaried jobs. In these cases, women tend to be in charge of the traditionally male responsibilities in rural communities, which increases their workload. This situation can lead to an increase in domestic violence as a coping process. Men taking over traditionally female roles, including domestic and reproductive tasks, is rare. The local communities see a change in traditional roles as a temporary change rather than a permanent one. Women are not seen as the new breadwinners of the family or as a "replacement" for a man. At the same time, they are not available (because they have moved away, are sick or have died).


### 8.4 The participation of women and men in the formal and informal economy

• Among the most important barriers when considering communities and people's productivity is the lack of access to land and credit for women because of the tradition that "women" continues to be perpetuated, a segregation that causes more than 57% of the options for vocational training and employment are related to domestic work. Women have an essential role in care and reproduction, which is why we find them providing service and care to others. Therefore, women are normally excluded from prestigious and powerful educational circuits, despite their high level of education and professional qualifications. This is due to a very sexual division of labour with the inclusion of men to higher levels of specialisation and women to non-specialised training or professions related to areas considered to be weak or average aptitudes.

• When comparing the 1975-2013 period, women who are currently of working age (between 15 and 64 years) work in the labour market for a longer time than their male counterparts. In 2013, this percentage was 80%, while in 1975 it was only 20%. An increase that is due to need rather than choice.

• Women are paid less than men for doing the same type of activities. In the primary sector (agriculture and livestock), women earn two times less than men, especially in rural areas. In the secondary sector (transformations and industry), they earn two times less and in the tertiary sector (services), they earn 1.5 times less.

27


---
**Page 208**
---

• Female-dominated responsibilities are undervalued in economic terms in comparison to traditional male work. The roles that are typically designated as female are often less valued than those designated as male. Women are generally expected to fulfill the reproductive role of bearing children and taking care of their families, including childcare, cooking, cleaning, and household management tasks, as well as home-based production. Men are more likely to participate in particularly paid work, and market production.

• In the Bolivian context where women’s overall participation rates are rising, they tend to be confined to a relatively narrow range of occupations or concentrated in lower grades than men, usually in informal sectors such as domestic services, small-scale agriculture, etc., which have been ingrained to under-valued, particularly in the informal sector and subsistence agriculture.7

• Inequality in women’s work and time is expressed in working conditions, especially in wages and job security. Women are more likely than men to be employed on a part-time basis or with participation in productive work without leaving aside their reproductive role; however, as we pointed out above, this does not necessarily mean that they do not participate in the economy.

• According to studies carried out in 2016 by the Women’s Coordinator78, just 3.6% of women own non-agricultural land and only 45.9% have access to credit. This is a reflection of the fact that one of the main characteristics of female workers, whose income is among the lowest in the Bolivian society as a whole. Women define their economic situation as follows: 71.4% earn it on average, 20% earn less than they need and only 8.6% earn more.

• A 45.3% of women say that they make decisions about their personal income and 26.2% say that the decision is made by a man in the family. The remaining 28.5% do not know who makes this decision, and 11.5% have no income. A 41.8% of women do not decide on their personal income; therefore, it can be stated that 4 out of 10 women decide on their personal income and a similar number do not decide on their personal income.

### The situation of men and women in the project area

• Gender-based roles are predominant. Men tend to play the role of community representative and decision-makers, perform physical labour in the agricultural sector, and engage in higher-valorization activities such as livestock production, etc.

• On the other hand, women are responsible for domestic tasks (cleaning and cooking), reproductive activities and care work, and other activities such as ensuring the fuel and water supply. Women also have a role in managing household resources and finances within their homes, allowing them access to a small-scale income. Their role as food providers makes them more vulnerable than men when it comes to being affected by climate change.

• The economic framework in which women tend to be less valued when it comes to an economic framework. It also suggests that, in general, women can only access community decision-making processes “through” the men.

• Women are often excluded from accessing resources such as land or services like loans and education. This is a disadvantage given the growing number of women that now find themselves as the head of household.

### The access to water in the project area and how this is affecting men and women

- In the Department of Cochabamba in the provinces of Campesí, Mizque, Esteban Arce, Carrasco, Germán Jordán, Ariani, Quillacollo, Chaquepe, Punata, Arque, Esteban Arce, Capitana, Punoña and Tapiacari, it is observed that access to drinking water in homes is mostly through major rivers, springs, and ditches. In these areas, the population is predominantly young, with high proportions of women, poverty rates are high, with a high incidence of women, and this has an impact on the poor redistribution of productive and adaptation tasks, with the possibility of emigration to urban centers or migration to other countries in search of work, especially among girls (see Annex 4, Cochabamba).
- In the provinces of Vallegrande, Torola and Manuel María Caballero, in the municipalities that make up these areas, it is observed that the level of poverty is coherent with the high presence of secondary water sources such as water carts, wells, rain, rivers, springs, irrigation ditches, among others. This means that the drinking water connections that reach women as representatives of productive activities are not always adequate and do not meet their needs in certain urbanised areas and do not benefit the majority of women living in these study areas, which is why a better distribution of access to the various existing basins is required.
- See Annex 5, Cochabamba.
- In Chuquisaca in the provinces of Tomina, Zudánez, Yamparáez, Oropeza, Azurduy, Bellavista Betoño and Nori Cinti, in spite of having drinking water connections, with around 70 % of participation by women in the communities that feed the study basin, it is observed that these are sources as substitutes for meeting the water needs of the households of women in the communities that are fed by the study basin. It is pertinent to note that poverty levels are high in these areas, with almost half of the population living below the poverty line and that the level of access can be optimised as these degrees of need are reflected in the group of women in the participating municipalities.
- In Potosí, in the provinces of José María Linares, Tomás Frías, Chayanta, Nor Chichis, Sur Chichis and Cornelio Saavedra, it has been established that at least one third of the population has access to piped drinking water, and in some municipalities it reaches 74 % of the population. However, it is observed that women are not represented as users of these sources, which indicates a low level of training of women and certainly a large gap in the division of tasks segregated by sex, since despite having a large number of direct drinking water connections, this has not been translated into greater participation of women in productive activities. For this reason, it is necessary to carry out interventions in watersheds to be better distributed with training and empowerment of community women in order to promote the economic and production activities they carry out and their access to decision-making spaces. In this way, gender equality and non-gender-neutral capacities will naturally be reflected in the level of women's resources and the impact on their families.
- In the provinces of Cercado, Méndez, Avilés and Arce there is a strong component of access to water from rain, rivers, springs and ditches, but the levels of poverty are not as high as in other departments with similar circumstances, so the field study should focus on economic activities carried out by women in these areas. In this respect, it has been observed that both the poor and non-poor live in these areas. In this respect, women in the municipalities included in these provinces show that also between 40% and 50% of them have sufficient access to all water sources (see Annex 6, Potosí).

* The croplands, pastures and forests within the Project area are increasingly exposed to threats from climate variability and climate change. Climate change in turn threatens to undermine progress towards achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs), especially those related to hunger, poverty reduction and ensuring environmental sustainability on a level consistent with human needs.
* In the study municipalities of the departments of Cochabamba, Chuquisaca, Potosí, Santa Cruz and Tarija, it is necessary to have a strategy that links access to water, agricultural production and forests. This is because in these areas, the number of rainy days and springs are reduced, thus implying knowledge of the sources of water for plants and trees in the study area. Agricultural women will only improve their condition if they are aware of the balance between agricultural production and forests, as well as the role of the water resource in achieving this balance.

### 8.7 Strategies for women and men to adapt to climate change

* The strategies of adaptation employed by women and men to climate change differ significantly due to the division of labour and existing gender differences in the control over resources.
* While men focus on large-scale community interventions (for example, irrigation, river defences), women are more likely to be involved with small-scale interventions based on practical and innovative improvements such as searching for alternative water sources, protecting trees and plants from droughts, and improving their own food security conditions through other local activities. The analysis shows that men tend to adapt to climate change by adapting the use of resources.
* Women's burden of work is the main barrier to promoting female participation in training and adaptation projects in the face of climate change. Research in this field has shown that women are more vulnerable than men because they have less time for education, less access to credit facilities, and less access to information on climate change, migration, and natural-disasters. This means that women have little time to participate in training or development projects, adapt to climate change, technical training and other related production activities.

### 8.8 Inequalities that may be exacerbated by the impact of climate change

* The different activities that men and women carry out in their roles and tasks to provide for themselves and their families depend on natural resources availability. Women, particularly in rural areas, are more vulnerable than men because they have less access to land, the livelihood, health and well-being of their family, which requires natural resources that are less well managed and a suitable environment. Climate change is a threat to all of these factors once it impacts on water sources, soil fertility, livestock production, forests and other energy sources.
* In the municipalities of the east of Bolivia has seen a rise in floods, rain, and heatwaves. The west is experiencing a reduction in available water sources (more droughts, variable levels of rainfall). All of this has had an impact on available food sources. These impacts directly affect the livelihoods of women and men depending on their capacity to ensure survival in all its forms, including water and food security.


---
**Page 211**
---

* In women in rural areas dependent on natural resources, these effects are detrimental and exacerbated in nuclear headed households. Unequal access to resources and decision-making processes has also magnified these adverse effects.
* Women are more vulnerable to natural resource use as they socially constructed roles and responsibilities affect their family's economic and political status, which is lower than that of men. They also tend to have limited mobility, which means that they are left in charge of household chores and childcare while men go out to work. As a result, women suffer from malnutrition and illness in children; there is more pressure on women as their families' caregivers.
* Access to land and other natural resources is crucial for the survival of rural communities. The possibilities of survival since water and fertile soil are prerequisites for those living in rural areas. Generally, men migrate in search of job opportunities, while women are left in charge of the household.
* Women play an essential role in natural resource management due to the particular knowledge they have about their environment. However, they are more vulnerable to climate change by resorting to different survival strategies when facing a crisis. They are the ones who collect firewood and water for cooking and drinking; they are also the ones who need to ensure their survival and finally, they experience of a vast number of plants used to treat illnesses.
* This is why it is necessary to equitably the division of labour – where women play an active role in decision-making, power relations in natural resource management and access to knowledge -the better they can respond to the environmental changes threatening them.

### 8.9 Inequalities that exist between different social groups that affect the capacity to adapt to climate change

* In general, the project's beneficiaries in the municipalities of the Valles Maronifos de Bolivia are in poverty (on average 42% of the population in the region).
* However, there is a great diversity among these communities which accentuate the vulnerability of these communities. For example, limited economic resources, food or shelter.
* Some individuals from the same community are more vulnerable than others; for example, women with disabilities and children under five years old who have severe physical or mental disabilities. Therefore, their adaptive capacities are differentiated.
* Bolivia has a complex social structure that includes different ethnic groups and social groups. However, equality policies have not yet been implemented in rural areas, only welfare measures implemented.

With regards to women, there has been progress made regarding access to land tenure. However, they still lack control over natural resources and their family's income. Very few have access to credit or microfinance services. This is due to the fact that women are victims of a lack of access to technologies and training. These have a negative influence on women since this limits their ability to participate in decision-making processes.

The Plurinational State of Bolivia is a signatory to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), the agreement was ratified by law No. 1058 of 2003, which guarantees women's rights and promotes the recognition of women's land rights. Under these considerations, in 1996 this recognition became a reality with the creation of the National Institute for the Promotion of Women and the Agrarian Reform Service, which in its Article 3 eliminates the all forms of discrimination against women. This law was also ratified by Law No. 3545 of Community Renewal. At present, within the framework of the policy to fight climate change, in the Nationally Determined Contribution 2022 - 2030, it has been presented by the Plurinational State of Bolivia to the climate convention.

# Roles of men and women: Implications in terms of time and need for mobility

The project will work with beneficiary families from different municipalities. Municipalities will be considered as a whole, but each family will have its own specific needs. Based on the content and supported by the country's laws and equality policies, and women's specific needs are often not taken into account in the management of resources within the municipalities, and therefore, often become responsible for the day-to-day management of the families' natural resources. This does not mean that more responsibilities will be added to their regular tasks but rather that they will have to manage these resources with a greater responsibility for both men and women, who will promote intuitive processes about the democratization of roles in the family.

The project will also consider the time required by each participant to travel to the project site, the time allocated to training and the support needed, for example, by implementing mobile nurseries (for babies brought by their mothers).

Alternatively, the project can be implemented through other mechanisms that promote gender equality, such as the Gender Units in the municipal governments, NGOs that work on gender, the Associations of Women Farmers, or the Indigenous Women's Association of Bolivia (Confederación de Indígenas Pobres Mujeres de Bolivia Bartolina Sisa), and others. This way, at least 50% of participants will be women, guaranteed in each project cycle. This will imply a specific budget.

## Access to resources (economic, financial, physical, natural and others) by men and women: Management and control of these resources

Generally, men have access to tangible resources, meaning land, water management, engagement with the market, and other forms of income generation. Women manage intangible resources such as technical assistance, training, and education. Men manage these resources and make decisions about how they are used and controlled.

Generally speaking, women do not have access to tangible or intangible resources previously mentioned, even though their work in the agricultural sector is fundamental to the family's survival. This situation has been historically determined by the division of labour, making women quasi exclusively responsible. This has not changed with the insertion into the labour market and their general condition as household income providers.

This inequality and unjust division of work prevents women from accessing better jobs, higher incomes, greater employability conditions, participating in politics and being recognized as active economic entities.

### Access to credit of rural women

Article 3 of law 138 concerns the "internalization of the democratization and diversification of the financial system", prioritizing the development of the productive sector and the demand among historically excluded producers, as well as food sovereignty and security, with a focus on gender equality.

In addition, Supreme Decree No. 2310 of 25 March 2015 establishes Group Credit for Women Producers, including rural women:

- **Single Article**: Women who work in the productive sector, in rural and urban areas, may access loans in the form of the product called "Group Credit for Women Producers," established by the Productive Development Bank (BPD).29 This is a credit line designed especially for women producers. The loan amount ranges from 10 to 50 million bolivianos. The loans can be awarded to groups of producers (solidarity-based credit) or individuals and must

### 8.16 Women's participation in food security

Bolivia has enacted Law No. 3525 (25 November 2006), the law on the Regulation and Promotion of Collective Farming and Forest Productivity, which created the National Agricultural and Forestry Council (CNAPF), which promotes, regulates and strengthens the sustainable development of ecological farming and non-timber forest products in Bolivia. This is based on the principle that food security depends not only on having enough food but also on producing more food. It is also necessary to ensure that the food produced is good quality and safe for human consumption, and that it reaches all Bolivians, especially those who are most vulnerable and cannot access it themselves. This means that food production, processing, industrialization and marketing processes must not cause a negative impact or harm the environment.

The government policy on food security also supports an increase in the productivity of family farming (agriculture and livestock) through its national programme, agricultural insurance. It seeks to respond to the needs of vulnerable groups by means of social benefits in the form of conditional cash transfers (the Conditional Cash Transfer Programme, or Programa de Transferencia de Peso por el Efecto), which facilitate access to food and safeguard the purchasing power of the beneficiary households.

• The Juana Aruquipa Law (Dec. 0564 of 1 April 2009) provides cash transfers to pregnant women, mothers or carers of children under two, provided that they make use of preventive health services during pregnancy, that the birth is attended by skilled health staff, and that the child receives its first vaccination before their second birthday. The total amount of the benefit is € 1800 USD (Bs36) per month.

• The Juancito Pinto Law (Dec. 28.889/2006) aims to promote school enrolment and attendance until primary schooling is completed, by providing households a cash transfer conditional on their children attending school regularly. The total amount of the benefit per schoolchild is € 200 USD (Bs37.81) per month. The benefit is paid throughout primary school in the basic education system, the non-basal education system and the special education system.

• The Universal Old Age Pension (Law 3791 of 28 November 2007) is a lifetime, non-contributory pension that the State provides to Bolivians over the age of sixty who are not covered by any other social security scheme or who do not have access to the social security system or any other income from the National Government Budget. The amount per year of the Universal Old Age Pension is € 105 USD (Bs23) for those covered by a social security system, who are only entitled to 75% of the amount of the Universal Old Age Pension; and those entitled to receive a benefit to cover funeral costs.

Furthermore, Article 17 of Law 338, "The law on rural native communities and economic organizations – OECAS and their role in the development of Bolivia", which is aimed at the integration of sustainable family agriculture and food sovereignty", stipulates that one of the obligations of the Plurinational State of Bolivia with regard to sustainable family agriculture is to promote "enabling access by women to the components of production with equal opportunities and in equal conditions,


---
**Page 216**
---

particularly access to credit to enhance production and specialized technical and technological education” (Clause 13). [5]

The country also has a Food and Nutrition Policy (FANP)20 based on legislation governing the human right to food and food and nutrition security with sovereignty. One of the key thematic areas of the FAN acknowledges that in Bolivia, women are the cornerstone of family farming, agricultural work is mainly done by women, they are responsible for feeding their families, and they participate actively in the labour market and are solely responsible for the family’s nutritional status. However, due to gender discrimination and social norms, women have limited access effectively due to their limited access to and control over resources (land, water, credit, agricultural inputs) and lack of decision-making power within the household. Removing these restrictions will enable women to realize their full potential to contribute to reducing food insecurity in the country. It is therefore recommended that gender equality be systematically integrated into all policies and programmes.

Based on the above, it can be concluded that there is a favourable legal framework and policies in place that recognize the role played by women in food and nutrition security. Accordingly, their participation in technical training, access to technology, access to agricultural credit and access to markets are essential for improving food security.

Rural women are equally involved in small-scale agriculture: 41.6% (Atlas of Rural Women) and they know how to select and adapt plant and crop varieties. They know how to choose certain varieties that are more resistant to pests, diseases, droughts, floods, heatwaves and climate conditions. They know about the nutritional and medicinal properties of plant species, how they taste and how easy they are to cook, as well as food processing and storage. Family agricultural practices are also important for promoting sustainable development and eliminating hunger, obesity and all forms of malnutrition.

Nevertheless, at technical and institutional levels there is still a failure to recognize the responsibilities that women take on, their management practices and their knowledge of how to conserve seeds and other resources. Women’s productive activities and interests and needs are not usually taken into account in local planning and decision-making.

It is for this reason that the project acknowledges the different conditions and responsibilities of men and women and seeks to address them by ensuring equal access to information, training, extension services and appropriate technology. It will also promote women’s active participation, as beneficiaries, as partners and in decision-making.


One area to which special attention will be paid is to create the conditions for women to participate actively in climate change adaptation actions. The main constraints they face as a result of being solely responsible for looking after the home and the family, with the consequent lack of time for doing other types of activities that strengthen their economic autonomy.


8.17 Women’s participation in climate change adaptation actions

Bolivia has enacted Law N° 071 on the Rights of Mother Earth and Law N° 300, the Framework Law on Mother Earth and Integrated Development for Living Well. This law is a Supreme Decree


---
**Page 217**
---

creating the Pluriational Authority for Mother Earth (APMT). This agency is responsible for formulating and implementing the Pluriational policy and plan on climate change, and developing, managing and implementing policies, strategies, plans and programmes for climate change mitigation and adaptation in the country.21

However, the legislation does not differentiate between the roles of women and men. The climate change impacts and resource use differ due to the division of labour and their differing degrees of control over resources.

While men focus on large-scale community interventions (irrigation and river defences), for example, women are responsible for small-scale water management in their communities. Women tend to focus more on searching for alternative sources of water, protecting local assets, planting new crops, implementing family vegetable gardens, or initiatives to complement traditional sources of income such as selling firewood or collecting non-timber forest products.

Women's overload of work is one of the main barriers making it difficult to promote women's participation in climate change adaptation projects and training, together with the persistent patriarchal structures that do not value the roles women play to safeguard the family's subsistence.

Environmental deterioration increases the number of hours that women must devote to collecting water, firewood and plants, and reduces their capacity to guarantee their family's wellbeing and food security. This is because environmental degradation and climate change have increased, with impact it has had on their lives and how it can be remedied.

In their role as food providers, women are highly dependent on natural resources and a healthy environment. This is why they are the first to be affected by the impacts of climate change, which have been particularly acute in recent years due to drought, hail and frost.


### 5.1.8 Participation of women and men from vulnerable communities in decision making processes

- There are social, cultural, economic, political and institutional barriers that restrict women's participation in the families and the community.
- Social: Household tasks and decisions (reproductive roles), which mainly occur in the private sphere, are traditionally assigned to women. In contrast, production decisions, decisions and representing the family and community occur in the public sphere and are assigned primarily to men. This division of labour by gender already places men and women in different positions within the family, community, society and the state.
- Cultural: Women's participation is limited because they are not considered as actors in the agricultural sector and guarantee food security to their families; these communities' agriculture is still considered an occupation only for men. Women are considered to be subjects, whereas it is regarded that men are the agents of change. This reason is why women were discriminated against when it comes to inheriting land. Land ownership was passed from fathers to sons, assuming that women would not inherit or manage their own property and therefore cannot use it. Since the new SPC women have access to land ownership; however, there are still problems with the control, use, and land administration.

## Economics: Bolivian rural women are a fundamental pillar of family agriculture, their participation in agriculture is 41.8%. They work in crops, caring for small livestock, forestry, market their products and are responsible for the nutritional status of the family. However, they do not have access to adequate productive resources such as land, water, credit, agricultural inputs, training, technical assistance and decision-making; therefore, they perform unpaid and full-time care work.

## Institutionalization: Women's leadership sensitivity-training are still managing public institutions. Furthermore, indigenous organisations are fronted by male leaders who, when elected, do not necessarily represent the interests of women in their communities. In some cases, women have been implemented where there is a required 50% participation of women in these instances. However, this has brought problems such as discrimination, abuse and violence against women.

### Opportunities to promote women's leadership in local governance/political systems and formal/informal institutions

- The SPC promotes women’s leadership in local governments and its political and institutional systems through laws implemented by the Plurinational Electoral Organ that guarantee women’s rights, especially regarding parity and alternation in candidate presentation processes. In the Department of La Paz, for example, there are 10 women out of 25 candidates; therefore, there is an important representation of women in local governments in the Departments (assembly) and in municipal councils.

- The restrictions that exist for women to assume leadership roles include: (i) the lack of experience they have working in public management sectors, (ii) lack of knowledge of laws, (iii) their limited access to decision-making spaces, (iv) their difficulty in being able to delegate their domestic roles and being overburdened with work, (v) experiencing discrimination from men who do not want women to assume leadership positions. These are the main reasons for this situation. However, there are laws that have been enacted to protect them from these discriminatory situations that need to be better disseminated for them to be more broadly applied For example, Law 348, which prohibits harassment and political violence against women.

### Gender-based violence

Gender-based violence not only hurts the victim but also their family and community. Violence against women is not confined to a specific culture, region or country, nor to particular groups of women in society. It roots itself against women in the persistent inequality and discrimination against women (UNITAR, 2013).

Gender-based violence is one of the greatest scourges in the everyday lives of thousands of women in Bolivia and around the world; because it violates the fundamental rights to life and wellbeing of many of the people who make up half of the population and because it damages the social fabric.

Law No. 348, the Comprehensive Law to Guarantee Women a Life Free from Violence, entered into force on April 19, 2013. Eight years after this legislation was enacted, the figures on violence against women are not encouraging; in fact, Bolivia is one of the countries with the highest rates of femicide in Latin America.

According to figures published by the Attorney General’s Office, 33 cases of femicides were recorded in the country from January to 19 April 2021. The departments with the most recorded cases are La Paz (8), Cochabamba (7) and Santa Cruz (6). In 2020, 51 femicides were reported.

### 8.22 The needs of specific (vulnerable) subgroups were considered in the project

• These subgroups have not been considered as direct intervention populations in this project. However, when working with whole family units, these subgroups will become indirect beneficiaries.

- It is important to mention that women acquire important visibility once again when being considered as producers and workers in the agricultural sector. This is because they are responsible for the children, older adults, and family members with disabilities. These actions are known as labour division.
- The unequal distribution of time within the households between men and women goes against women's autonomy and overall food security for the family. It is a fact that women's productive activities are more demanding in terms of time than those of men. This leads to an unequal distribution of time dedicated to paid and unpaid work, and to domestic care work. Women have less access to resources and opportunities for income generation or entry into the labour market into the domestic and the caretaking world. This has seen to be predominant when there is a sustained increase in households led by women and an increase in the

population’s life expectancy, which forces a large number of older adults to depend on their family.

- Key data on the vulnerable populations:
    - Children: Almost 3 million children live in Bolivia; 51% are male and 49% female.
        - Sadly, many die before reaching age five due to poor infant survival and avoidable malnutrition, malaria, and diarrhea. Currently, 1.6 million children under the age of 5 die from these preventable diseases. Furthermore, the lack of water will cause more drought and an overall reduction in crop yields.
    - Older adults: They represent 8.3% of the total population in Bolivia, 37% of the rural areas, and 21% of the urban areas.

- Currently, the average age is in the country is 27. However, the population pyramid follows a decreased base, while there is continuous growth in the centre and the top sections.
    - Older adults are particularly vulnerable because they have a higher risk of chronic diseases such as diabetes, which can be exacerbated by climate change. They also face reduced mobility and overall lack of strength, visual and auditory impairment, higher vulnerability to infectious diseases, and limited access to health care services due to their age.

People with disabilities: They are particularly vulnerable because they need help to access safe potable water, food supplies, and shelter.


### E23 The different vulnerabilities of both men and women

- The effects of climate change manifest in the increase of extreme climate conditions, such as warmer summers, droughts, and storms that cause the loss of crops. Faced with these types of disasters, which have increased in frequency and intensity in the last few years, the rural population is more vulnerable because they are less able to adapt due to their lower income (less than men) and because they bear a disproportionate amount of the workload.
- Women face additional challenges as they are responsible for most domestic tasks. This means that they are subject to a direct dependence on natural resources to cope with their domestic duties, such as collecting water or firewood. The increase in extreme climate conditions has caused a scarcity in resources and makes traditional forms of employment less reliable, which has pushed (people) men to migrate, leaving women from the rural areas with double the responsibility for household tasks.
- Women are also more vulnerable because they have fewer opportunities than men in poverty as they lack access to and control over natural resources, technology, and loans. All these factors make them more vulnerable when faced with extreme climate conditions associated disasters.

- Based on these findings, some response actions proposed by the project include:
    - Developing an effective and comprehensive environmental education programme for the communities, to change unsustainable habits will be reflected in a reduction of environmental degradation and desertification.
    - Improving access to water resources through the construction of wells and boreholes as well as an increase of civil participation where women will be part of prevention, conservation, and management activities.

- Days, times and places for training will be considered, in consultation with the availability of time and mobility of the women. It is also possible to establish mobile day care centres for young children.


### E24 Use of knowledge and skills of women and men from vulnerable populations to contribute to project results

Food sources become more unpredictable and scarce because of climate change, which is exacerbating existing conditions of poverty. Women's knowledge and experience in maintaining biodiversity through the conservation and domestication of wild edible and medicinal plants is crucial to effective adaptation to climate change.
* Men, in general, possess vast knowledge and already have productive skills—because they were involved in the project during the K'aychana, which also strengthened women’s participation. This way, there will be complementary participation for both men and women in the project's activities.

### 8.25 Equitable opportunities and actions to challenge gender stereotypes and improve positive gender relations

* Gender stereotypes respond to the patriarchal culture that specifies or "defines" men to exercise violence towards women. In Bolivia, 7 of every 10 women suffer from some type of violence in their lifetime when the purpose is to “correct” behaviour that is out of the norm and establish a hierarchy between men and women. This kind of violence also justifies any man's power to intervene or control women by using different types of violence against women who "defies" or transgresses the cultural boundaries of gender.
* The project will work on the idea of co-responsibility within the homes. Furthermore, the project will work with the government and other actors in the country to establish policies regarding the allocation of gender-sensitive budgets. Finally, it will work towards the prevention of all forms of gender violence.
* Some examples include: the exchange of experiences between female producers' organisations, their participation in the preparation of adaptation plans to climate change, in emergency drills, among others.

## 9. PROJECT FORMULATION AND IMPLEMENTATION PRINCIPLES

The project builds on best practices and lessons learned from past adaptation-focused interventions in Bolivia that have had a transformative impact on enhancing the resilience of smallholder farmers. These include: improving access to credit and other financial services at on-farm and micro-level watershed level, improving crop productivity, enhancing water resource management, and increasing the capacity for climate change adaptation (e.g., hail and frost), managing competition for depletion and pressure on scarce natural resources, and reducing vulnerability to extreme weather events. These approaches will impact on ecological but also social resilience, which is a major underlying goal of the Joint Mitigation and Adaptation Strategy (JMAS) and the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) Paris Agreement to keep global warming below 2°C above pre-industrial levels. All this will be achieved through mainstreaming gender equality and promote gender transformational change.

To achieve this, the project is considered key principles for its design and implementation. The project will monitor and evaluate its achievement via qualitative and quantitative sex

30) ONU/MUJERES Annual Report 2012: p. 12


---
**Page 223**
---

9.1 Strengthening female technical community

The number of female professionals is limited in disciplines that have traditionally been considered masculine, such as irrigation, land and water management, climatology, information technology, agriculture sciences, and animal and veterinary sciences. The project resiliency of women's households to climate change will be enhanced by increasing the number of female professionals in these areas through training, technical assistance, and decision-making to prepare for, adapt to, and mitigate the effects of climate change. The project will engage female professionals and train young female professionals in these disciplines.

9.2 Leveraging gender-differentiated tasks and interests

Women and men are largely engaged in different tasks, and women are more willing to take up new practices. They also have stronger interests in food and nutritional security. The project will leverage these differences to introduce new agricultural products for diversification and for food and nutrition security. This will contribute significantly to the improvement of the health of all household members. Most importantly, children's health and development, in which balanced nutrition plays a critical role, will be positively affected.

The project has planned to work with women farmers' associations, which have the minimum criteria:

- Identify associations made up of women
- Identify the products of the association
- Support the formation of its board, then with its legal personality
- Implement a training plan based on methodology and consult with them about the days and times when they can be trained.
- Design graphic support materials that are easy to use.
- Develop communication materials in graphics and simple language.
- Provide training in the value chain, basic accounting
- Provide technical assistance
- Support with supplies, equipment and technology

9.3 Provide training in leadership, self-esteem women's participation in coping with negative shocks

The project household-level viable means to cope with shocks that climate change has brought about. The project will help women acquire the capacity to increase food available to each household by means of kitchen gardens to minimize the risk of having to consume less expensive food items or to eat less altogether. If the products yield cash income, it would supplement financial resources for other needs in the household. In addition, the project will provide credit facilities, endowing women with the ability to bring cash home is considered the surest way to empower them.

43


---
**Page 224**
---

9.4 Assisting women in initial investment

The lack of access to financial resources by women poses a great obstacle to adopting climate resilient agriculture, which includes vegetable production and animal rearing. The project will aid in leveraging women’s existing assets such as land, livestock, other assets and ensuring women learning how to grow vegetables and raise and care small animals. This assistance will free women who receive the material for starting a new venture, from the need to borrow from commercial lenders or informal sources of credit. Women who are unable to access formal credit often link their association with them is often linked to perpetuating poverty.

9.5 Personalized technical advice and capacity building

Farmers will be specifically trained through the Farmer Field School (FFS) system to understand climate-resilient farming techniques and understand the exposure to climate hazards posed by solidification of soil, water scarcity, and other climatic changes. The FFS system, which is a ACA enable the rapid dissemination and adoption of resilient technologies and practices and contribute to the decentralization of extension services. In addition, capacity-building sessions will be provided on how to improve their knowledge of climate risks, climate information and how to plan for adaptation. Capacity and technical advisory services integrate a transformative and gender-sensitive approach through the adoption of participatory strategies to ensure women’s active participation and improved access to knowledge and technologies, and to improve their economic situation through the use of climate-resilient technologies.

Farmers will be specifically trained through the Farmer Field School (FFS) system to understand climate-resilient farming techniques and understand the exposure to climate hazards. FFS will enable the rapid dissemination and adoption of resilient technologies and practices and contribute to the decentralization of extension services. In addition, capacity-building sessions will be provided on how to improve their knowledge of climate risks, climate information and how to plan for adaptation.

Capacity and technical advisory services integrate a transformative and gender-sensitive approach through the adoption of participatory strategies to ensure women’s active participation and improved access to knowledge and technologies, and to improve their economic situation through the use of climate-resilient technologies.

9.6 Promotion of an equal distribution of reproductive and productive tasks between men and women

Despite the wide range of agricultural work they carry out, women are subordinated in gender relations and are assigned subordinate roles culturally determined by the local society. This gives rise to the various inequities that affect women and it means that they devote more time to domestic chores than men do. The project will aim at promoting an equal distribution of reproductive and productive tasks between men and women through a multi-sectoral approach, based on a more integrated approach, focusing on these economic, social and cultural rights. According to the UN Women’s Empowerment Principles, the project will aim at empowering women and men equally and specific activities for the social and economic empowerment of women.

Women tend to stay in the communities, while men leave in search of work. This means that women often become responsible for the day-to-day running of the family and natural resource management. The project will aim at promoting an equal distribution of reproductive and productive tasks between men and women through a multi-sectoral approach, based on a more integrated approach, focusing on these economic, social and cultural rights.

The project will add even more responsibilities to their regular tasks and roles. Instead, the aim is to


---
**Page 225**
---

9.7 Equal participation

- The project will work with beneficiary families from communities. It will put in place mechanisms to ensure that women can participate actively and effectively, under the principle of equal opportunity. This will be achieved by ensuring a full understanding of the laws and policies on equality in the country; women and their specific needs are considered.
- The project will promote the participation of both men and women, using affirmative action or other measures to ensure that at least 50 percent of all participants are women and at least 10 percent are young people throughout the entire project cycle. Next, it will put in place conditions in place to facilitate their access, control over and management of resources.
- Family planning services will be provided with a focus on promoting equal gender relations. The project also proposes to work with women heads of household and young people to ensure that they are able to make informed decisions about family planning.
- Project design tools into account sex-disaggregated information and women were involved in order to find out their needs, capabilities and constraints associated with their access to tangible resources. This will be done through focus group discussions and other methods to ensure that women participate in this project, especially women who are heads of household.

9.8 Improving women's access to information

Women do not have good access to information and information technology, and despite the inclusion of the topic gender in various policies, new initiatives are launched without the consideration of the needs and constraints faced by women in accessing these services. The project will raise awareness among policy makers and community-support professionals on women’s access to information; its importance and the need for its improvement. Radio broadcasting on climate resilience and disaster risk reduction is planned with a significant audience and produce programs that specifically target them. The establishment of female farmers’ networks would also contribute to improving women's access to information.

9.9 Sustainability through gender inclusion

The project aims to set up structures that guarantee the governance of the project by receiving help to implement and evaluate the project from technical committees of local authorities and social organizations, which will be responsible for the implementation of the project. The project will promote the sustainability of actions through governance processes such as strategic alliances (Women councilors from the municipalities and Women leaders of grassroots organizations) and the creation of a network of women’s associations in the region of Baritonia Sisal to access economic resources from gender-sensitive organisations. Additionally, the project will work with local authorities to implement policies that have gender-sensitive budgets and support other policies that contribute to reducing gender and generational inequalities, violence against women and social exclusion.

Likewise, the Plurinational Service for Women and Depariticialization will be part of the Project’s training component.


---
**Page 226**
---

# 9.10 Communication strategy

This strategy will be worked within the framework of communication for development (dialogue and local participatory design) which has as its main focus the dissemination of knowledge of the topics addressed by the project, where special emphasis will be placed on equal opportunities for men and women, in addition to the recognition of the co-responsibility of care work, the incorporation of roles and the prevention of gender violence.

## 9.11 Monitoring and evaluation

The monitoring and evaluation system will seek to ensure that all the indicators at the project beneficiary level are disaggregated by sex and age. It will also measure the quantitative indicators proposed in the Gender Action Plan and the consultations with the indigenous population.

The monitoring and evaluation system will be able to produce reports on the budget allocated for gender equality gaps and inequalities in the project, and thus contribute to participatory accountability.

## 9.12 Strategic alliances

At the national level, alliances will be promoted with the ministries involved and the Plurinational Service for Women and Dismantling Patriarchy to implement policies, plans and agendas related to the thematic areas to be addressed by the project, with gender equality criteria.

In the territory, alliances will be made with institutions that promote gender equality, such as the Municipal Councils, the Municipal Intendants, the Integral Legal Services in the Municipal Governments, the Association of Women Councillors in each municipality and grassroots social organizations. Advocacy will be sought for the implementation of gender-sensitive budgeting.

At the territorial level, alliances will be forged with institutions that promote gender equality, such as the Municipal Councils, the Municipal Intendants, the Integral Legal Services in the Municipal Governments, the Association of Women Councillors in each municipality and grassroots community organization. The project will seek to advocate for the implementation of gender-sensitive budgeting.

Awareness-raising on gender equality will be taken forward with technical staff in government institutions, with a view to increasing men's access to information about gender equality and encouraging the construction of new masculinities, thus contributing to the sustainability of the work project.

## 10. GENDER ACTION PLAN

The outputs and activities proposed in the Gender Action Plan for the "Upcycling Ecosystem Based on the Recovery of vulnerable rural communities in the Valleje Macromission of the Plurinational State of Bolivia" project contribute to the targets of SGD 5, as described below.

The project's contribution to SGD 5

The project fits with Sustainable Development Goal 5 (among others) and, for the purposes of this document, mention will be made of this SGD: "Achieve gender equality and empower all women and girls". Gender equality is not only a matter of justice; it is also about women and girls as not just a basic human right; it is also crucial for sustainable development.


---
**Page 227**
---

The targets for Sustainable Development Goal № 5 that the project will help to achieve when it is implemented are the following:

- S.1 Reduce the prevalence of violence against all women and girls everywhere.
- S.5 Ensure women’s full and effective participation and equal opportunities for leadership at all levels of decision-making in political, economic, and public life.
- S.7 Adopt and strengthen sound policies and mechanisms to promote gender equality and the empowerment of women and girls at all levels.

The Action Plan for addressing gender in the project is aligned with the Global Environment Facility’s Policy on Gender Equality and the FAO Policy on Gender Equality. It will also help to operationalize national policies on gender and dimentional patriarchy in the Plurinational State of Bolivia.

This Plan seeks to ensure that the project achieves the expected results in terms of closing the gender gaps considered a priority, through gender mainstreaming.

The data collection and analysis for this Action Plan is based on the Fondo Project, Upcycling Ecosystem Based Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Rural Communities in the Valle Micro-region of the Plurinational State of Bolivia (MECEM-Vale), will be led by a full-time project-recruited Gender Specialist. As part of the implementation and monitoring and evaluation of this Action Plan, they will so be in close collaboration with other project-recruited staff who will lead day-to-day delivery of project activities at the regional and municipal levels.

The Gender Action Plan is based on the following data on the Project’s areas of action to be done during the lifespan of the project, ensuring that all actions are gender responsive so that they will track gender-related changes over time, this including across outcome areas, not just in terms of outputs. The monitoring and evaluation strategy for tracking progress and the evaluation of the set indicators and targets will employ both qualitative and quantitative data collection methods to contribute to the triangulation of results and to capture change that is difficult to measure quantitatively. In addition, lessons learned from other projects and best practices through narrative reporting – sometimes these lessons are the most significant for achieving change and are the most difficult to disseminate.

The Project will ensure that gender mainstreaming is found in all project activities. However, in order to work with more direct and transformative actions, at the beginning of the Project, a baseline of the project’s gender equality gaps will be established through the Gender Baseline Study. The Monitoring and Evaluation strategy to measure and report progress in reducing gender inequality gaps. Likewise, the communication strategy will accompany the implementation of the Project’s gender equality gaps. In addition, the project will establish a Gender Working Group in the project. To consolidate sustainability and advocacy with a gender perspective, special emphasis will be given to the participation of women and girls as well as of their counterparts at the national and subnational levels. The baseline will also provide more accurate information for proposing gender-responsive actions.

The project will seek to strengthen the participation of adult and young women across the board, including in decision-making positions. This will ensure that at least 30% of project beneficiaries are women. If necessary, positive affinities criteria will be used to ensure the equal participation of all stakeholders and a personal and economic empowerment strategy will be developed, which will include: i) participatory methodologies to ensure leadership


---
**Page 228**
---

opportunities and influence on women’s decision making through capacity building on issues related to gender roles, self-esteem, leadership, information on laws that are favorable to gender equality in the country; ii) training in the topics addressed by the project, technical assistance and access to technologies on the part of productive associations led by women. The criteria for selection of the associations is that they should be either exclusively formed only by women and also mixed Associations, whose board is composed of 50% women.

Also, all the tools and training support materials developed in the project (access to information, access to credit, etc.) will be gender-sensitive and will be adapted to the needs of women as they are developed considering the intersectionality of the women to whom they will be destined.

In component 1, the project will build capacity and implement climate technologies and climate-resilient agroecological and organic practices to address vulnerability and increase productivity of agricultural systems. It will promote climate-resilient value chains by providing technical assistance, input training and certification to community-based productive associations, prioritizing women-led associations in the selection process for access to inputs, credit lines and generate income.

In component 2, the project will promote small farmers’ access to water resources with improved irrigation systems, encouraging women, especially those who are heads of household, to access family and community water reserves, and to be trained in field schools in the implementation of climate-proof irrigation systems

In component 3, a CCA adaptation strategy will be developed with a focus on conservation and restoration of natural ecosystems, where women will be able to participate in micro-watershed use plans. A water source monitoring system will also be developed and it is expected that women will have access to this information to make decisions on the use of water resources for agriculture and other uses.

Component 4, will strengthen local governance structures for participatory planning of climate adaptation and fund mobilization, where financial mechanisms will be specified that consider rural women’s needs. Women will also participate in the development of microfinance credit portfolios, as well their participation and leadership in territorial consultative platforms for integrated and resilient water management and sustainable production systems.

The project also aligns with the FAO Gender Equality Policy objective of achieving equity between men and women in sustainable agricultural production and rural development for the elimination of poverty. The project will promote gender equality by working closely together with men as decision-makers in rural areas, in institutions and in the development of laws, policies and programs that affect agriculture, including access to land, credit, inputs and other productive resources, decent employment and income, goods and services for sustainable agricultural development, and for markets.

The project will promote gender equality by working closely together with men as decision-makers in rural areas, in institutions and in the development of laws, policies and programs that affect agriculture, including access to land, credit, inputs and other productive resources, decent employment and income, goods and services for sustainable agricultural development, and for markets.

### 10.2 Support of a gender advisor during project implementation

A gender specialist must be hired to monitor the “Uplifting Economic Based Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Women and Families in the Valencia Province” (hereinafter: the Provincial) State of Bolivia Project and ensure that gender equality is mainstreamed throughout it by being responsible for focusing on supporting the gender focal points, conducting gender analysis, providing technical consultation with women from the communities; training the technical staff and the beneficiary population; forging strategic alliances with government institutions and indigenous organizations; and ensuring that the project promotes a culture of respect and sensitivity towards the risks involved with promoting gender equality; ensuring that the reports mainstream gender throughout them. The advisor will also be responsible for monitoring the indicators.

It is also planned to hire a specialist in masculinities, to support in update on gender Data in the Project’s areas of action and then in a sensitization plan with the men who are part of the project promoting their participation as well as care work within families.

# Impact Statement: The objective of project "Upscaling Ecosystem Based Climate Resilience of Vulnerable Rural Communities in the Valles Macro-region of the Plurinational State of Bolivia (RECCIM Valley)" is the increased resilience of vulnerable communities, including women and girls, to the negative impacts of climate change by adapting the production systems and restoring critical ecosystem services on which they depend.

## Outcome Statement: Increased resilience of 58,000 vulnerable smallholder farmers in the Valles Macro-region against reduction of water availability. Of this total, demonstration plots will be established at 17,530 hectares. RECCIM Valley will contribute to increasing access to safe water supply, will ensure that rural families have safe water in the face of prolonged drought, and will contribute to improving ecosystems and ecosystem services at 17,530 hectares farmland and through the promotion of soil and water conservation, agroforestry systems, and appropriate wastewater management.
