11. APPLICABLE SOCIAL SAFEGUARDS POLICIES

This proposal has been prepared following the policies social safeguards standard of GCF and FAO. These guidelines are described below.

FAO has a set of environmental and social environmental and social risk management safeguards in its strategies, policies and projects on the ground. These guidelines aid in identifying and early and systematically assessing environmental and social risks and their integration into the project cycle (including planning, implementation, monitoring and evaluation) with application in 9 areas[63].

However, the Gender Plan will consider those that are more closely linked to gender equality.

A.1 FAO social Safeguards considered are:

ESS 7: Decent work
ESS 8: Gender equality
ESS 9: Indigenous peoples and cultural heritage

A.2 FAO Policies considered are:

• FAO whistleblower protection policy (administrative circular N°2015/06) applying to any FAO personnel when internal or external reporting according to the consideration of the circular.

• GCF Policy on the Protection of Whistleblowers and Witnesses (2018) aims to empower staff and witnesses to report wrongdoing without fear of retaliation so that the GCF can effectively protect its interests, resources, and mission.

• FAO Policy on Gender Equality 2020-2030 strives to achieve equality between women and men in sustainable agriculture and rural development for the elimination of hunger and poverty.

• GCF Gender Policy (2019) reinforces the responsiveness of GCF to the culturally diverse context, including efforts to address gender inequalities for the achievement of gender equality and climate change.

• FAO Protection from sexual exploitation and abuse (PSEA) N° 2013/7. The principles are non-discrimination, respect for human rights and the dignity of all people underpin FAO’s commitment to preventing and addressing acts of sexual exploitation and abuse.

• FAO Policy on the prevention of harassment, sexual harassment and abuse of authority N° 2015/4. Harassment is any unwanted conduct that offends or humiliates another person. Sexual Harassment in all its forms is contrary to the United Nations Charter, the Staff Regulations and Staff Rules of the Organization and the Standards of Conduct for the International Civil Service.

A.3 GCF Safeguards

### ESS 8: Gender equality

- Promote fair treatment, non-discrimination and equal opportunity for all workers.
- Protect and support women, particularly disadvantaged and vulnerable categories of women.
- Promote the application of international labour standards in the rural economy, including the prevention and elimination of child labour in agriculture.
- Provide equal access to and control over productive resources, services, and markets.

### ESS 9: Indigenous peoples and cultural heritage

- Strengthen women and men's participation in decision-making in rural institutions and policy processes.
- Ensure that indigenous peoples benefit equally from development interventions and that inequality is not reinforced or perpetuated. The rights of indigenous peoples are respected in all FAO’s projects and programmes.

### 12. RISK CLASSIFICATION

The assessment classified project risk as moderate, although large-scale, significant, or irreversible environmental impacts are not expected. The potential impacts identified are mainly impacts associated with activities that include community participation, especially of indigenous communities. These risks will be mitigated through the project’s selection criteria and social and environmental plan of action.

### Related Risks to SEAH and GVB

Project activities can bring minor influx of labor and employment income differentials in local communities. Projects with minor labor influx of workers may increase the demand for sex work, including prostitution, which is often linked to early sexual initiation among girls and early marriage. Furthermore, higher wages for workers in a community can lead to an increase in transactional sex. The risk of incidents of sex between laborers and minors, even when it is not transactional, also increases.

Risk of SEAH/GBV by project staff: e.g., regional and provincial level officials who may ask for sexual favors from women and girls for them to be selected as project beneficiaries. The proposed project envisages increase in agricultural activities on and off the farm, improve production efficiency, and diversify income sources. This will require more people to move into the project target areas, and hence enhanced mobility of both women and men. Higher mobility exposes women and girls to increased risk of sexual exploitation.

Women are responsible for household chores, which require much time and energy. They will be adding another task to their already full day by participating in the project. It cannot but result in less time spent in household chores and caregiving, which may well cause dissatisfaction among men who feel that they have lost out on the care of children.

While all project related personnel are engaged on the condition that they follow various Codes of Conduct, including those on sexual exploitation and abuse, we cannot rule out the possibility that some men may resort to violence or other forms of related favors in exchange for participation in the project or for obtaining agricultural inputs.

Women may face opposition from their husbands and other male family members for their wish to participate in the project. In some households, the situation may escalate to violence. If their spouse is not involved in the project, women may be forced into early marriage. Women may be harassed or attacked also by men but outside their households, who are not involved in the project and take out their frustration on the women.

If women are forced to give up their earnings from agriculture to feel insecure and turn violent against the women. If women refuse to give up their earnings to their husbands and other male family members, that is likely to trigger violence against the women.

Considering that many are heads of households, there is a strong incentive for children to be involved in agricultural activities. The proposed project has no agricultural intervention so it is neutral in terms of child labor. The children are affected by the changes in adults' responsibilities


---
**Page 249**
---

In the field and also by the changes in the adults' ability to carry out household chores, which are caused by the changes in the field.

FAO has mandatory courses for staff working on projects: Policy on the Prevention of Harassment, Sexual Harassment, and Abuse of Authority Working Harmoniously, in addition to the CODE OF ETHICAL CONDUCT, where GBV and prevention policy is addressed.60

### 12.3.1 Gender-based violence

In order to ensure that the protection of populations at risk or affected by GBV while ensuring that no individuals are exposed to harm as a result of AD/HA activities, GBV concerns must be mainstreamed into FAO projects and programmes.

There are documents to provide practical guidance to FAO staff and partners on how to mainstream GBV in their work. These include the Code of Conduct for Staff and the Manual of Interventions.61 The following is a list of guiding principles that aim to ensure humanitarian and development interventions are safe, nondiscriminatory, participatory, accountable and sustainable.

### 12.3.2 Guiding Principles for protection mainstreaming

- Understanding the local context and building on women's and men's strengths and assets: Build on the strengths of existing services and respect local cultures without perpetuating stereotypes and discrimination.
- Safety, dignity, and empowerment: Ensure that your interventions are not perceived as reinforcing negative gender norms or practices. Consider the regular effects of your intervention that can increase people’s vulnerability to both physical and psychosocial risks Inclusive access: Pay attention to access issues (e.g., disability, discrimination, age) when planning activities such as training sessions, meetings, and workshops. Make arrangements to facilitate access, ensuring that no one is left behind.
- Participation and empowerment: Empower men and women by ensuring programming is based on their needs and priorities. Ensure that all participants are able to fully participate in the project and be informed about project objectives and their participation ensured throughout the project cycle.
- Coordination and partnerships: Promote and maintain strong and respectful partnerships with other sector specialists, in particular those with protection and GBV expertise (including GBV subsectors). Share information on best practices, knowledge sharing and to ensure work is in line with and complements others agencies' efforts.
- Accountability to affected populations: Set up appropriate mechanisms for affected people to provide feedback about the project. This should be done throughout implementation. Programmes should be reactive to feedback, concerns and complaints.

It is also important to mention that FAO has the policy against gender-based and child violence, sexual harassment and harassment (Appendix 4) that will be socialized with the technical staff and the direct target group of the project to prevent this type of actions and, if necessary, they can also file complaints.

13. GRIEVANCE REDRESS MECHANISMS

FAO is committed to ensuring that its programs are implemented in accordance with the Organization’s environmental and social obligations. To better achieve these goals, and to ensure that beneficiaries of FAO Programs have access to effective and timely attention to their complaints about non-compliance with these obligations, the Organization, to complement mechanisms at the project level, has established a grievance redress mechanism at the management level, which gives the Office of the Inspector-General the mandate to independently review and address concerns related to alleged or potential violations of FAO’s social and environmental commitments. For this purpose, concerns may be communicated in accordance with the procedures set out in the document “Guidelines for the Disposition of Complaints Related to the Organization’s Environmental and Social Standards,” which apply to all FAO programmes and projects.

Concerns should be addressed at the closest appropriate level; i.e., at the project management level (if possible), and if necessary, at the Regional Office level. If a concern or complaint cannot be resolved through consultation and action at the project management level, a complainant may file a grievance with the Office of the Inspector-General (OIG) in accordance with the Guidelines. Program and project managers will be responsible for addressing concerns brought to their attention by the point of contact. Principles to be followed include respect for human rights, including indigenous peoples’ rights; protection of human rights, including those related to indigenous peoples, compliance with national standards, consistency with standards, gender equity, transparency, honesty and mutual respect.

### 13.1 Project-level grievance mechanism

The project will establish a grievance redress mechanism at field level to receive complaints; this grievance mechanism has been agreed with beneficiaries (including through the FPIC) and indigenous populations to take place in the context of the Consultative Territorial Platforms to be facilitated by FAO (see Section 13.3). Consultation will determine the process on the process to file a complaint will be refined once the consultative platforms are operational and will be disclosed at all meetings, workshops and other related events throughout the life of the project. The grievance mechanism will ensure that beneficiaries have access to information including the necessary information regarding the contacts and the process for filing grievances, including on the availability of and ways to access the GCF’s Independent Redress Mechanism.

SEARCA has agreed with FAO to establish a gender-sensitive grievance redress mechanism centered and gender responsive approach, including confidential reporting and mandatory involvement of the FAO E&S and Gender specialists in monitoring the process. In case of GBV, the reporting party will be referred to the appropriate mechanisms for referral pathways by the CRM personnel, and directed as necessary to medical care, psychological support, legal support, community-driven protection measures, and reintegration services.

* * *

*(Available online at: http://www.fao.org/3/a-i4439e.pdf).*


---
**Page 254**
---

The project will also be responsible for safe and ethical documenting and reporting as part of the safeguards performance monitoring on any grievances received and how they were addressed.

The mechanism includes the following stages:

a) The complainant files a complaint through one of the channels of the grievance mechanism. This will be sent to the Environmental and Social Safeguards Specialist to assess whether it is within scope. The confidentiality of the complaint must be preserved during the process.
b) The Environmental and Social Safeguards Specialist will be responsible for recording the grievance and how it has been addressed, if a resolution was agreed.
c) If no resolution is reached or the complainant does not accept the resolution, the complaint must be sent to a higher level, until a solution or acceptance is reached.
d) For every complaint received, a written report will be produced within thirty (30) working days; alternatively, if the proposed resolution takes longer than thirty (30) working days.
e) In compliance with the resolution, the person in charge of dealing with the complaint, may contact the complainant, or may call for interviews and meetings to better understand the reasons.
f) All complaints received, their responses, and resolutions, must be duly registered.

Resolution

Upon acceptance a solution by the complainant, a document with the agreement should be signed

14. GENDER MARKER

The gender marker is used to mainstream gender equality throughout projects, which means constantly and coherently including women and men when designing, implementing and monitoring policies, programmes and projects.**

(. ) mainstreaming gender does not only focus on involving women in projects but also making a conscious effort to make sure that they, along with the men, can define from the outset the objectives of the project and its implementation methods, ensuring that the needs, wishes and aspirations of both groups are recognized by the project.

Given the above and the importance given by the project to gender, the gender marker assigned is Gender Marker 1 which has the following characteristics:

Table 11: Gender Marker 1

| Concept Note requirements | Pro/Doc requirements |
| --- | --- |
| The promotion of gender equality is relevant. | The project is designed on the basis of a gender analysis that identifies roles, opportunities and constraints (supported by GAD). |
| The gender dimension is systematically integrated into the project, but the promotion of gender equality is not the objective of the intervention. | The logical framework includes activities related to gender, and gender is taken into account in outputs and indicators. |
| Gender responsiveness is seen when the project wishes to contribute to a lasting development objective by providing a solution to the needs and interests of the women and men in the target group through targeted activities, i.e., when minimal response is afforded to these interests (gender-responsive). | A sufficient budget is allocated to meet implementation needs. |
| Considering the Gender Integration Continuum of the Green Fund Gender Policy (Page 12), the gender marker in the Project is Gender Responsive: | Gender-related tasks and responsibilities are included in the terms of reference for targeted activities. |

Addresses differentiated gender needs equitable participation and equitable distribution of benefits, resources, rights and status, but does not address root causes of inequalities.

# ANNEX G: GENDER-BASED AND CHILD VIOLENCE, SEXUAL EXPLOITATION AND HARASSMENT AGAINST WOMEN AND CHILDREN: DEFINITIONS

This appendix gives the definitions of terms related to violence, exploitation and harassment and considered under this ESMP.

## Harassment

The "Policy on the Prevention of Harassment, Sexual Harassment and Abuse of Authority," Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) Administrative Circular, No. 2015/13, defines harassment as:

Any improper and unwelcome conduct by an individual or group of individuals that is directed at, and offensive to, another person and which the individual(s) knew, or reasonably ought to have known, would cause offense or harm to that person. Harassment does not have to be intentional or deliberate; it can take the form of a single incident or repeated incidents.

It further characterizes harassment as normally repeated incidents. It sees harassment to include sexual harassment and abuse of authority, which are specific forms of harassment. FAO defines these terms below:

Any unwelcome sexual advance, request for sexual favor, verbal or physical conduct or gesture of a sexual nature, or any other behavior of a sexual nature that might reasonably be expected or be perceived as creating an intimidating, hostile or offensive work environment. While typically involving a pattern of behavior, it can take the form of a single incident and may occur between persons of the opposite or same sex.

### Abuse of authority

In the context of harassment, is improper use of a position of influence, power or authority against another person. This is particularly serious when a person uses his or her influence or power or authority to improperly influence the career or employment conditions of another, including, but not limited to, appointment, promotion, transfer, dismissal, or other terms and conditions of employment.

Abuse of authority may also include conduct that creates a hostile or offensive work environment which is sexual in nature. Harassment by abuse of authority includes discrimination and harassment, including sexual harassment, are particularly serious when accompanied by abuse of authority.

In order to be considered harassment it must occur between persons of the opposite sexes or of the same sex and that unwelcome sexual behavior may be obvious, or it may be subtle and persistent. While typically involving a pattern of behavior, it can take the form of a single incident.


## Violence against women

As defined by the 1993 UN Declaration on the Elimination of Violence against Women, is any act of gender-based violence that results in, or is likely to result in, physical, sexual or mental harm or suffering to women, including threats of such acts, coercion or arbitrary deprivation of liberty.


---
**Page 266**
---

exploitative and often sexualized actions that are perpetrated against a person’s will and are based on socially ascribed gender differences between women and men. The definition contains characterization of GBV as “a widespread and life-threatening health, protection and human rights issue with serious negative consequences not only for survivors but also for the achievements made in the areas of development, poverty reduction and sustainable states.” The majority of victims and survivors are women and girls, who suffer a range of mental and physical health problems. These include depression, anxiety, post-traumatic stress disorder, loss their potential and undermining efforts to reduce poverty and promote peace, security and sustainable development.

FAO sees the following six types of GBV as relevant to its work:

1. Denial of resources, opportunities or services: denial of rightful access to or control over productive assets, including resources, livelihood opportunities, information, education, health or other social services. Examples include preventing a widow from receiving an inheritance, land grabbing, earnings forcibly taken by an intimate partner or family member, and preventing a partner from working.

2. Physical violence: An act of physical violence that is not sexual in nature. Examples include hitting, slapping, choking, cutting, shaving, burning, shooting or use of any weapons, acid attacks or any other form of violence in private or public injury.

3. Sexual violence: Sexual violence takes many forms, including rape and marital rape, sexual slavery and/or trafficking, forced pregnancy, sexual harassment, sexual exploitation and/or abuse (e.g. forced prostitution), sexual slavery and transactional sex (i.e., sex for food/food).

4. Emotional and psychological assault: includes verbal abuse, humiliation, cruel and degrading treatment, threats of violence, deprivation of sleep, denial of access to food and restrictions on freedom of movement or behavior, thus causing increased dependency and fear.

5. Harmful practices: These include forced marriage, child marriage, honor or dowry killings, son preference which may mean a female child is disadvantaged from birth in quality and quantity of parental care).

6. Sexual exploitation and abuse (SEA): Refers to acts of sexual exploitation and sexual abuse committed by United Nations, NGOs, and intergovernmental Organization (IGO) personnel against women and girls.
